Proposed: an on-ramp from compliant e-invoicing into the W1 loop of D86 (Contacts/Products, CRM, Sales, basic Stock, Invoicing plus Accounting with the German pack, PDF and email documents and attachments; the `documents` DMS app is W3, D25), one company, ICP 5-50 person services and light trade; France second (2028-03), Poland third (2028-08), re-scored in 2027-Q2. Preconditions (Proposed): written SKR03/04 and DATEV confirmation; e-invoices travel by email or file, and Peppol goes through a certified access point the user chooses, with bring-your-own credentials and no access point operated by SpaceCorps (D83). Out of the first delivery: POS, payroll, manufacturing, multi-country tax, and the items D86 moves out (purchase orders, the services variant).

**Why.** Germany scores 84 of 100, has about 281k firms of 10-249 staff without ERP and the 2028-01-01 all-issuer mandate. **Superseded alternative.** A hosted Free plan as the on-ramp into a seven-app loop that included Purchase, Payments and Portal, with Peppol through a SpaceCorps-chosen partner access point (former D4). **Rejected.** Vertical wedge; platform first; UK/US (no compliance wedge); BE/NL (small base). **Consequences.** The W1 loop fits B2B invoicing and light trade best; services firms arrive with the services variant (W3, D86), so whether the ICP stays "services and light trade" is a Q8 question. Flip France and Poland if no French compliance lead or PA partner by 2027-03-31; SKR/BAS licences may force data-pack delivery. **Evidence.** OR-7; R25 A7-A8, R20 s9, R09.

#### D5 Funding path and hiring gates
**Status** SUPERSEDED by D84 (OR-6). **Reversibility** not applicable. **Owner action** none.

**Decision.** None. The gated plan is gone; D84 records the self-funded posture. **Superseded alternative.** About EUR 3M seed for 24 months, then about EUR 5M against EUR 1M ARR, at most 17 FTE before EUR 1M recurring ARR, a cut to 6 FTE if paying organisations were under 60 at M24, an NLnet Restack application, and a pledge, veto and investor covenant before any priced round (former D5). **Evidence.** OR-6; R26 B5-B7 and C1-C3 (historical).

#### D6 Tenancy unit
**Status** BINDING (the Free-tier clause was removed by OR-2 and OR-11). **Reversibility** two-way door (pool mode can be added); very high cost if `company_id` discipline is skipped. **Owner action** no for the mode; the spike readout sets the cell size (Q6).

**Decision.** One PostgreSQL database per business is the only mode. `company_id` on every row, a router trait (`TenantRouter`, renamed `BusinessRouter` in D24), no cross-business SQL. Cells hold at most 250 business databases, with PgBouncer per cell and a control-plane directory business-to-cell; a cell is a sizing rule for whoever operates many businesses (a self-hoster, a partner or another third-party host), since SpaceCorps hosts none for others today apart from the sample-data sandbox (D83; the hosting service of OR-22 runs dedicated servers, not cells). Pool mode is not built (if built: `tenant_id` leads every key, composite FKs, RLS primary). Spike: 250 databases of 800 tables on PG 18; pass means fan-out migration under 30 minutes and idle memory under 4 GB; readout 2026-11-27. If the spike fails, the cell size shrinks and the owner decides whether a pooled mode is ever built (DEFERRED).

**Why.** Odoo and Frappe parity; per-business backup, erasure and residency. **Superseded alternative.** A pooled Free tier decided on COGS and conversion triggers (R26 risk M7), which assumed a hosted Free plan (former D6 clause). **Rejected.** Schema per business; pool default; silo and pool both first-class. **Consequences.** Migrations are fleet jobs (about 190k tables per cell). **Evidence.** R23 DR-2, R22 s10, R08, R18 s4, R26 risk T6.

#### D7 Realtime and queue signalling
**Status** BINDING. **Reversibility** two-way door (low to medium). **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** `NOTIFY` is forbidden in business transactions; CI allows it only in `obop-bus-notifier`. Durable events are outbox rows plus per-subscriber delivery rows. Wake-up ladder: in-process `tokio::sync::Notify`, then a coalescing notifier (at most 10 per second per node, `SET LOCAL synchronous_commit = off`) on one bus database per cluster, then polling at 250 ms to 2 s. Each node keeps one LISTEN connection, on the bus database only. Browser realtime is a WebSocket resuming from `(xid8, event_id)` below the snapshot xmin. Single-business installs use the same path.

**Why.** Source reading shows the NOTIFY commit lock is cluster-wide and LISTEN is per database. **Rejected.** NOTIFY in transactions; plain polling; NATS now (COULD). **Consequences.** The pgbench spike (32 databases; coalesced at least 90% of plain) may relax the ban to SHOULD, never the one-LISTEN rule. **Evidence.** R23 DR-1, R22 s1.4.

#### D8 PostgreSQL floor
**Status** BINDING. **Reversibility** two-way door to raise the floor, hard to lower it. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** Support 17 and 18 at 1.0; 18 is the reference. CI runs 17, 18 and 19 from RC. Kernel DDL uses 17-level features; 18-only features (temporal foreign keys, virtual columns, `uuidv7()` default, `RETURNING OLD/NEW`, `NOT NULL ... NOT VALID`) sit behind `requires_pg = 18`. Raise the floor to 18 when 17 is 12 months from EOL (2028-11) or Debian stable and Supabase ship 18.

**Why.** 16 has under two years left at a 2027 release and lacks incremental backup; Debian 13 and Supabase do not yet ship 18. **Rejected.** Floor 16; 18 only. **Consequences.** Overlap guarantees use `EXCLUDE ... gist` on 17. **Evidence.** R23 DR-3.

#### D9 Keys and money
**Status** BINDING. **Reversibility** one-way door once data exists; the key freeze waits for the spike (2026-11-27). **Owner action** no; the spike result decides the key type (Q6).

**Decision.** Every model has an application-generated UUIDv7 primary key (`Id<M>`); ISO catalogues use natural codes; module records carry external ids `module.name`; mutating commands carry a unique `command_id`; `created_at` is explicit. A per-model `IdType` flag lets ledger, stock-move and log tables switch to `bigint` plus a public uuid if the 300M-row spike exceeds +25% size or 15% slowdown. Ledger money is `bigint` minor units plus `currency_code` (exponent as data); document totals `bigint`; unit prices `NUMERIC(24,8)`; quantities `NUMERIC(24,6)` in base UoM; FX rates `NUMERIC(28,14)`; valuation `value_minor bigint`. Per-company rounding `per_line` or `per_group` (mandatory for EN 16931). Floats are banned.

**Why.** Stable ids across databases; exact arithmetic. **Rejected.** `bigint` identity everywhere; `bigint` plus public uuid as default. **Consequences.** About +20% ledger row width. **Evidence.** R23 DR-5, DR-6.

#### D10 Authorization algebra and RLS
**Status** BINDING. **Reversibility** one-way door in practice (every module's seed access data). **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** Odoo 20's algebra: OR of permission rows, AND of restriction rows, compiled from the Domain AST to SQL, one path for list, by-id, export, webhook and AI reads. Default-deny at model, field and action level; every permission declares a scope (`all`, `own`, `company`, `domain`); an implicit TRUE is a lint error. Elevation is a typed, audited `Env<Elevated>`. Field security is enforced in the projection and the query compiler, which rejects filter, sort and group on unreadable fields; sensitive classes live in separate tables or columns behind `obop_cls_*` roles. RLS is a fixed row-local backstop (FORCE, non-owner non-BYPASSRLS role, `set_config(..., true)`). Bulk import stages through non-RLS tables.

**Why.** RLS cannot express the algebra; Cedar cannot filter lists. **Rejected.** Default-allow `ir.rule`; ABAC engine; RLS primary. **Consequences.** A property test must give identical answers from list, by-id, export and RLS paths; benchmark the restrictive policy on 10M ledger rows. **Evidence.** R23 DR-4, R18 s3, R02.

#### D11 Extension and scripting model
**Status** BINDING (the licence-boundary condition fell away with OR-1). **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** Code tiers X1 declarative pack, X2 CEL, X3 Wasm component, X4 remote app, X5 first-party native crate (labels renamed from R24's T1-T5, D24; RATIFIED, OR-14). CEL is the only expression language stored in data for conditions, modifiers, formulas, validation and policies; Rhai is not shipped in W1-W2 (COULD later, only inside a signed pack). Template languages are a separate, bounded category (Proposed with D14 and D20): minijinja templates and Typst markup are allowed only as sandboxed render-time templates with fuel, recursion and no-I/O limits, and never express conditions, modifiers, formulas, validation or policies. CEL needs save-time limits (4 KB, AST depth 32), a step budget and a deadline, added in a W1 spike. X3 pins a wasmtime LTS (v48; next v60 about 2027-08-20) behind `obop-wasm-host`, patches within 7 days (24 h for High), never relies on fuel alone, and runs in the `ext` role. UI tiers F0 declarative, F1 trusted ESM (first-party and business-own only), F2 remote-dom, F3 sandboxed iframe; third-party UI never enters the main realm.

**Why.** In-process plugins have the worst security record. Under Apache-2.0 they are no longer licence derivatives, so the rule is a security decision only (OR-1). **Rejected.** In-process third-party crates; Rhai in data; main-realm registry UI. **Consequences.** W1 cannot promise Wasm; X3 moves to W3 (D86) behind an internal connector spike. The AGPL Application Exception and the counsel condition that came with it are gone (R24 s8 is superseded). A business may still compile its own crate into its own build, or copy a plugin's source into its instance (D81); that is its own code, not a supported third-party tier. **Evidence.** R24 s3-4, R21.

#### D12 Expression storage
**Status** PROPOSED. **Reversibility** two-way door early, costly after data exists. **Owner action** yes (Q9).

**Decision.** Domains are a JSON AST. Modifiers, visibility, conditions and formulas store CEL source plus a type-checked compiled form, run by one Rust evaluator (server and Wasm client) under conformance tests. A CEL-like authoring syntax for domains may compile to the AST.

**Why.** Domains must compile to SQL; CEL fits everything else. **Rejected.** JSON AST for all (R19); CEL text for all. **Consequences.** One evaluator kept in lockstep across server and client; the W1 spike adds the cost limits. **Evidence.** R24 s3.2, R19.

#### D13 Offline scope
**Status** PROPOSED. **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** yes (Q11).

**Decision.** 1.0 targets Odoo-19 parity for the apps that ship by then: a read cache of visited views and an explicit degraded-feature list. POS order capture with post-reconnect invoicing ships with `pos` and `obop-edge` in W3 (D25), after 1.0 (2028-04-18), unless the owner pulls a POS-capture slice into W2 (Q11). Queued writes for allow-listed models come later (W3+). No Electric, PowerSync or Zero. Architecture is D37.

**Why.** Odoo 20 adds queued writes, but a sync engine is expensive and none fits (non-Rust, FSL, no offline writes). **Rejected.** Odoo-20 parity at 1.0; local-first engine. **Consequences.** POS and warehouse get an edge command outbox from W3; the degraded-feature list is published; 1.0 makes no POS offline promise. **Evidence.** R22 MUST 3-4, R19, R12.

*D13 amendment 2026-10-27 (D87, OR-34): a native app has a device command outbox.* A native app (D87) keeps its queued writes in a SQLite database on the device, which must itself be encrypted at rest, each with a UUIDv7 `command_id`, and replays them through normal domain services, so permissions are checked at replay. What makes a replay safe is the idempotency key of `bulk_create` (D70: scoped to person, model and key, a repeat of the identical request within 24 hours returns the first answer, the same key with other rows is refused, a key is forgotten after 24 hours) and unique constraints (D57) that fail a later replay closed; a replayed `update` sets the same values again. For location points the key is `client_trip_id:first_seq-last_seq` (spec/mobile.md 5.3). The optimistic lock `if_version` is reserved by D75 and not yet accepted by `update`; needing it would be a required capability (spec/mobile.md 5.8). A device that is revoked on the server (the `device` model of D87) has its queued commands refused. This is the contract of the edge command outbox (D37), not a sync engine: the rule "no Electric, PowerSync or Zero" stands, and the 1.0 scope above (a read cache and a degraded-feature list for the web apps, no promise of queued writes in the web back office) is unchanged. What changes is that queued writes exist earlier than W3+ for the commands of one native app (the creation of a trip, batches of location points, an update of the trip's expected counts, `end` and `submit`, spec/mobile.md 5.3), because a phone offline in a tunnel is its normal condition. *Not delivered:* all of it; Proposed with the technical design of D87 until the Mileage slice is built.

#### D14 Storefront rendering
**Status** PROPOSED. **Reversibility** two-way door until W3. **Owner action** yes (Q14).

**Decision.** Rust-rendered block snapshots (minijinja, also built to Wasm for editor preview) with Vite islands; no Node in production; islands and the manifest are built only with `vp build`. Astro, Leptos or Dioxus would each need an explicit owner waiver of the Vite+ rule (F2), because none is part of Vite+.

**Why.** Keeps the runtime Rust-only and cacheable; vite-ssg is broken under Vite+ (#1315). **Rejected.** Node SSR sidecar; separate Astro app. **Consequences.** App-like checkout flows stay Rust plus islands. **Evidence.** R12 s9, R07 s3.9.

#### D15 Frontend stack and Vite+ risk
**Status** BINDING; the nightly exit drill is a RATIFIED named exception to "`vp` only" (I-4, OR-9). **Reversibility** two-way door, moderately costly. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** React 19 and TypeScript 7, TSX only; the React Compiler via `@rolldown/plugin-babel`; TanStack Query, Router, Table and Virtual; no `baseUrl`; no `.vue` or `.svelte` in core. No commercially licensed runtime dependency (CI licence allow-list). Rust owns contracts: OpenAPI 3.1 from utoipa, meta-model types via ts-rs or specta, generated files diff-gated. All `vite-plus` imports pass through one config factory and one test re-export module. Nightly exit drill on a throwaway copy of the tree (section 1.1; RATIFIED, I-4) and a named owner of Vite+ triage (the owner or an agent assigned to it). The web UI is a client of the typed commands (D80) and holds no business logic.

**Why.** The thin alias layer and a days-old 1.0 are the risk, not the components beneath. **Rejected.** Vue, Svelte, Solid. **Consequences.** TanStack Start stays out until it leaves RC; AG Grid Enterprise, Handsontable and similar are excluded. **Evidence.** R07 s2-3, R26 T1-T2, R27 B9.

#### D16 Release, LTS and deprecation
**Status** SUPERSEDED by D85 (OR-12). **Reversibility** not applicable. **Owner action** none (the open residue is Q28).

**Decision.** None. The support promise, the release train and the deprecation window are recorded in D85. **Superseded alternative.** Two series a year on the third Tuesday of April and October, every fourth an LTS (`N.0`) with five years of support (years 0-2 full, 2-5 security and legal-critical), `N.1`-`N.3` with 9 months, a monthly patch train, hosted-fleet rings, and a 24-month deprecation floor (F1 12 months) with a release-month counting rule (former D16; withdrawn by OR-12). **Evidence.** OR-12; R27 C1-C8, R24 s5.3 and R21 (historical for the LTS and the 24-month floor).

#### D17 Compliance operating model
**Status** RATIFIED (OR-2, OR-10, OR-11) for the operating model; the CRA role of SpaceCorps is with counsel (Q5). **Reversibility** partly one-way (legal roles). **Owner action** the counsel questions (Q5, Q20).

**Decision.** All pack content is open and free, and SpaceCorps sells, warrants or operates nothing in compliance: no Assurance service, no legal-change SLA, no capped warranty, no hosted Peppol, KSeF or Veri*Factu gateway, no certification programme. Packs are "as is" under Apache-2.0 (D38) and earn trust through golden cases and oracles, not a contract. Country packs are signed bundles with an oracle-gated ladder (D34). Transmission to authorities and networks goes through adapters with bring-your-own credentials, or through a certified access point or platform (PA) the user chooses; SpaceCorps operates no access point. France by integrating with a PA, not accrediting; Spain gated behind counsel (EUR 150,000 per year per system type). SpaceCorps is the publisher (D1). Whether it is a CRA manufacturer, an open-source steward or neither, for a free project (with the sample-data sandbox of D83 and the planned hosting service of OR-22 in view), is for counsel (Q5). Until advised, the vulnerability-handling runbook (24 h, 72 h, 14 days) is prepared as if SpaceCorps were the manufacturer (Proposed; drilled by 2027-06-01).

**Why.** Mandates move faster than releases, and a free project cannot sell a service level against them. **Superseded alternative.** The company as CRA manufacturer plus an EU non-profit steward, paid operated, warranted or certified services, the Assurance service with a capped warranty, and an own Peppol access point near 250 businesses (former D17). **Rejected.** Owning every regime; partner-only. **Consequences.** Counsel before any pack claims conformance (CN-16). The E&O, Data Act, SLA and hosted-contract items of the old operating model fall away for the software and the sandbox; the hosting service of OR-22 reopens the Data Act, hosted-contract and CRA-role items for it (ENGINEERING 11, C10). The L2 "paid local partner" pack tier becomes a third-party maintainer tier (section 4). **Evidence.** OR-2, OR-10, OR-11; R25 B, R17 s7, R09.

#### D18 AI posture
**Status** BINDING. **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** Bring your own key or run models locally; there is no obop credit wall and no hosted credits (OR-2; the former optional paid convenience at cost plus 20% is withdrawn). The typed command surface is the only agent surface (D80); MCP 2026-07-28 via `rmcp`. Agents are principals (permissions are the intersection of user and token scopes), use dry-run with a digest-bound `plan_id` and idempotency keys, and create drafts but never post or pay without human approval. A governed semantic layer answers questions; a fully self-hosted profile exists; every AI feature can be switched off per module; no non-commercial weights by default.

**Why.** Odoo 20 meters AI through IAP credits; agents need the same permission path as users. **Rejected.** Metered credits; a parallel AI API; raw text-to-SQL. **Consequences.** An approval policy engine (auto, confirm, approve-by-role) is a W2 deliverable (placement per D25, Proposed); HR and credit-scoring features are AI-optional for the EU AI Act high-risk date 2027-12-02. **Evidence.** R16, R21, R26 A2 (its hosted-credit line is superseded by OR-2).

#### D19 Payroll strategy
**Status** BINDING; reference country DEFERRED. **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** A generic rule engine in core (CEL, decimals, explainable and reproducible runs), separately versioned country packs, and provider connectors. No statutory filing in core, no arbitrary code in the database, no AI in hiring decisions by default, effective-dated employee versions. A pack without golden cases cannot be stable. The first reference pack (France via `modele-social`, or US federal) is decided on 2029-04-01 (M30) and need not match the first accounting country.

**Why.** Payroll is the moat and the liability; Odoo's open payroll left Community in 13.0. **Rejected.** Country engines in core; connectors only. **Superseded alternative.** A paid, warranted payroll service alongside the open engine (former D19 consequence). **Consequences.** Rule content is open and comes with no warranty; there is no payroll service (OR-2). Payroll stays a W3 or later item in demand order (D86). **Evidence.** R13, R25 B7, R26 (its payroll-service line is superseded).

#### D20 Report and PDF engine
**Status** PROPOSED. **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** yes (Q12).

**Decision.** Typst behind `obop-report` (PDF/A-3b, PDF/UA-1) for all documents in W1-W2; user-designed templates through minijinja (fuel and recursion limits) feeding Typst. No wkhtmltopdf. A browser renderer only ever runs in an isolated worker with no network or file access.

**Why.** One renderer, archival output, no headless-browser attack surface. **Rejected.** Typst plus pooled Chromium; paper-muncher. **Consequences.** User-designed HTML templates are limited to the minijinja-to-Typst path. **Evidence.** R15, R18, R24, R06.

#### D21 Performance claims
**Status** BINDING. **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** Publish no multiple over Odoo until the Odoo-versus-obop harness runs on fixed hardware; cite Odoo only by its documented worker formula (about 6 users per worker). Position Rust on correctness and operations.

**Why.** Gains are unmeasured: the research infers 3-10x per box (design inference [I], R22 s3.2; not for publication) and models infrastructure at about 6% of year-3 revenue (estimate, R26; that financial model is not independently reproducible and is historical since OR-6). **Rejected.** Publishing 5-10x targets now. **Consequences.** Running the harness monthly from M9 (calendar per D26, Proposed) and publishing driver and results is the way to lift the ban. **Evidence.** R22 MUST 9, R27 A7, R26 T8.

#### D22 Odoo-compatibility surface
**Status** BINDING; endpoint DEFERRED. **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** Odoo's domain syntax as the AST, `module.name` external ids, and an importer (JSON-2 and exports, dry-run in a transaction, identity ledger). No XML-RPC. Clean-room (F3). A JSON-2-compatible `/json/2/` endpoint is DEFERRED until three design partners ask or Odoo's RPC removal (fall 2028) creates migration demand.

**Why.** Switching cost is the main acquisition lever. **Rejected.** Clean break; XML-RPC compatibility. **Consequences.** The importer is a product with a CI round-trip test. **Evidence.** R08, R21, R03, R04.

#### D23 Pin policy
**Status** RATIFIED (OR-8), with I-3 and I-4 (OR-9). **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no (Q15, the pnpm major, stays open).

**Decision.** "Latest" means the newest release that has passed a cooldown of 7 to 14 days, or its `x.y.1` patch. The rules are section 1.1. From 2026-10-01 until Rust 1.99.x is adopted the project intentionally runs one release behind the literal F1 and F2 wording; in general the toolchain runs up to about three weeks behind the newest release. Rust 1.99.x is adopted inside the rule's window (eligible 2026-10-15, adopt by 2026-10-22); that follows mechanically from this policy and is not a separate ruling. **Why.** Three recent Rust patch releases fixed miscompilations and Vite+ 1.0 is days old. **Superseded alternative.** Literal newest-on-release-day, floating `stable` (R06 MUST 1 "move to 1.99 immediately"). **Rejected.** Adopting 1.99.0 on day zero. **Consequences.** A dated adoption log lives in the repository. **Evidence.** OR-8; R06, R07, R27 B8.

#### D24 Canonical vocabulary
**Status** BINDING for the terms and the ambiguous words they forbid (business, company, party, pack, module, app, port and the rest of section 4); the renames (`BusinessTx`, `BusinessRouter`, `business_release_state`) and the X and L prefixes are RATIFIED by the owner on 2026-10-01 (OR-14); the W0-W4 labels are RATIFIED in use (OR-13); the T0-T3 tier labels are PROPOSED with D25 (Q8). **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** yes for the T labels (Q8).

**Decision.** Section 4 defines terms and forbids ambiguous ones. The renames there (`BusinessTx`, `BusinessRouter`, and the X and L prefixes) are naming changes, RATIFIED on 2026-10-01 (OR-14); no code uses either name yet, so they cost nothing today. The rulings add vocabulary: plugin, typed command, parity, publisher, verified and the install and copy modes. **Why.** "Tenant", "business", "company", "group" and "T" labels carried several meanings across R01-R28. **Rejected.** Keeping R23's "T0 (21) and T1 (19)" sets next to R01's tiers. **Evidence.** R23 DR-2.

#### D25 Module tiers and delivery order
**Status** PROPOSED (the T0 app list needs the owner); the W1 cut itself is D86 (RATIFIED, OR-13, OR-15). **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** yes (Q8).

**Decision.** Tiers T0-T3 and wave placement as in section 5. D86 (OR-13) governs W1, replacing R20's cut lines; R23's graph is the dependency law, not the delivery list. Tier answers "is a business OS complete without it"; wave answers "when". **Why.** R01, R20 and R23 scoped the first delivery differently, and OR-13 then cut it. **Superseded alternative.** R20 s9.3's seven-app loop (with Purchase, Payments and Portal) as the W1 list. **Rejected.** R23's 40-module sets as the delivery list; R01's full T1 as the MVP. **Consequences.** HR, assets and expenses are T1 but ship in W3, and the employee core that `timesheet` needs ships with `timesheet` in the services variant (W3, D86). **Evidence.** OR-13; R01 s6, R20 s9, R23 D.3.

#### D26 Release waves and calendar
**Status** RATIFIED (OR-13) for the German pack target 2027-09-30, which the ruling names; RATIFIED as kept, on the reading below, for the wave windows W0-W4 and the 1.0 target 2028-04-18; every other date (the 0.x releases, the French and Polish pack dates, the CRA dates) is PROPOSED. **Reading of OR-13.** The owner's words are "keep the dates" and only the German date is named; taking "the dates" to include the wave windows and the 1.0 target is an interpretation, not a separate ruling. **Reversibility** two-way door except regulatory anchors. **Owner action** the reading above and the Proposed dates are confirmed with Q8 and Q16.

**Decision.** Waves W0-W4 and one calendar (section 5): keep the dates, shrink the scope (OR-13). Scope moves and dates do not: if M0 or capacity slips, W1 compresses along the slip order of D86, never the 2027-09-30 target, because the all-issuer e-invoicing mandate from 2028-01-01 is the only hard external driver. Three milestones once conflated as "GA" stay separate: "public beta" (0.5, 2027-07-06, a public pre-release with no commercial offer; the free hosted sandbox of D83 opens with it), "pack stable" (DE, 2027-09-30) and "1.0" (2028-04-18, the first stable release, carrying the D85 support window). "Paid beta", "paid cloud", "SLA plan" and "Assurance service" no longer exist (OR-2, OR-11). Gate G0 (public repository) was overtaken on 2026-10-26, when the owner made the repository public with its full history (OR-26). What the gate named stays open as work and no longer gates visibility: counsel on the name, the CRA role and the DCO wording (Q2, Q5, Q16), the DCO check (a CI job built when outside contributors appear, OR-30), and the D33 P1 files (`SECURITY.md`, signed releases, SBOM). Exit criteria are correctness and adoption criteria that a very small team controls (section 5.2); none depends on paying customers or ARR.

**Why.** The owner's ruling: keep the dates, shrink the scope. R20, R25, R26 and R27 dated "GA" and "paid cloud" differently. **Superseded alternative.** A paid beta on 2027-07-06, an SLA plan and Assurance DE service from 2027-10-01, exit criteria of 10 paying and 50 production organisations (40 paying at the W2 exit, EUR 1M ARR in W3), SOC 2 and ISO dates, and a G0 that needed a live CLA bot and a published paid list (former D26). **Rejected.** One "GA" word; shipping a "1.0" early to dodge the CRA (Q5). **Consequences.** Whether a very small team can meet the German date is a named risk (D84); the mitigation is scope, in the order D86 states. The CRA dates (technical file 2027-11-30, sign-off 2027-12-10) apply only if counsel finds SpaceCorps to be a manufacturer (Q5). **Evidence.** OR-5, OR-13; R20 s10, R25 A8, R27 C10.

#### D27 Integration contract
**Status** BINDING. **Reversibility** one-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** Mechanisms in order: port call (`Arc<dyn Port>` from the owner's `*-api` crate; optional ports are `Option<Arc<...>>` and need no bridge), transactional `TxEvent`, extension point, durable event (outbox plus per-subscriber delivery, `SKIP LOCKED`, lease). Rules R1-R7: one command one transaction; per-aggregate ordering with `aggregate_version`; at-least-once with `inbox_processed`; compensation by reversal documents, sagas only across businesses; an `xtask` dependency law (implementation crates depend only on kernel crates and `*-api` crates; no implementation-to-implementation edges); handler traits owned by `obop-events`; `auto_install` per business. Hub ports `DemandPort`, `QtyContributionPort`, `PostingPort`, `InvoiceableSource` keep bridges linear. Implementers (CONVENTIONS 7.1; those beyond the first are Proposed until this record is amended): `stock` for `DemandPort`; `sales` for `QtyContributionPort` (`purchase` for purchase lines, Proposed); `account`, by account role, for `PostingPort`; `sales` and `subscriptions` for `InvoiceableSource` (`purchase`, `pos` and `expenses`, Proposed). `timesheet` is not an `InvoiceableSource` implementer, so time-only invoicing is an open product question (ARCHITECTURE 16.3 item 7, decided with Q8), and whether `payment` publishes a hub port instead of the `sale_payment` bridge would amend this record and is open (CONVENTIONS 9.1).

**Why.** Odoo's 400 auto-install bridges create gravity wells (`account` has 398 transitive dependents). **Rejected.** `_inherit` glue; Frappe-style stringly `doc_events`; microservices. **Consequences.** Build-time graph check; async ports use boxed futures until native dyn async stabilises. **Evidence.** R23 D, R01 s2.

#### D28 Kernel model and group structure
**Status** BINDING. **Reversibility** one-way door, mitigated by expand-only growth. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** Party is person or organisation; customer and vendor are per-company roles in `party_profile`. Principals (user, portal, guest, API client, agent, system) are not parties. `company` is flat and tied to one legal-entity party; `branch` is separate; groups are `ownership_interest` edges; VAT groups are `fiscal_unit`. Documents follow a header-line pattern with state machines as registry data, a three-value kernel lifecycle and derived progress fields. W1 ships one company with `company_id NOT NULL`, composite FKs and a dormant `branch`; multi-company arrives in W2, intercompany in W3, consolidation in W4 (wave placement per D25 and D26, Proposed).

**Why.** Odoo overloads one company tree (a parent cannot later become a branch). **Rejected.** A single company tree; customer and vendor as separate tables. **Consequences.** Later work rewrites no keys. **Evidence.** R23 B-C.

#### D29 Registry, ORM and Rust stack
**Status** BINDING. **Reversibility** one-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** One runtime registry (typed Rust models plus business rows) drives ORM, API, OpenAPI, MCP, views, access, webhooks, imports, docs and the semantic layer. Field storage has three tiers: typed columns, promoted custom columns, JSONB properties; no EAV. In-house ORM on sqlx 0.9 and sea-query 1.0; SeaORM only for fixed system tables. Domain AST compiled to SQL with bound parameters. Stack: tokio only; axum isolated in `obop-http`; utoipa 6; `Money` over rust_decimal; jiff for time-zone logic.

**Why.** Studio-class customisation, APIs and agents are cheap only if built-in behaviour is metadata. **Rejected.** A static ORM as the core; EAV. **Consequences.** Compile times in a 100-plus crate workspace need a strict crate DAG and sccache. **Evidence.** R08, R06, R02, R23.

#### D30 Database-enforced integrity
**Status** BINDING. **Reversibility** one-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** PostgreSQL enforces a deferred balanced-entry trigger, posted-row immutability, lock dates, gapless legal numbers assigned at posting from a row-locked counter, a per-journal hash chain, a unique quant key with `ON CONFLICT`, and immutable done stock moves. Corrections are reversal documents. Stock valuation serialises per company and product.

**Why.** Odoo checks balance only in the ORM and tolerates duplicate quants. **Rejected.** Rust-only invariants. **Consequences.** Triggers and the hash chain ship in migrations; posting assigns numbers late in the transaction. **Evidence.** R08, R09, R10, R23.

#### D31 Public API and versioning
**Status** BINDING. **Reversibility** two-way door until stable endpoints exist. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** The API is generated from the registry: REST/JSON with action endpoints, OpenAPI 3.1 (3.2 later), RFC 9457 errors, cursor pagination with `updated_since` and tombstones, atomic `POST /$batch`, `Idempotency-Key`, Standard Webhooks, an OAuth 2.1 provider, MCP. URL major `/api/v1` plus a date-versioned `Obop-Version` header; stable endpoints are never removed within a major. Free and ungated (there are no plans, OR-2). REST, MCP and the CLI expose one command catalogue (D80). No XML-RPC; no GraphQL in W1.

**Why.** Odoo gates its API to the Custom plan, removes RPC in 2028 and has no atomic multi-step call. **Rejected.** Plan-gated API; OData or GraphQL first. **Consequences.** Per-business OpenAPI includes custom fields and installed packages; deprecation windows follow D85 (12 months before 1.0; after 1.0 Proposed). **Evidence.** R21, R27 C2, R08.

#### D32 Extension packaging and trust
**Status** BINDING; the trust levels were amended by OR-3 (D81). **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** One `obop.toml` for every package kind (pack, wasm, remote, native, theme), replacing R02's `module.toml` and R21's `obop.app.toml`. OCI artefacts with publisher ed25519 plus cosign keyless signatures, CycloneDX SBOM and a capability manifest diffed per upgrade. Trust levels: `private` (business-own), `community`, `verified` and `core` (first-party); the registry tiers are `community` and `verified` (OR-3, D81). Sideloading is always allowed; where one operator hosts many businesses it is limited to X1-X4 (R24 s9). Nightly compatibility runs; a kill switch (a revocation list that the CLI checks). Three data layers (module, business overlay, user); business customisation exports as a package.

**Why.** Compatibility is a product; Odoo's lack of it created OCA and OpenUpgrade. **Superseded alternative.** Five trust levels private, community, reviewed, partner, core, with `partner` as a commercial SLA tier and a package registry service shipping in W2 (former D32). **Rejected.** Twenty-style no-review; raw SQL for third parties. **Consequences.** `reviewed` merges into `verified` and `partner` is retired (no partner programme, OR-2). The registry ships in stages (D81, Proposed): a git-backed index with the CLI in W2, a service later. **Evidence.** R24 s5, s9; OR-3.

#### D33 Security invariants
**Status** BINDING. **Reversibility** one-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** The R18 P0 list is design-time law: a business-scoped transaction handle (`BusinessTx`, the renamed `TenantTx`, D24); one query path with default-deny; typed elevation; append-only hash-chained audit log; Argon2id, TOTP and passkeys with revocable sessions; one SSRF-hardened egress client and an isolated report renderer; integer money with `overflow-checks`; personal-data flags driving erasure; expand/contract migrations; cargo-deny, cargo-audit and a frozen pnpm lockfile from the first commit.

**Why.** These cannot be retrofitted. **Rejected.** Hardening later. **Consequences.** P1 items (signed releases, SBOM, SECURITY.md, OIDC, SCIM subset) precede the first public release; P2 (cargo-vet, audit checkpoints, pentest) precede audits. **Evidence.** R18 s12.

#### D34 i18n and localisation mechanics
**Status** BINDING. **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** PO catalogues with an ICU MessageFormat 1 subset (Lingui on TypeScript; PO parser and `icu` in Rust); Weblate; 12 target locales at first pack stable; machine translation is a suggestion and banned for statutory labels; RTL and CJK fixtures in CI; search is capability-detected (`pg_trgm`, `unaccent`, ICU; PGroonga for CJK). Packs are signed, effective-dated bundles on `stable`, `preview` and `edge` channels; gate ladder draft, preview, stable, certified; oracles CEN artefacts, KoSIT, KSeF DEMO. BAS data never enters the public repository. MessageFormat 2 is reviewed in 2027-H2.

**Why.** Odoo itself uses Weblate; law moves faster than releases. **Rejected.** Fluent (doubles catalogues); MessageFormat 2 now (no mature Rust implementation). **Consequences.** Data, pack and core cadences differ; extension availability on managed PostgreSQL is unverified. **Evidence.** R25 B-C.

#### D35 UI architecture and accessibility
**Status** BINDING. **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** Views are versioned JSON ASTs with named slots and an effective-view resolver; view-type and field-renderer registries. WCAG 2.2 AA is a release gate (React Aria, axe, every drag has a non-drag path). Logical CSS only; DTCG 2025.10 tokens as runtime CSS variables; four saved-view scopes; imports with dry-run and batch rollback. The v1 catalogue includes views Odoo gates (report viewer, reconciliation split view, approvals inbox). Views are clients of the typed commands (D80): a view shows and does only what the command catalogue exposes, and UI parity with the commands is a gate of the first stable release (OR-4).

**Why.** Avoids Odoo's multi-era UI and makes accessibility structural. **Rejected.** XPath-style patching; commercial grids. **Consequences.** CI bans physical margins; a 100k-row performance budget is enforced. **Evidence.** R19, R07.

#### D36 Quality and release engineering
**Status** BINDING. **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** Template-database test harness; append-only migration ledger, never squashed inside a line; N-1 chain test from 0.3, N-2 plus a 1,000-business fan-out from 0.5 (release numbers per D26, Proposed); a registry-generated IDOR matrix before any multi-business deployment; proptest invariants for ledger, tax, pricing and stock; a Tryton-style inherited structural suite; expand/backfill/contract tags enforced in CI; reproducible double builds for stable releases; artefacts, SBOMs and attestations kept 10 years (Proposed, pending the CRA role, Q5); a CLI/API/MCP parity test from the first commands (D80); the repository is public (since 2026-10-26), so it runs on GitHub-hosted standard runners, free for a public repository, with a budget of USD 300-800 per month if larger runners are needed (self-funded, OR-6); the runner plan for heavier builds stays open (Q17).

**Why.** It is what a very small team can sustain, with ERPNext and Tryton precedent. **Rejected.** Migrate-per-test; hand-made fixtures. **Consequences.** Quality-platform work is a standing duty of the owner and the agents, not a headcount (R27's 0.5-1 FTE is historical); benchmark the three clone strategies in sprint one. **Evidence.** R27 A-B.

#### D37 Runtime topology and edge store
**Status** BINDING; the "SQLite is never the domain database" clause is superseded by D44. **Reversibility** two-way door (low to medium). **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** One binary with roles (`http`, `cron`, `bus`, `ext`, `hub`, `all`), static musl with embedded frontend assets, PostgreSQL behind PgBouncer 1.26+ (direct connections for migrations, LISTEN and session locks), blobs behind an `object_store` trait on S3-compatible storage (not MinIO), in-process cache, pgBackRest for PITR. Solo and demo installs use embedded PostgreSQL (superseded in part by D44: SQLite is the default for local and solo use; embedded PostgreSQL stays possible and is not built). SQLite is only the fixed-schema edge store `obop-edge` (at most 15 tables, `PRAGMA user_version`), never a projection of the server schema. The store hub is the same binary on rusqlite; POS shells are Tauri 2 plus a Rust hardware agent, with a PWA baseline; browser-only mode uses sqlite-wasm. No Node on customer servers.

**Why.** One binary plus PostgreSQL is the operations differentiator; two SQL dialects would double the domain layer. **Rejected.** Dual-target SQL; Electric, PowerSync, Zero; Redis 8. **Consequences.** The NOTIFY lock stays bounded by cell size. **Evidence.** R23 DR-7, R22, R24, R12.

#### D38 Docs, disclosure and legal pack
**Status** BINDING. **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** Docs are plain Markdown on VitePress 2 inside Vite+ (pinned; Starlight is a fallback only with an explicit owner waiver of the Vite+ rule (F2), as for Astro in D14, because it is an Astro framework with its own CLI that `vp` does not drive; the waiver is not assumed, Q14), with reference pages generated from the registry. Security advisories reach everyone at once (no customer-tiered embargo). Full data export ships in every install, never gated. SpaceCorps operates no hosted service for others today apart from the free sample-data sandbox (OR-11, OR-20, D83), which holds no customer data and no customer contracts, so there is no hosted contract and there are no Data Act switching terms today; anyone who hosts obop for others carries those duties, SpaceCorps itself once the hosting service of OR-22 is offered (open counsel items, ENGINEERING 11, C10). The warranty disclaimer of Apache-2.0 applies, with no compliance warranty of any kind; every tax or payroll computation stores the rule-pack version and inputs. Default storefront, checkout, portal and POS are WCAG 2.2 AA and ship the consumer withdrawal function.

**Why.** Odoo's Enterprise-first disclosure and paid upgrades are grievances; export is a user right, and a Data Act duty for any host. **Superseded alternative.** A hosted contract with Data Act switching terms, a tiered Assurance warranty and capped statutory carve-outs (former D38 clauses). **Rejected.** Tiered embargoes. **Consequences.** VitePress 2 is still alpha, so content stays plain Markdown. The documentation is Apache-2.0 like the rest (D1). **Evidence.** R28 s2-6.

#### D39 Collaboration and messaging services
**Status** BINDING. **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** Chatter is first-party and record-bound. The email gateway is built on Stalwart's permissive crates (`mail-parser`, `mail-builder`, `mail-auth`), `lettre` and `async-imap`, never the AGPL Stalwart server. Mailboxes are OAuth-only (Gmail API, Microsoft Graph): no EWS, no basic SMTP AUTH. Collaborative documents use Yjs 13.6 with `yrs` 0.28, pinned together. E-signature is honest SES with QTSP connectors for qualified signatures. One generic approvals engine. No copyleft-only components in core (HyperFormula, BlockNote XL, Documenso).

**Why.** Microsoft begins default-disabling EWS in Exchange Online tenants from 2026-10-01 (rolling, admin-reversible) and turns it off permanently on 2027-04-01; it disables basic SMTP AUTH by default for existing tenants at the end of December 2026. **Rejected.** LISTEN/NOTIFY bus; self-labelled "advanced" signatures. **Consequences.** A CI licence gate (now also enforcing the Apache-2.0 allow-list of D1); a cross-implementation Yjs/yrs test. **Evidence.** R15, R21.

#### D40 Mask-and-restore at the command boundary
**Status** PROPOSED. **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** yes (Q21).

**Decision.** Every read surface of the typed command API (REST, MCP, CLI and the AI gateway) shapes its response through one masking policy, resolved per principal and per data class ([CONVENTIONS.md](CONVENTIONS.md) section 5) from registry metadata. Modes: `allow`, `mask` (a reversible token such as `PERSON_12`, stable inside a business and principal session), `redact` (irreversible) and `deny`. Defaults for agent principals: the three sensitive classes `deny`, other `personal_data` fields `mask`, `secret` never leaves the vault. On write, a token in a command input is resolved server-side only if the principal could read the underlying record under D10; an unresolvable token rejects the command. Token tables sit in the business database and are erased with the data subject (erasure, [spec/security-privacy.md](spec/security-privacy.md) 4.6). Masking is applied where the data leaves the permission path, so it cannot be skipped by a client, a module or a model route.

**Why.** Agents and external MCP clients send what they read to a model the business does not control; a block-or-allow egress rule (ai 4.5) either withholds too much to be useful or leaks too much. **Rejected.** Client-side masking; masking only inside the AI gateway (W3), which leaves W1 MCP unprotected; raw text-to-SQL. **Consequences.** The masking engine is a kernel component; a field without a data class cannot be masked, which D41 forbids. Whether token stability should span sessions is open (Q21). **Evidence.** D10, D18, D33, R16, R18 s12.

#### D41 Agent-ready module contract
**Status** PROPOSED. **Reversibility** one-way door once third parties publish. **Owner action** yes (Q21).

**Decision.** Everything a module adds is reachable by agents and operators through the same generated surfaces, and nothing is reachable without metadata. Every model, field, command, view and event declares a description, a permission group and a data class (or an explicit non-personal marker); REST, OpenAPI, MCP tools and `obop` CLI commands are generated from the registry, never hand-written, so a command exists on all four or on none. A module may narrow who may call a command (permission, `agent_readable`), not remove it from the surface. Its `obop.toml` also declares its network destinations and the data classes it will egress. The structural suite and the install gate reject a first-party or marketplace package that fails any of this.

**Why.** "Everything has AI and CLI access" and "agents are principals" (D18) only hold if no module can add a side door or an unclassified field. **Rejected.** Opt-in agent exposure; hand-written per-module CLIs. **Consequences.** Extends the structural checks of [ENGINEERING.md](ENGINEERING.md) section 1.4 and the package manifest of D32; raises the cost of writing a module slightly and removes a class of review work. **Evidence.** D18, D29, D31, D32, R21, R24.

#### D42 Agent safety timing
**Status** PROPOSED. **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** yes (Q21).

**Decision.** Agent principals and delegation, the dry-run and `plan_id` write protocol, a minimal approval policy (`auto`, `confirm`, `approve-by-role`, with posting and paying always at least `confirm`) and D40 masking ship in W1 together with MCP. D18 and D25 place the full approval policy engine in W2 and the AI gateway in W3; those stay, and only the minimum needed to expose MCP safely moves forward.

**Why.** MCP in W1 without them lets any connected client read unmasked data and propose unapproved writes. **Rejected.** Delaying MCP to W2 (loses the agent-first differentiator); shipping MCP read-only without masking. **Consequences.** Adds W1 scope (written under the D5 hiring gate, which OR-6 and D84 have since withdrawn: the capacity is the owner plus agents); [spec/ai.md](spec/ai.md) 4.1 to 4.3 and ROADMAP need the W1 content list updated if ratified. **Evidence.** D18, D25, R16 s11.

#### D43 Hosting providers
**Status** PROPOSED. **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** yes (Q21).

**Decision.** obop is hosting-neutral. It needs one container (the `obop` binary, D37), PostgreSQL 17 or 18 and optionally an S3-compatible store; nothing in the kernel names a provider. Provider integrations implement one `HostingTarget` port (provision, deploy, upgrade, backup and restore, domains, logs, scale), exposed as `obop host ...` subcommands (name Proposed) and as X4 packages. Reference targets: plain self-hosting (container plus PostgreSQL) and Sliplane, chosen as a convenience target because it gives a one-click path from a template to a running business; more providers (Hetzner, Fly.io, Render and others) are added through the same port. Sliplane's PostgreSQL versions, backup guarantees and extension support are unverified and are checked before the adapter ships. SpaceCorps already has a `Sliplane-Cli` that the adapter can build on. (Note 2026-10-27: this record is older than OR-22. There are no hosted plans for the software (D2); the paid hosting service that OR-22 plans runs on Sliplane dedicated servers, and the Sliplane provisioner of `obop-platform` already exists.)

**Why.** Easy deployment is a product goal; coupling to one vendor would contradict the open edition and exit promises (D38). **Rejected.** A Sliplane-only deploy path; per-provider code in the kernel. **Consequences.** The port needs a conformance test that every adapter passes, including a verified restore (ENGINEERING 1.1). **Evidence.** D37, D38; the provider facts above are not yet researched.

#### D44 Storage backends: SQLite locally, PostgreSQL for deployments
**Status** BINDING (owner-directed in chat, 2026-10-16). **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** Local development and solo use run on SQLite (`sqlite://PATH`, the default is `obop.db`); every deployment, including hosted targets such as Sliplane (D43), runs on PostgreSQL 17 or 18 (`postgres://...`). The storage layer (`obop-storage`) is one small contract with memory, SQLite and PostgreSQL backends, selected by `--db` or `OBOP_DATABASE_URL`, and every backend must pass the same contract test; PostgreSQL is tested in CI and locally against a container. This supersedes the clause of D37 that SQLite is never the domain database and the embedded-PostgreSQL solo profile as the default; embedded PostgreSQL stays possible. The database-enforced integrity guarantees of D30 (balance, immutability, gapless numbers, hash chain) exist on PostgreSQL only, so a SQLite database is for development and evaluation and the product never claims those guarantees there; the Assurance and compliance claims apply to PostgreSQL deployments.

**Why.** A zero-setup local start removes the biggest barrier for module authors and agents trying obop; PostgreSQL stays the production target. **Rejected.** SQLite everywhere (cannot meet D30); PostgreSQL required locally. **Consequences.** Migrations and the schema manager (`obop-schema`) target PostgreSQL first, so SQLite support for typed columns lags and may stay document-style; an export from SQLite to PostgreSQL is needed before any production use (Proposed); the SQLite backend must not appear in the compliance test matrix. **Evidence.** D6, D8, D30, D37.

#### D45 Installable declarative packages and the instance admin
**Status** BINDING (owner-directed in chat, 2026-10-16). **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** An instance administrator installs, upgrades and removes packages while the server runs, from an uploaded `obop.toml` or from a marketplace catalog. The first package tier is X1 (D11): models, fields with a data class and permission group, and state-transition actions, with no code. The install path parses the manifest, builds registry metadata, runs the D41 gate, checks for name collisions, and registers generated CRUD plus actions on the one dispatcher, so REST, MCP, the CLI, masking and agent approval apply to a new module with no further work. Model names must be the module id or start with `<id>_`; a package may not declare network destinations. Removal unregisters commands and keeps the data. Installing, upgrading and removing are `platform.admin` commands that agents may only propose (D18). The marketplace is a trait with a bundled catalog and an optional HTTP catalog (`OBOP_MARKETPLACE_URL`). `platform.admin` is a superuser group: it satisfies every group check for a human, while an agent's token scopes still list concrete groups. The control plane (`obop platform serve`) lets a person sign up and get their own instance; the owner's password hash seeds the instance's first admin.

**Why.** "Install modules, upload your own, download from a marketplace" needs code-free packages first: they are safe to install at runtime and already inherit every cross-cutting rule. **Rejected.** Native or Wasm packages as the first tier (X3 stays in D11 and comes later). **Consequences.** Package signing, publisher accounts, paid listings, schema migration on upgrade and custom commands beyond state transitions are not built; behaviour that needs code waits for X2 (CEL) and X3 (Wasm). **Evidence.** D11, D18, D32, D41.

#### D46 Registry-driven backoffice with floating panels and a Customize app
**Status** BINDING (owner-directed in chat, 2026-10-16). **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** The backoffice renders from `GET /api/meta`, which lists the modules, models, readable fields (with data class and personal-data flag) and actions the signed-in principal may use, so a module installed at runtime appears with no frontend change and a field a principal cannot read is never described to it. The design takes Odoo's concepts (app launcher, a control panel with breadcrumbs, search, group-by and a view switcher, list and kanban views, a form with a status bar and action buttons, a command palette on Ctrl or Cmd + K) and presents them as floating rounded panels on a tinted canvas, with the record opening in a side panel beside its list. The Customize app, an Odoo Studio counterpart, holds the theme (accent, mode, density, corners, per person or as the instance default), the app launcher layout (order, names, colours, visibility), module install and removal, and a module builder that writes an `obop.toml` and installs it. Instance settings are the `setting` model (readable by every principal, writable by `platform.admin`) so agents and the CLI see them too.

**Why.** The first backoffice was a generic table and read as unfinished; the product promise is one platform whose apps all look and behave alike. **Rejected.** A per-module hand-written UI (breaks "install and it just works"); copying Odoo's visuals. **Consequences.** Views beyond list and kanban (calendar, pivot, graph, report), a view designer, inline editing, bulk actions, saved filters and the chatter are not built; field types were added afterwards by D47, so the UI no longer has to infer money or state from names. **Evidence.** [spec/ux.md](spec/ux.md) 4.1 to 4.6 and 4.9, [research/19-ux-design-system.md](research/19-ux-design-system.md) section 2, Odoo 18 and 19 documentation for Studio, views and search.

#### D47 Typed fields
**Status** BINDING (owner-directed in chat, 2026-10-16). **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** Every field has a kind, serialised lowercase in manifests and in `/api/meta`: `text` (the default, JSON string), `longtext` (string, multi-line), `number` (JSON integer), `money` (JSON integer in minor currency units, never a float or a numeric string), `date` (string `YYYY-MM-DD`, a real calendar date, leap years included), `bool`, `select` (string, one of `options`) and `ref` (string, the id of an existing record of model `to`). A field also has `required` (default false): a required field must be present and non-null on create and may not be set to null on update; null or absent is allowed for the others. The generated create and update commands validate every input against these kinds before anything reaches the store, with errors of the form `<model>.<field> must be <kind description>` (HTTP 400); a `ref` is looked up in the store, so a dangling reference is an error. Validation sees real values, because the dispatcher restores masked tokens before the handler runs (D40). In `obop.toml` a field gains `kind`, `options` (select only), `to` (ref only) and `required`; inconsistent combinations are refused at parse time. A `ref` target must be a model of the same package (checked at parse time) or an already registered model (checked at install time). A `select` named `state` is the conventional status field and its options are the status order; `from` and `to` of a transition action must be among the options. The D41 gate additionally refuses a select without options or with duplicates, a ref with no target, and a field named `id`. `GET /api/meta` adds `kind` and `required` to every field, plus `options` for a select and `to` for a ref.

**Why.** The UI guessed types from names (`_minor` for money, `state` for status); agents and views need a contract they can rely on, and bad data should be refused at the door on every surface. **Rejected.** Validation in the UI only; typed PostgreSQL columns now. **Consequences.** Column types in PostgreSQL stay JSONB documents until the schema manager exists (D44), so kinds are enforced by the command layer, not by the database, and direct store writes (seeds, the erase command) bypass them. Floats are not a kind. Existing records are not re-validated, and the CLI converts `number`, `money` and `bool` arguments from text using the metadata. **Evidence.** D29, D40, D41, D45, D46.

#### D48 Governed fields and implicit transitions
**Status** BINDING (owner-directed through the integration review, 2026-10-16). **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** A *governed field* is a `select` field that is the `field` of at least one declared action (a transition: `field`, optional `from`, `to`). Transitions are part of the registry metadata (`CommandDef.transition`), declared by manifests and by built-in modules alike (`lead.stage`, `sale_order.state`, `invoice.state`). The installer registers, from that metadata, guards on the one dispatcher: a guard is a pre-hook on one command that sees the principal and the restored input, may refuse, normalise the input, name extra groups and demand approval, and is re-run when a proposal is approved. (1) `create` accepts a governed field only omitted (it becomes the first option) or equal to the first option. (2) `update` accepts a change of a governed field from `a` to `b` only as an implicit run of a declared transition with `to = b` and `from` unset or `= a`: the caller must hold that action's group, an agent's update is turned into an approval plan when the action needs approval (the approver must hold the action's group), and approval runs the action's own handler, so its side effects happen; any other change is refused with the targets reachable from `a`. An unchanged value is not a change. Ordinary fields of the same call are validated before any action runs, so a mixed update is applied completely or not at all. (3) Running an action (explicitly or implicitly) requires the current value to equal `from` and every `required` field to be set; the error names the field. (4) Guarded calls run under one dispatcher-wide lock from check to write. Manifests: action names are unique per model and may not be a generated command name; an action's `field` must be a `select` (any, not only `state`); `from` and `to` must be options and must differ.

**Why.** An integration review showed that any holder of a model's `write_group` could set `state` to `posted` through `update`, skipping the stricter group and the approval flag of the action; a permission model that the generic command bypasses is not one. **Rejected.** Special cases per module in the handlers (every new package would have to repeat them); removing `state` from `update` (breaks the UI's kanban drag and status bar, which send `update`); a separate permission on the field alone (cannot express per-transition groups or approval). **Consequences.** Seeds and direct store writes are not checked (D47 says the same of kinds). The lock serialises one server; two servers on one PostgreSQL database can still race between check and write until the database enforces transitions (D30). Only a single `select` field per action; no guards on other fields, so a posted record's other fields remain editable. Records created before an action gained `required` fields may need them filled before they can move. **Evidence.** D18, D41, D45, D47, MODULES.md section 6.1.

#### D49 Delete and server-side search
**Status** BINDING (owner-directed through the integration review, 2026-10-16). **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** Every model gets two more generated commands. `<model>.delete {id}` needs the model's `write_group`, is marked `needs_approval` (an agent only proposes it) and removes the record through `Backend::delete`; it is refused while any registered model has a `ref` field pointing at the record, with the referencing models, counts and up to five ids in the error. `/api/meta` reports `can_delete` per model. `<model>.list` accepts the optional `filter` (conditions `{field, op, value}` with ops `eq`, `ne`, `contains`, `in`, `gt`, `gte`, `lt`, `lte`, `is_null`, ANDed, validated against the field kinds), `order` (`[{field, dir}]`), `limit` (1 to 5000, default unlimited) and `offset`, and still returns a bare array. `<model>.search` takes the same input and returns `{rows, total}` with `total` counted before `limit` and `offset`. Comparison is typed (integers, calendar dates, option order). Filtering and ordering happen before masking and withholding, so a field the caller cannot read in clear (no group, secret class, or personal or sensitive data for an agent, D40) is refused with the same error as a field that does not exist, which closes the inference channel of D10. The query is evaluated in the command layer over the records of the model on every backend (memory, SQLite and PostgreSQL); only the returned page is masked.

**Why.** The client held every record to filter, sort and group it, which neither scales nor lets an agent ask a narrow question; and there was no way to remove a record. **Rejected.** Pushing predicates into PostgreSQL JSONB now (the typed columns of the schema manager are the right place, D44; a strict field whitelist makes it safe but it would be thrown away); a soft delete (no tombstones before the audit log exists); cascading deletes; changing the shape of `list`. **Consequences.** Search reads the whole model from the store, so it is linear in the table size; `obop-schema` replaces this. `delete` is a hard delete and is not the erasure story: personal data is anonymised by the owning module's `erase` command (`party.erase`), which keeps references valid and needs the privacy officer; delete only works on unreferenced records, and an agent can never delete without a human. The ref check sees only installed models. **Evidence.** D10, D29, D40, D41, D44, D47, D48.

#### D50 Public exposure and the public site
**Status** BINDING (owner-directed, 2026-10-16). **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** A package may declare `[model.public]` (docs/MODULES.md 3.6): `read` with a `published_field` and a whitelist of `fields`, `submit` with `submit_fields` and a `rate_limit_per_hour`, and token `receipt` fields. The D41 gate refuses a public field that is not class `none` or `public_directory`, is personal data, or is restricted to a group; a form may not collect sensitive classes or a governed state. The public is not a principal: `obop_commands::PublicApi` is the only door, it never goes through `Dispatcher::call`, and no bearer token can act as it. Submissions run the same typed validation and governed first-state rule as `create`; refs a visitor supplies must point at published records of public models; fields the route computes (prices) are validated but never come from the visitor; batches (an order and its lines) are one transaction (`Backend::insert_many`). `obop-site` renders the public website and shop server-side; `obop serve --public-site` mounts it at `/` and moves the backoffice to `/admin/`. A page or post is public through its `state` select (`published_value`), not a second boolean, so the approval on `publish` also gates public visibility.

**Why.** Every instance should be able to serve a shop and site from its own data without a framework, a build step or a second system, and without widening what anonymous visitors can touch beyond what the package states. **Rejected.** A public bearer token (any holder is "the public" everywhere); a separate boolean next to `state` (an agent could flip it without the publish approval); client-side rendering (no SEO, no agents). **Consequences.** No payment provider and no images yet (docs/STOREFRONT.md, Known gaps). **Evidence.** `docs/STOREFRONT.md` threat model with a named test per finding.

#### D51 Hosted MCP
**Status** BINDING (owner-directed in chat, 2026-10-17). **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** Every instance serves the MCP Streamable HTTP transport at `POST /mcp` (also `DELETE /mcp`; `GET /mcp` answers 405 because there is no server stream). It speaks the handshake-based revisions `2025-11-25`, `2025-06-18` and `2025-03-26` (JSON-RPC batches only on the last, as in those specifications) and answers `application/json`, never SSE. It is a transport only: `obop-http` carries messages and `obop-mcp` holds the one implementation of `initialize`, `tools/list` and `tools/call` that `obop mcp` (stdio) uses too, over the one `Dispatcher`, so permissions, masking and approvals are those of REST. Rules: `Authorization: Bearer` on every request, resolved by `Authenticator` like REST (session and agent tokens); a missing or bad token is 401 with `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer resource_metadata="<origin>/.well-known/oauth-protected-resource"`; `/.well-known/oauth-protected-resource` (RFC 9728) names the resource and the two token endpoints and omits `authorization_servers`, because there is no OAuth authorization server. `initialize` issues an `Mcp-Session-Id` (256 random bits) bound to the token that opened it; the session holds the masking `Vault`, so mask tokens are stable and restorable inside a session and isolated between sessions (this is the default of ai.md open question 0); an unknown, expired (2 h idle) or foreign session is 404, `DELETE` ends it. An `Origin` header must match the allow-list (`OBOP_ALLOWED_ORIGINS`, `OBOP_PUBLIC_URL`, same-origin on a loopback host) or the request is 403. Limits: 1 MiB body, 600 requests per minute per token, 25 sessions per token, 10 000 in all. Tools: one per visible command, `<model>__<command>`, with an `inputSchema` and `outputSchema` generated from the registry (kinds per D47, `filter`/`order`/`limit`/`offset` per D49, governed fields per D48 left out of `create`), annotations that are hints and never enforcement, and one extra read-only tool `obop__meta`. A result is structured content plus text; an approval-required call is a normal result with `status: "approval_required"` and a `plan_id`; a refused input is `isError`; an unknown or hidden tool is the same `-32602`. Discovery: `/.well-known/mcp/server-card.json` (and `/mcp/server-card`, `/.well-known/ai-catalog.json`), `x-mcp` in the OpenAPI document and a `Link` header on `/api/meta`.

**Why.** An agent should connect to a hosted instance by URL and token, with no local binary and no database access; the is-agentic checks look for an MCP endpoint and manifest. **Rejected.** Waiting for `rmcp` and OAuth 2.1 (D18 and ai.md 4.1 plan both; neither is needed to ship a safe token-based endpoint, and the hand-written subset is small); the stateless `2026-07-28` revision now (its per-request model has no place for the session vault, so token stability would have to be keyed on the token as REST does; deferred, not refused); faking an authorization server in the metadata; SSE streaming (nothing to stream); `listChanged` notifications (module install changes the list; clients start a new session). **Consequences.** Clients that only authenticate through an OAuth browser flow (the connector dialogs of the Claude apps) cannot connect until the OAuth provider of [integrations](spec/integrations.md) 4.2 exists. The MCP Server Card is an extension still in incubation (SEP-2127; the earlier draft used `/.well-known/mcp/server-card.json`), so the document may change shape; the file says so. Sessions and rate limits are records and counters in the database since D59, so several processes may serve one database without sticky routing. `rmcp` still replaces the hand-written core when 2026-07-28 and OAuth arrive; the tool generation module stays. **Evidence.** D18, D29, D31, D40, D42, D45, D47 to D49, ai.md 4.1 and 4.3, MCP specifications 2025-11-25 (transports, authorization) and 2026-07-28 (versioning), RFC 9728, SEP-2127.
#### D52 Declarative translations
**Status** BINDING (owner-directed in chat, 2026-10-17). **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** A package may translate its own metadata. Translations are `[labels.<lang>...]` tables in the `obop.toml` document (module name and description; model name and description; field label and description; select option labels; action label and description), so one text is the unit that is validated, installed, upgraded, stored, served by a marketplace and removed. A package may keep them in sibling files (`<id>.labels.toml`, or the `labels/<lang>/<id>.toml` layout of the bundled catalogue) that tooling concatenates. English remains the manifest itself; a field has no English `label` (the client derives one from the name) and a language entry overrides it. Language codes match `^[a-z]{2,3}(-[A-Z]{2})?$`; every key must name something the package declares; values are plain text (non-empty, trimmed, one line, no control characters, no `{ } < >`, 80 characters for labels, 400 for descriptions); an unknown table is a schema error. The registry carries them as `Module.translations`; `GET /api/meta` adds `labels`, `descriptions` and per-field `option_labels`, each only where translated, in the shape the client's `labelFor` and `describeFor` already read. The five built-in modules and the admin module declare theirs in the same format. The 22 bundled packages and the built-in modules are translated into German, French, Polish, Finnish, Swedish and Spanish; those texts are machine-drafted until a native reviewer sets them to `reviewed` in `packages/labels/<lang>/status.json`. A test fails when any bundled text is missing or identical to English without being allow-listed.

**Why.** Module and field names came from manifests in English only, so an installed module showed English inside a German or Finnish interface, and a third-party author had no way to fix that. **Rejected.** A per-language catalogue in the client for every package (cannot cover packages written later); one manifest per language (versions drift); label keys separate from the schema (typos pass silently); translating permission group names (they are identifiers). **Consequences.** Packages grow by roughly the size of their English text per language (the 256 KiB limit still applies to the whole document); free-text error messages, record data and the marketplace listing of a package that is not installed stay as the server sends them; labels are not inflected, so sentences that insert a model name use frames that tolerate the nominative; the machine-drafted state of the six languages is tracked, not hidden. **Evidence.** D34, D45, D46, D47, docs/MODULES.md section 3.7, docs/brand/LOCALIZATION.md sections 1.4 and 8.

*D52 amendment 2026-10-27: the instance default language is public: it is served as a meta tag to the pre-sign-in screens; nothing else of the settings is public.* The setting `OBOP_DEFAULT_LANGUAGE` (one of the seven shipped languages, `en` `de` `fr` `pl` `fi` `sv` `es`; a tag such as `sv-SE` names its language; an unknown value stops the server at start and names the variable; empty means none) names the language of the sign-in, two-factor, enrolment and forced-password screens, which are shown before `setting.get` can be called. `obop serve --static` adds `<meta name="obop-default-language" content="sv">` to the served back office page at request time, under the root and under the admin prefix: a language code and nothing else, and the page is sent `Cache-Control: no-cache` (when the tag is added the answer drops its validators and ignores the request's, so a copy kept from before the setting cannot hide it). The page reads the tag before its first render. The order is `?lang`, the person's saved choice, `settings.language` once the settings are known (while it is empty, the variable), the browser, English. *Not delivered:* a language read from `settings.language` for the screens before sign-in (that would make a setting public), the control plane's own pages, and the server-rendered pages (OAuth sign-in, forum, shop), which keep their own rules.

*D52 amendment 2026-10-28: the refusal of an assertion is worded in the language of a REST request.* An assertion may carry a `key` (`[a-z_]{1,40}`, unique in its action): `assert = [{ expr = "...", message = "...", key = "open_only" }]`. Label tables get one more slot, `[labels.<lang>.model.<m>.action.<a>.assert.<key>] message = "..."`, with the value rules of a label and the limit of a description, 400 characters, so that the longest manifest message fits. An unknown key in a label table (or one whose assertion has no `message`) refuses the install; `obop modules check --complete` lists a keyed assertion with a message and none in a language of the file; `obop modules labels` templates carry the messages. A REST call that fails an assertion answers with the message for the language of the request, else the manifest's: `?lang=`, the `obop_lang` cookie, then `Accept-Language` with q-values, a region read as its language, only `en` `de` `fr` `pl` `fi` `sv` `es` counting and a source that names none of them falling through to the next (the order of the forum and the status page, whose function, `obop_community::i18n::negotiate`, it uses). English is the manifest's own language, so a request for it, and a request that asks for nothing, get the manifest message and the answer is byte for byte what it was; a translated text keeps the pattern `<message> (assertion <expression> failed)`. The language travels in a request scope of `obop-commands` (`locale`, a thread-local the way the fault scope is) that only REST opens: MCP and the command line keep the manifest message. The same scope notes which assertion refused, so the 400 carries `code` `assertion_failed` (a new row of the error table, D56) and, for a keyed assertion, `assert_key`, whether the action was called or started by a `set_state` effect. The order has a consequence for an app: `?lang=` and the `obop_lang` cookie both outrank `Accept-Language`, and the cookie of a same-origin page can differ from what the page shows (an instance default the person never chose, a link with `?lang=`, an HttpOnly cookie of the forum that `document.cookie` cannot overwrite), so the back office sends the language it shows as `?lang=` on every command and approval, and as `Accept-Language` too (`en` for a pseudo-locale). `@obop/client` takes an optional `getLanguage()` and sends it as `Accept-Language`, which decides for an app with no cookies (the mobile apps), and the option `languageParam` adds `?lang=` to `call` and `bulkCreate` for a web app. *Not delivered:* a translation of any other free-text refusal (a required field, a state error, a guard), of the text of an assertion without a key, and in MCP, the command line and the bundled packages, which declare no keys.

*D52 amendment 2026-10-28: a text field can show the label of an option of a select field.* A `text` or `longtext` field may declare `shows_option_of = "<model>.<field>"` and an optional `when`, an expression of the expression language (D56) on the same record: `shows_option_of = "desk_case.status"`, `when = 'kind == "status"'`. The back office then draws a value that equals an option of that select with the option's label in the active language, by the lookup of the select itself (the `option_labels` of D52 for the active language, then the client table of the built-in modules, then the key as words), and any other value, or the value of a record on which the condition does not hold, as stored. Only the display changes: the stored value, `create` and `update`, filters, sorting, grouping keys, import, REST, MCP, the command line, an edit input and the field's own column in a CSV export are what they were (a record is named by its first required `text` field, else a numbered one, else its first `text` or `longtext` field, so a field that shows an option names the records of its model by the label, and a `ref` that points at such a record shows, sorts by and exports that name, as a ref column exports the name of a record and not its id). No language data is added, and a label table on the text field itself is still refused. The target is a reference like a ref's: `obop modules check` and the marketplace require the module that declares the select in the dependencies, and a live install accepts any registered model, as for a ref. The install gate refuses a field that is not text or longtext, a target that is not a select field with options, `when` without `shows_option_of`, and a condition that does not parse, names a field that is not on the model, is not true or false, reads a field of another permission group than the text field's (the label would show whether a value that the reader may not see holds), or reads a field of class `secret` (a user is never sent one, so the browser would see it empty; a test keeps the gate in step with `mode_for` of obop-masking). The condition is evaluated by the browser, because the API does not change, so it is limited to the part of the expression language the browser implements and the gate checks (`expr::check_display_subset`): fields of the record (every kind except `decimal` and `datetime`), text and whole-number literals up to 2^53 - 1, `true`, `false`, the six comparisons, `&&`, `||`, `!`, parentheses and `is_set`; no arithmetic, minus sign, decimals, instants, `if`, `concat` or aggregates. It has the meaning of the server's evaluator, which keeps the two honest: both read one file of cases (`web/apps/backoffice/src/displayCondition.fixtures.json`), so a change to either fails the test of the other (one limit cannot be pinned: a stored `number` or `money` value above 2^53 - 1 reaches the browser as a JSON double, so it is right against a literal but two such values compared with each other can differ from the server). `/api/meta` carries `shows_option_of` and `shows_option_when` on the field (the manifest key is `when`), the registry carries `FieldDef.shows_option`, and an MCP field schema says in its description that the field may hold an option key.
