A database holding only what operator commands wrote (a stored plan, an attestation, catalogue records, security events) is still new and is stamped at its first start. **Every database carries a random id** (`obop_meta/instance`, written once, at the stamp or at the first migration or start): its backups (manifest and data header) and rehearsal receipts repeat it, so a gate can tell a backup or a receipt of this database from one of another. `obop_meta/schema` stays the version servers compare. `obop migrate status` prints the ledger; `verify` compares ledger, code and version (not yet the live catalogue). *DDL ownership.* `obop_migrate::ddl` holds the table of known PostgreSQL 17 and 18 statement forms and their cost class (read as tokens, so a keyword inside a string or a name is not a keyword; one statement at a time, two are an error; `VACUUM` with an option list, the inline `UNIQUE`, `PRIMARY KEY`, `CHECK` and `REFERENCES` of an added column and a default that calls a function not known to be stable are priced; a form with no price is refused, not guessed; more than thirty statements and the shapes the review found are tested), the policy (`lock_timeout` 5 s, `statement_timeout` 30 min, a bounded, jittered retry) and the rule that a `blocking` or `rewrite` step on a table above 1,000,000 records runs only with `--window`; `Step` carries the class, the DDL class, `compat_n_minus_1` and `downtime` (a step that is neither compatible nor declares downtime fails a test), and `obop migrate plan` prints them with the row counts of the tables the step names and stores the plan (`obop_migration_plan`, keyed by a digest of the pending steps' checksums). **The one component that executes DDL, and the test that no other code issues DDL on the serving path, are D77's**; this record only prepares the classifier, the policy and the hook.

*Gates (053).* `obop migrate run` with nothing pending is a no-op that says "up to date" and exits 0 on every database, and asks for none of what follows (it still refuses a database newer than the build or a drifted ledger). With steps pending, `obop migrate run` on PostgreSQL is **strict**: it refuses unless (a) a verified backup of this database (its id equals the database's) no older than 24 hours is named (`--backup FILE`, opened with its key when sealed) or is taken first into `--backup-dir`/`OBOP_BACKUP_DIR` (read back and authenticated; the backup an earlier failed attempt of this migration took is used again, not another), and its name goes into the ledger; (b) `obop migrate plan` stored this plan; (c) the backup volume, and a SQLite file's, have twice the database's size free, and for a PostgreSQL host's own disk (which a client cannot read) the operator passes `--disk-ok`; (d) a rehearsal of this plan passed (`obop migrate rehearse --from BACKUP --out receipt.json` restores into a scratch database that may not be the production one, runs the plan, the drill's checks and a fixed read and write set, and reports time, the longest single transaction and records; the receipt is an HMAC under the server key, valid a week, bound to the plan's digest, to the id of the database the rehearsed backup came from, to the engine of the scratch database and to the number of records it held; a receipt of another database fails the gate, and one made on another engine than production (the default scratch is a temporary SQLite file) passes with a `rehearsal_engine` warning that the statements' time and locks on this engine were not measured; a table-locking step over one second fails it); (e) the ledger is clean, the database is not newer than the build, the migration lock is free, and a long-lock step on a big table has `--window`. With WAL archiving on, `pg_create_restore_point('pre-migration-<version>')` must succeed and its name and position go into the ledger. A PostgreSQL server that cannot be asked about itself (its own connection failed) is a failing `server` check in strict mode, never a silently dropped gate, and `--disk-ok` is asked for either way. On SQLite and in memory the same safeguards warn (`--gates strict|relaxed`, `OBOP_MIGRATION_GATES`, an unknown value is an error); the ledger, the schema, the lock and the window stay failures. **`--dry-run` is a preview that needs no gate**: it prints the plan, what each step would do and the checks that would refuse a real run, and writes nothing (`migrate plan --dry-run` stores no plan either); `migrate plan` without it writes the plan to the database. `module.upgrade` and `module.rollback` that rewrite records pass a `MigrationGate` port of `obop-pkg` with the module lock held and before any write: the binary takes the verified backup into `OBOP_BACKUP_DIR` first; on PostgreSQL no directory means refusal with nothing changed. A server that migrates itself at start (`--auto-migrate`) takes the verified backup **inside the migration, under its lock, once the drift check and the plan say that steps will run** (a start that is refused takes none, two servers started together take one, and a server that fails to migrate and is restarted again and again reuses the backup of the first attempt, while it is under 24 hours old, instead of writing one per start), checks that the volume has twice the database's size free, and stops if the backup fails; without `OBOP_BACKUP_DIR` it says so and migrates; it does not demand a stored plan or a rehearsal (that is the choice of auto-migrate). `serve` reads and checks the settings of the backup timer before it opens the database, so a typo stops the start with nothing migrated. **Rollback is `obop migrate rollback --from BACKUP --reason TEXT --yes`**: under the migration lock, verify the backup (a backup of another database is refused without `--other-database`), replace the database with it, and record the reason, the backup and what the discarded ledger said in the ledger and the security log **in the same transaction as the restore**, so a restored database never exists without them (the uploaded files follow the records; if one cannot be written the error says `restore_files_failed`, exit 5, the records are in, and the same command with `--force` writes the files). Every uploaded file of a backup is checked to hash to its id when the backup is made (a file that rotted on disk is left out and named in the manifest) and again when it is verified, so a restore does not find that out half way.

*Backup format 2 (046).* The format-1 stream (gzip JSON Lines) is sealed in 64 KiB chunks with ChaCha20-Poly1305 (`obop_keys::stream`: the STREAM construction, nonce = random prefix, chunk counter, final flag; the header is the associated data of every chunk; compress-then-encrypt is acceptable because a backup is not interactive) under a random per-backup data key that the cleartext, authenticated header carries wrapped by the current key of the **backup key ring**: `OBOP_BACKUP_KEY` (with `OBOP_BACKUP_KEY_PREVIOUS`), else the server key ring from the environment; never a key generated into the database (it is inside the backup) or the development key. The manifest adds the chunk count, the head of a hash chain over the chunk tags and an HMAC-SHA256 over itself under a subkey of the key; `records_sha256` (a digest over the record lines alone) is added to both formats' manifests for the drill. `obop backup verify` opens every chunk, checks the chain and the MAC, and the stream's end; `--structure-only` checks a sealed file without a key and says it proved nothing about authorship (the chain commits to the chunk tags, their number and order, not to the bodies: the manifest's checksum of the whole file covers those against damage, and only the key authenticates them); `--canary TEXT` fails if the text is in a sealed file as stored, and always fails for a plain backup, which gunzip reads. **Where a key is configured a plain (format 1) backup is refused** (`backup_not_sealed`, exit 4: a plain pair can be made by anyone who can write the backup directory, so it proves nothing; `--allow-plain` says the operator vouches for it); with no key configured nothing changes. A sealed backup cannot be given a `.gz` name. Backups are made so that a crash leaves nothing half there: the manifest and the data file are each written under a name of their own, flushed, published without replacing an existing name, and the directory flushed; a run that fails removes what it began, a killed run's part files are swept by the next, and files and directories are created 0600 and 0700 on Unix. A sealed backup without its key stops `verify`, `restore`, `migrate rollback` and `drill` with the code `restore_key_required` and **exit status 3**, writing nothing, not even the target database; `backup_not_sealed` is 4 and `restore_files_failed` 5. `OBOP_BACKUP_KEY_PREVIOUS` alone (no current backup key) opens older backups behind the server key. Format 1 backups still restore. `--plain` and `--encrypted` choose; with no key a backup is format 1 and says so. *Scheduled backups, retention, copies (047).* `obop backup run` (cron) and a timer inside `obop serve` (`OBOP_BACKUP_SCHEDULE`; the decision is made from the catalogue in the database and a lease in the shared store, so it survives restarts and one process takes each backup) do: backup, read back and authenticate, record in the catalogue (`obop_backup`), copy to every sink and prove each copy by reading it back and comparing its hash (`BackupSink` port; `FsSink`: write-once, deleting a separate permission), apply retention (35 daily, 12 monthly by default, ISO weeks optional, the newest of each UTC period; **and none older than `OBOP_BACKUP_MAX_AGE_DAYS`, 400 by default**, because the counts alone keep "the N most recent periods that have a backup" and an outage or a sparse schedule stretches them past twelve months; scheduled backups only; never delete the newest, nor one without a verified copy in every sink, proved again by reading the sink at the moment of deleting, nor, in a bare `backup prune` that was given no sink while the catalogue records copies, anything at all unless `--no-sinks` says so; the timer and `backup run` follow their own configuration and do not wait for copies recorded earlier). A record leaves the catalogue only when its file is gone; paths are recorded absolute, with the host (`OBOP_HOST_ID`), and only the host that took a file removes it. A backup whose copy failed, or that predates a sink, is copied on the next run and once an hour by the timer; a sink that is the backup's own directory is refused (it would read back the file itself and retention would delete the only copy). A backup stamped later than now (a clock that was wrong) is not the newest backup and is flagged. Cron runs and the timer set and clear the same failure state, and the timer waits a minute, two, up to an hour after a failure instead of writing a backup a minute. `FsSink` publishes without replacing, so two writers of one name leave one whole object and a refused loser. `/metrics` gains `obop_backup_age_seconds`, `_size_bytes`, `_duration_seconds`, `_copy_age_seconds`, `obop_restore_drill_age_seconds`, `obop_backup_last_run_failed`, and on PostgreSQL `obop_wal_archive_lag_seconds` and `obop_wal_archive_failed_total`; the alert rules are in DEPLOY.md. *Drills (049).* `obop drill BACKUP` restores into a scratch database and checks backup verification, the restore, the counts per model, a digest over every restored record against the backup's, schema version (or the migration of the copy with `--migrate`), the ledger, the accounts, the sealed secrets, uploaded files and a read/write set; it records a `restore_drill` with the measured recovery point and time; `deploy/restore-drill.sh` runs it on a scratch PostgreSQL database and then starts a server on the copy, and records the drill (`DRILL_RECORD_IN`) only after the server answered or failed, so a drill whose last check failed is evidence of a failure (`obop drill --record-later FILE`, then `--record-from FILE --server ok|skipped|failed`); the drill exits 3 and 4 as `verify` does; a lent scratch database is emptied again, the uploaded files of the drill included, and the temporary scratch database lives in a private directory; CI runs a sealed and a plain drill (the plain one refused without `DRILL_ALLOW_PLAIN`), a wrong key (must exit 3) and a corrupt copy (must fail); docs/RUNBOOK-RECOVERY.md has the roles, decisions, the three paths and the drill schedule, with the numbers as measured-not-promised. *Host and storage (048, 056).* `obop storage check` asks the server (a `ServerInfo` port; the real one is `pgadmin::Pg`) for `wal_level`, `archive_mode`, the presence (never the text) of `archive_command` or `archive_library`, `archive_timeout`, `pg_stat_archiver` lag and failures, durability and checksum settings and TLS, and checks the evidence (backup age under 26 hours, sealed, with an off-host copy; a passed drill in 35 days; an attestation in 12 months); `obop storage attest` records who attested what mechanism, where the keys are and who can read them, with evidence (`--by` names the person, by default the operating system user); `storage check` prints it as "ATTESTED, NOT VERIFIED": a mechanism text that contains "256" is a typo guard, not a check that anything is encrypted. "Plane" in `--plane` is one store of an instance's data that an operator protects separately; there is one today, `main` (the instance database), and the option exists so that an attestation or a drill belongs to a named store when a second one does (none of D75 to D78 adds one). DEPLOY.md gives the configuration (pgBackRest sketch, restore points, the PITR procedure and the host gate) and says plainly that none of it was tried on a managed service.

**Threat model.** *Assets:* the records in a backup file, the key that opens them, the integrity of the backup you will rely on, and the history of what ran against the database. *A stolen or leaked backup file, a shared bucket, a copy on a laptop:* a sealed file shows its header (versions, time, key id, database id) and the model names, counts and **two unkeyed SHA-256 digests of the plaintext record stream** in its manifest (`content_sha256`, `records_sha256`; they let someone who can guess a whole instance's records byte for byte confirm it, and show whether two backups hold the same content; with ids that carry random bits and per-record salts no outsider can rebuild the stream; keeping them only inside the sealed stream would break `verify --structure-only` and the drill, and is deferred), nothing of the records (canary tests); a plain file shows everything and the tools say so where they write one (`backup`, the scheduler's banner, the pre-migration and pre-update backups, the module gate) and where they read one. *Tampering:* a flipped bit, a cut at any point (including exactly at a chunk boundary), an extension, reordered or repeated chunks, chunks from another backup, an edited header, an edited manifest field, or both files rewritten with fresh checksums, sizes and chain, all fail verification; only the holder of the key can make a manifest that authenticates (tests flip 100 bits, edit every field, forge a pair). **That holds for files that claim to be sealed; a forged plain pair needs no key, so where a key is configured a plain pair is refused (exit 4) by verify, restore, the gates, rollback and the drill, unless the operator says `--allow-plain`.** A backup or a receipt of another database is refused by the gates and the rollback (the database id); an id is a name, not a proof of origin: whoever holds the key can make any pair. *Wrong or missing key:* a stable code and exit status, a key named by id and never by content, nothing written. *Key custody:* the server holds the key it seals with, so a compromised server can read its own backups; the file's protection is against everyone but it. Losing the key loses the sealed backups; the tools and DEPLOY.md say what to keep apart. *Silent migration damage:* a changed step stops the server; a failed or interrupted one is a row; a migration cannot run without a backup that opens, a plan read, a rehearsal and room (strict). A receipt cannot be written by hand. *A rehearsal or drill that destroys production:* the scratch database may not be the production database (compared by host and name, user and parameters ignored), must be empty, and is emptied again. *Deleting backups:* the sink's credential does not delete by default; retention keeps what has no verified copy; the newest backup is untouchable. *Out of scope:* a compromised operator, who can disable every gate with `--gates relaxed` (the security log records the run, the gate mode and the backup, under the operating system user, but a user who can run the command can also edit the log); the host's own disk encryption (attested, not verified); timing of the cipher on this host.

`old_or_missing_evidence_fails_and_a_young_database_that_is_not_postgresql_only_warns`, `a_server_that_cannot_be_asked_is_a_failure_in_production_and_the_last_run_failure_is_shown`), `pgadmin::tests::a_real_server_answers_the_questions`, `storage_cli::tests::`.

**Why.** D59 gave obop a backup and a schema version and said so plainly: the checksums do not authenticate, records are in the clear, the version is one integer, the way back is a backup nobody was made to take, and a hosted instance has no scheduled backup. A business that keeps its only copy of its records in obop needs the next layer: a backup file that is not a breach when it leaks, a migration that cannot run without a way back and cannot be edited after the fact without being noticed, a copy that is proved by reading it, and a restore that has been done before the day it is needed. Each piece is useless without the next (a ledger without a backup gate records damage; a backup without a drill is a hope; a drill without a record is not seen), so they ship together.

**Rejected.** A separate SQL table for the ledger now (the storage has no typed tables yet; records are written in the step's transaction by the batch that exists; the typed layout, OBR-DAT-011, which no record has taken yet, and the immutability trigger of D77). Hashing the compiled code or the function body of a step (the toolchain would change every checksum; the source file text, normalised, is stable, reviewable and what a reviewer reads). Wrapping the data key to recipient public keys with a signed manifest (OBR-DAT-046 asked for it so that the server holds no key that opens its backups; this step is owner-directed to use a key ring, which needs no new cryptography crate, no key generation tooling and no key distribution, and `ring` cannot import a persistent X25519 key; the header has room for a second wrapping kind later). An Ed25519 signature (no private key to custody; the MAC under the key is the authentication, and `--allow-unsigned` has nothing to allow). A CRC or the file hash as the "authentication" (the existing damage-only checks). An in-process job runner for backups (step 5 builds the runner; the timer here is one thread that asks the catalogue). An S3 client written for this step (a signed multipart upload without a real store to test against is a guess; an operator-run uploader with object lock plus a read-back proof does the job and is documented). Deleting through the sink by default (the writer should not be able to delete). Retention applied to backups someone asked for by hand. Failing a migration step because it held a row lock for a second (only a statement that locks a table is held to the rule; a data step is one transaction and is only reported). Making `--auto-migrate` demand a plan and a rehearsal (a one-process hosted instance cannot rehearse before it starts; it takes the backup, which is the part that can be automatic). A disk-space guess for a remote PostgreSQL host from the database size (a number that looks like a check is worse than a flag that says it is the operator's). Trusting a key generated into the database to seal backups (it is inside them).

**Consequences.** Honest limits. **Not delivered** of the seven requirements: asymmetric key wrapping and an Ed25519-signed manifest with `--allow-unsigned` (046 items 1 and 2: the server can read its own backups); the blob manifest instead of inlined bytes above a size threshold (046 item 3, depends on the blob storage requirement OBR-DAT-013, not built); the system-field format, chain head and plane id in the header (018, 025, 050 do not exist yet); memory at 256 MiB for 50 GB (by construction a few megabytes, never measured); a resumable chunked restore above 10 GB (049 item 3); the S3-compatible sink and object lock enforcement (047 item 1: the doctor cannot see the bucket, the operator attests); the age of the last base backup as a metric (048 item 4: that is your WAL tool's number; the WAL archive lag and failures are metrics and an alert rule is given, but an idle database shows lag without a fault); the drill checks for system fields, history counts, freeze hashes, the chain, guard triggers, the privilege matrix, the blob manifest, the invariants of a separate store of certified data and the erasure replay (049 item 1: those objects do not exist yet; the checks that can run are done and say "not checked" where they cannot); the loss-of-primary drill and the point-in-time drills (a person with a stopwatch and a host); the ledger as a physical, trigger-guarded table with an `owner` role (051 item 1, D77), the single DDL executor (item 3, D77), `adopted` entries for a customer's earlier history (item 5, a downstream tool), the live-catalogue comparison of `migrate verify` (item 6); the check that binary N-1 serves schema N (053 item 4), a rehearsal on a 2,000,000-record copy that measures `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` locks (this step's steps take no table locks; the measure is the longest transaction); the half of OBR-DAT-056 that is a provider's (volume encryption, key custody, a provider snapshot unreadable without the key): attested, not verified. **Behaviour that changes for existing deployments:** a backup is sealed (and named `.obbk`) as soon as `OBOP_SECRET_KEY` is set, so scripts that glob `*.jsonl.gz` need `--plain` or the new extension; a server key that rotates leaves older sealed backups needing the previous key (a key of their own, `OBOP_BACKUP_KEY`, avoids that and is recommended); `obop migrate run` on PostgreSQL is refused without the gate flags; a hosted `module.upgrade` that rewrites records is refused without `OBOP_BACKUP_DIR`; a step edited after it ran stops the server; where a key is configured a plain backup is refused (`--allow-plain`), and an explicit `--out FILE.gz` for a sealed backup is an error (name it `.obbk`, give a directory, or pass `--plain`); retention also removes scheduled backups older than 400 days; `obop migrate plan` writes the plan to the database (`--dry-run` does not) and `migrate run --dry-run` is a preview; the exit statuses 3, 4 and 5 are stable. The `step-NNNN` history lines of D59 stay. A stored plan, a receipt and the catalogue are records in the database being migrated, so a restore (a rollback) brings back the plan and catalogue of the backup's moment. Retention is by UTC calendar periods; a backup taken at 23:59 and one at 00:01 are two days. The timer checks every minute and takes the backup in the request-free thread holding no other lock; a very large database makes that thread busy for a long time (the lease expires in three hours, longer than a backup should take). Two names exist for compatibility with the report of the requirements (`restore_key_required`, `pre-migration-<version>`). **Second review round.** Fixed: unfinished uploads in a sink are swept after six idle hours and reported (`list`, `storage check`, every run); the timer and `backup run` retain by their own sink configuration (a bare `backup prune` stays protective); a pair that both predate database ids is warned about as not compared, not passed as compared; ids are cut by characters and a manifest's `database_id` must be a hex id; a count that cannot be read counts as records in the module gate (fails closed); the Sliplane upgrade passes `OBOP_BACKUP_DIR` to instances made earlier; `migrate run` exits 3 or 4 when a gate refuses for a key or a plain backup; the text of a plain backup accepted under a key says why; the `restore_files_failed` hint fits a rollback too; the security log lines of `migration_run`, `storage_attested` and `drill_recorded` are tested; the usage text lists the flags. **Not delivered, from that round:** crypto-n3 (no test pins that the manifest is published before the data file and flushed: the order is by construction in `create_at` and a failed run leaves nothing, which is tested); crypto-n2 (complete backup pairs a killed run left unrecorded are not swept: `obop backup` by hand writes unrecorded pairs into the same directory by design, so a sweep could delete a wanted backup); a plain backup can be written under a `.obbk` name (`--plain --out x.obbk` writes gzip with a format 1 manifest), although the documents point at the extension as the sign of a sealed backup: the manifest, not the name, says which it is, and `backup verify` reports it; the default `OBOP_BACKUP_MAX_AGE_DAYS=400` overrides an explicit `OBOP_BACKUP_KEEP_MONTHLY` or `_WEEKLY` that needs more than 400 days, without a warning (the policy line prints "none older than 400 days"; an operator who needs longer sets the age to 0); `is_same_place` compares canonical paths, so the same host directory mounted at two paths in a container is not detected and the off-host copy would sit on the same volume as the backup (the operator attests the sink's place); the self-migrating start takes its backup while holding the migration lock, and another process waits for that lock for at most 60 seconds, so with two replicas started together and a backup longer than a minute the second exits with "timed out waiting for lock obop.migrate" (start the replicas one after the other, or migrate with `obop migrate run` first); a persistent failure of a step adds one `failed-<ms>` ledger row and rewrites its partial row on every start, so an orchestrator restarting every few seconds grows the ledger (the backup is no longer repeated; the rows are not pruned).

**Decided at the review, not changed:** the sealed manifest keeps its two plaintext digests (above); the attestation's mechanism check stays a typo guard because any self-attestation can lie (the record names its person and the check says "not verified"); a backup that predates database ids cannot be tied to a database, so strict mode refuses it and `rollback` restores it and says so; the `Local` provisioner (development) no longer passes `OBOP_BACKUP_SCHEDULE`, `OBOP_BACKUP_COPY_DIR` or `OBOP_HOST_ID` to its tenants, but still passes `OBOP_BACKUP_DIR` and the keys, as its documentation says; the database id is a name, not a signature (a holder of the key can make any pair, which is the key's meaning). **Licences and crates.** New in the lockfile: `fs4 1.1.0` (the free space of a volume; MIT OR Apache-2.0), with `rustix 1.1.5` and `linux-raw-sys 0.12.1` (Apache-2.0 WITH LLVM-exception OR Apache-2.0 OR MIT) and `errno 0.3.14` (MIT OR Apache-2.0); `hmac`, `sha2` and `postgres` were in the tree already (`hmac` through `hkdf`) and are now direct dependencies of `obop-keys` and `obop-ops`; no new cryptography crate. All are compatible with Apache-2.0. **Not tried against real systems:** a managed PostgreSQL or the Sliplane cell (restore points, `pg_stat_archiver`, `archive_command`, a role without the privileges the doctor assumes), pgBackRest or wal-g, an object store with object lock, a bucket lifecycle rule, any provider's volume encryption or key service, a real Prometheus, PostgreSQL 18 (the throwaway server is 17), a database of more than a few thousand records (the 14-month retention test simulates the clock, the cipher tests use kilobytes), a restore on a second machine of a different operating system (SQLite and PostgreSQL contents were moved between backends in tests). **Evidence.** D30, D44, D59, D63, D66, D67, docs/DEPLOY.md ("Backups", "Upgrades"), docs/RUNBOOK-RECOVERY.md, ENGINEERING 4.3, the tests above, `crates/kernel/obop-keys/src/stream.rs`, `crates/kernel/obop-migrate`, `crates/kernel/obop-ops`.

#### D74 Connection pool for PostgreSQL
**Status** PROPOSED (owner-directed in chat, 2026-10-25, step 1 "storage foundations"; requirement OBR-DAT-006, connection pool and non-blocking reads; not yet ratified as binding). Revised after seven adversarial review rounds (26 confirmed findings the first time, then six re-checks of the fixes, the last one closed by the "fail-closed II" branch; see "Review" below and "Left open" under Not delivered). **Reversibility** two-way door (the `Backend` contract does not change; every setting is an environment variable with a default; nothing is stored; `OBOP_DB_POOL_SIZE=1` with every time limit set to `0` comes close to the old single connection). **Owner action** operators: check `max_connections` against the sizing rule in docs/DEPLOY.md ("Connection pool and poolers"); behind PgBouncer in transaction mode set `OBOP_DB_POOLER=transaction` **and** `OBOP_LOCK_DATABASE_URL` (the server refuses to start with one and not the other); point a scraper at the new `obop_db_pool_*` series; clients of the API should honour `Retry-After` on 503.

**Decision.** *Three small pools instead of one connection behind a lock.* `obop-storage` gets a blocking pool written in the crate (`pool.rs`, generic over a manager that opens and pings connections, so it is tested without a database; `pgpool.rs` is the PostgreSQL side) and no new dependency. The PostgreSQL backend (`pg.rs`) keeps a **main pool** (`OBOP_DB_POOL_SIZE`, default 8, at most 64) for reads and writes, a **lock pool** (`OBOP_DB_LOCK_POOL_SIZE`, default and maximum 8) for session advisory locks that does not count against the main pool, and the blob store (`blob.rs`, `DbBytes`) a **blob pool** (`OBOP_DB_BLOB_POOL_SIZE`, default 4); every connection of every pool is opened through `PgConnector`, so TLS (D66) applies to all of them, and carries `application_name` `obop-main`, `obop-lock` or `obop-blob` (unless the URL names one), a connect timeout, TCP keep-alive and a TCP user timeout (each only where the URL does not set it itself). The `Mutex<Client>` is gone. *Acquire.* A call takes a connection for the length of one database call. If none is free it waits at most `OBOP_DB_ACQUIRE_TIMEOUT_MS` (default 2000), counting the time to open a new connection, which happens on a helper thread (a panic there gives the slot back) so a slow connect never keeps a caller past the timeout; and only if fewer than `OBOP_DB_MAX_WAITERS` (default 256) callers already wait, otherwise it is refused at once. The newest idle connection is taken first so the others age out; idle connections close after `OBOP_DB_IDLE_TIMEOUT_SECS` (300), every connection after `OBOP_DB_MAX_LIFETIME_SECS` (1800) (a sweeper thread, plus a check on checkout and on return); a connection idle for `OBOP_DB_HEALTH_CHECK_IDLE_MS` (1000) or more is pinged when handed out (a fresh one never is, a seeded one is; `0` pings every checkout; the ping gets no more time than the caller has left), and one that fails the ping takes the other idle connections with it, because they most likely share its fate. A connection whose call failed in a way that leaves its state unknown (socket broken, server shutting down, a failed rollback, an idle-in-transaction kill) or whose holder panicked is discarded, and the idle ones are closed with it. After a failed connect the pool tries again no sooner than 200 ms and one probe at a time; callers meanwhile get `storage_unavailable` at once, and a caller that is waiting while other connections are out wakes when the pause is over and tries again. A **read** (`get`, `list`, `find`, `count`, `total`, counter and value reads, blob chunks) that finds an old connection dead is repeated once on a fresh one; a **write** is never repeated (it may have been applied) and fails once. *Time limits by command class,* all configurable and `0` meaning "leave the server's own `statement_timeout`": point read or write 5 s, list or search 10 s, aggregate (a scan, `total`) 15 s, batch and import 60 s (a multi-statement transaction, a restore, an export, `ensure_unique`, `purge_expired`). A single statement sets the limit with a session-level `SET statement_timeout` that is issued only when it differs from what the connection already has (tracked per connection, `RESET` for "server default"; a new connection is given the point limit when it is opened), so a run of point reads costs no extra round trip; a transaction or a scan starts with `BEGIN; SET LOCAL statement_timeout = ...` (or `TO DEFAULT` for a class without a limit, whatever limit the previous call left on that connection) in one round trip and never touches the session value. A cancelled statement is recognised by its SQLSTATE (57014), not by the server's localised message. *Long reads.* `scan`, `export` and `import` hold one pooled connection and nothing else. `scan` and `export` run in one `REPEATABLE READ READ ONLY` transaction (one snapshot from the first row to the last, no writer blocked); a scan fetches 500 rows at a time through a portal, so memory is bounded and the time limit applies to each fetch and not to however long the consumer takes; a consumer that stalls longer than `OBOP_DB_IDLE_IN_TRANSACTION_MS` (120 s) between fetches loses its snapshot (`SET LOCAL idle_in_transaction_session_timeout`). At most `OBOP_DB_LONG_READS` (default half the pool) of them run at once (a permit taken before the connection), so a few heavy reads cannot take every connection from interactive calls. *Batches.* Several writes of one process used to be serialised by the one connection; now they run at the same moment. An atomic batch (`apply_checked`, which `create`, actions, `bulk_create` and checkout use) first takes, in one statement and in ascending order, a transaction-level advisory lock (two-integer key space, which `Backend::lock` does not use) for each of the **128 stripes** its records hash onto, so batches that name the same records in opposite order queue instead of deadlocking and two that create the same record cannot both think it is missing, and **never more than 128 locks however large the batch**: one lock per record let a few 1,000-row batches (a bulk import) fill the server's shared lock table, 6,400 slots with the defaults, after which the server refuses every statement that needs a lock with "out of shared memory"; the price is that batches whose records share a stripe wait for each other, and that a batch of hundreds of records lands on nearly every stripe, so the other atomic batches of the database wait while a bulk write (one short transaction of at most 1,000 rows) runs; the transaction that stores a blob takes the advisory lock of the blob's key first, so the same content uploaded twice at once is stored once and both uploads succeed (the second used to fail on the primary key; the writers of one process queue at a gate before they lease a connection, so waiters do not park the blob pool); **within a process the batches take turns before they lease a connection**: a bulk batch (more than 32 stripes) takes the turn exclusively, so it cannot park the pool, and the small writes and the reads of that process wait or run, and are not refused as busy; other processes on the same database still queue behind a bulk write in the database (a 1,000-row `bulk_create` takes 3 to 5 s on the measured machine, and every other atomic batch of the database waits for it, each holding one of its process's connections), so a bulk import is an operation for a quiet moment, and `obop_db_pool_waiting` and `obop_db_pool_in_use` show the queue; a transaction the server still rolls back for a deadlock (40P01) or a serialisation failure (40001) is run again (`apply_checked`, `insert_many`, `swap_fields`: up to three more times after a short random pause). Database error texts now carry the SQLSTATE and the server's message (no detail, which may hold values).

**What a fault does to a request (the rule, as documented and as tested).** The storage contract stays `Result<T, String>`, and callers turn errors into other things on the way up (`.ok()`, `unwrap_or_default()`, "not signed in"), so a typed error would be lost exactly where it matters. Instead: (1) the error text starts with a fixed code word (`storage_busy:`, `storage_unavailable:`, `storage_timeout:`), which `Fault::of_message` recognises in one place; (2) the pool *notes* the fault on the calling thread; (3) `obop-http`'s `blocking()` opens a **request scope** (`obop_storage::fault::begin_request`) on the thread that runs a handler's blocking part, and closes it after; (4) inside a request scope the **guarded backend** (`obop_storage::guarded`, wrapped around the PostgreSQL backend by `open`) refuses every change after the first refused call of the request, with the same fault, and notes every change of a record that commits (counters, short-lived values, the dedup markers of webhooks and the security log are not changes: they record or protect, and a repeat does no harm); writes that undo or follow what the request itself did are let through (`fault::bookkeeping`: releasing a key the request claimed, revoking a token it issued, un-claiming a webhook; `fault::allow`: the holds of a file to the record just created, the save of a vault), and the security log is never refused, so the entry of a change that committed is not lost; (5) the layer `ops::storage_faults` then answers. What it answers is made in one place, `obop_commands::errors` (`errors.rs`, the table that classifies every call error for REST, MCP and the CLI): the three code words are rows of its `TABLE` (503, wait 1 s for `storage_busy`, 5 s for the others), `errors::applied` is the rule that turns what the request did to the store into `none`, `unknown`, `partial` or "keeps its own answer", and `errors::storage_fault` is the answer itself (code, hint, `Retry-After`, `applied`); `ops.rs` only gives it the shape of the route. **Never a silent partial failure: a request is either all done, or it says what is not.** Three cases, and the 503 body (`"applied"`) and the `X-Obop-Applied` header say which. **(a) Nothing was changed** (`"applied":"none"`): a request that hit a fault before it committed any change is answered 503 with `Retry-After` (1 s `storage_busy`, 5 s the others) and has changed nothing, so repeating it is safe; **the exception is `storage_unavailable`, `"applied":"unknown"`:** a change in flight when the connection broke may or may not have been applied (`storage_busy` and `storage_timeout` leave nothing behind, the server rolled the statement back). **(b) Part of it was applied and a step it needed was refused** (`"applied":"partial"`): the step is tried again first (`fault::patiently`, four or five tries 150 ms apart), and only if the store still refuses it is the request marked partial (`fault::mark_partial`, which counts only after a change has committed) and answered 503 whatever the handler said, never 200 and never a 4xx; so is a request that committed and then ends in an error answer while a fault was noted. The needed steps are: the holds of an uploaded file to the record just created or changed; ending the sessions, tokens, connected apps and pending sign-ins of an account that is offboarded or disabled, a password reset, a group change (relimiting the live sessions) and a removed second factor or passkey; the save of the mask vault or of the MCP session that the answer's tokens belong to; the claim of a payment or connector webhook and the release of it when the event could not be applied (the sender gets a 503 and the event id is not used up); the release of a checkout's idempotency key (a claim that cannot be given back and has no order behind it is taken over by the next submit after two minutes, so the buyer is never sent to an order that does not exist; a release deletes only a key it has read to be its own, and a submit that finds the key taken and then gone claims it again); marking an order paid, giving the units and the code back, closing the payment and writing the provider's reference on the payment (money and stock: **a refused read of the order is never taken for "the order is gone", so a paid order is never refunded for it**; the webhook gives its event id back and answers "retry", and the provider's redelivery finishes the follow-up of an event whose payment was already recorded; the sweep also finishes cancelled orders whose units were not returned, and never cancels an order whose payment it could not look up); undoing an access token issued for a failed OAuth grant write; ending an OAuth grant; ending or relimiting the sessions after passkey removal, two-factor off, an administrator's two-factor reset and a group change, whose repeat finishes the work (a note on the account says what is owed; without it the repeat would meet "not on" or "no such passkey"); putting an approved plan back when its run was refused before it changed anything (decided from the state of the request, a fault noted and nothing written, whatever the run answered, and not from the words of the error, which the engine wraps; **not after a statement was on a connection that broke** (`fault::note_in_flight`: `storage_unavailable` where a change may have committed, and the repeat would run an approved action twice: the answer says "unknown" and the agent is asked to propose again), while a pool that could not open any connection (nothing was sent) puts it back like a full pool; if it cannot be put back within two seconds the answer is a partial 503 and the security log has a `plan_lost` entry naming the plan, and the plan id goes to stderr when the log is refused too). **The class sweep (a refused call read as a default, a plain "no", an "ok" or silence).** A grep of every `let _ =`, `.ok()`, `unwrap_or*`, `if let Ok`, `Err(_) =>` and `is_ok()` of the store layer, the kernel crates and the operator commands found 405 statements; the 390 that are left after this round are, by a rough count, 166 that are not a store result at all (a poisoned lock, a header, a JSON field, an iterator search) and 224 that swallow one. The rule that sorts them: **in a request scope a swallowed refusal is noted by the pool, the guard refuses every later write of the request, and the layer answers 503 when nothing was committed, so a read that came back as "none" cannot become a wrong write, and a page, a lookup or a sign-out that swallowed one is answered 503 and not 200 (class 1); it is a decision (class 2) or a claim of success (class 3) only where nothing of that holds:** on a thread with no request scope (the connector worker, the backup timer, the operator's command line, start-up), or when the code has already committed (an optional step after a commit), or when it acts outside the store. Class 1 and why: request-scope lookups, listings and display (obop-site, obop-http, obop-commands, obop-community, obop-auth lookups, the control plane: the layer); housekeeping that a later run repeats (token, state and idempotency purges, `Seen::sweep`, blob garbage, the sessions of the MCP, the community's caches and its status lease, the deletions of `bury` and `complete` whose lease expiry retries them); counters that only throttle an event line (`auth.evt:*`) or a metric; the fake provider of the demo. Class 2 and 3, fixed with a test each (the groups of the commits): the operator's host allow-list, the provider's agreement, the vault entries, erasure, revocation and the queue of retries (connectors); the lookup of the external id and the link of an imported record, the pseudonym keys and their forgetting, the burial of a dead job and the OAuth state of a pull or a callback (connector worker); the lockout counter; the identity check, the ledger and the schema version of a rollback, a scheduled backup and the backup before a migration or an update, the database id of a backup and the uploaded files of the migration backup (operator and scheduler); the counts behind an upgrade plan, the data steps that need a backup, the install gate and the check of stored decimals, and the old settings an audit line compares with (packages). Left open: see the list under "Left open after the sixth round". **Money:** a refund whose money went back and whose record was refused is a needed step and then a partial 503 whose error says "the money was returned" (money that moved outside the store counts as a change, `fault::external_change`); units go back only for an order that recorded a reservation (`stock_returned` is exactly `false`: a staff-made order or an import never reserved any, and adding its quantities would invent stock), the sweep finishes cancelled and refunded orders whose units are owed and returns the money of any cancelled order whose payment succeeded (a late payment whose follow-up was lost; five per sweep), a payment whose refund failed is left alone for an hour and only a call to the provider counts towards the five of a sweep (five payments that can never be refunded do not hide the sixth), and it runs in a request scope of its own when a checkout starts it (`fault::detached`: what it meets or changes is not the buyer's); a refund of delivered goods records that none are owed before the order moves, so there is no moment at which the sweep would shelve delivered goods; cancelling a pending order whose payment already succeeded cancels the order first and returns the money after it (a refund whose record is refused leaves a cancelled order with a succeeded payment, which the sweep finishes, never a pending order that the sweep or a redelivery could mark paid), and a failed mark-paid or cancel is judged by what the order is now (paid: done; cancelled: refund or release; still pending: an error, so the webhook answers retry); a move of the state through an `update` (the backoffice kanban, an approved plan) runs the action's own wrapper like a call of the action, so the money and the units go back either way; a refused claim of an idempotency key is not a claim, answers at once and releases nothing. **Accounts:** the notes that let a repeat finish the work are one per change (`sessions_owed.<change>`), so a pending offboarding survives a later reset or disable and enabling the account still finishes it; a note is not counted as a change (`fault::uncounted`) and is taken back only when its change certainly did not happen (a full pool, a statement the server rolled back, the guard, and no commit made), never after `storage_unavailable` (the factor may be off with only the acknowledgement lost, and the repeat must still end the sessions), and a note that an earlier request of the same change had left is put back, not removed (a repeated offboarding that is refused at its own write must not erase the first one's note); a session is written and the account is looked at again (every way of signing in): one made while an administrator was disabling the account is revoked at once, so it cannot wake up the day the account is enabled (either the disable has committed by then and this sees it, or it commits later and the list of sessions it ends already holds this one: no lock between the two); a password change reads the two-factor policy until no read of it was refused before it writes whether the session stays limited, and a two-factor or passkey enrolment that cannot free its session from "enrolment only" is a needed step; a lockout counter that cannot be read is a lockout; the repeat of an administrator's reset runs every step again (a person whose only factor was a passkey has no `mfa_enabled` to show how far the first request got, so their recovery codes, passwordless flag and proof are cleared too); the second step of a sign-in refuses an account that was disabled after the password step; a passkey enrolment that cannot lift the enrolment limit of its session is a needed step (a partial 503), not a quiet 200; a passkey is removed together with the user's record and the note in one atomic batch, a disable or an offboarding whose tokens could not be ended leaves the same note and **enabling the account finishes it first (or is refused)**, so nothing the administrator cut off comes back; the one change a repeat cannot finish is a person's own password change (it needs the old password): the log says to sign out everywhere under Security. **(c) Only something optional was lost** (the read-back of what a create or update wrote, a counter, a notification, a log entry after four tries): the request keeps its own answer, the fault is counted in `obop_storage_faults_total{answer="kept"}` and logged. A handler error that is really the store's refusal (`CallError::Handler("storage_busy: ...")`, a two-factor or control-plane error that carries a code word) is a 503 with `Retry-After`, never a 400; on `/mcp` a tool call that met a refusal is a JSON-RPC 503 with `Retry-After` and `error.data.applied`, not a 200 tool error. Only an error answer (a status of 400 or more) after a commit is `partial`: a redirect or a 2xx that worked keeps its answer. The code word is recognised only at the start of a message, so text a caller chose (an echoed field name) is never taken for a fault. Because the guard refuses writes after a refusal, the swallowed-read hazard (a vault read that failed and was taken for "no vault yet", a lookup that came back empty and ended a grant) cannot turn into a wrong write; the places that matter were also fixed at the source: the vault read now fails the call, a create or update whose read-back is refused answers with what was written, the OAuth refresh answers `temporarily_unavailable` instead of ending the grant, the security-log write is tried four times, offboarding and the other account changes fail as partial instead of reporting zero ended, a refused lookup of a user or of the administrators is the store's trouble and not "no such user" or "the last administrator", the retention run of the connectors stops instead of deleting by a guess, an outbound connector flow is released only with the usage read behind its rate and volume caps, the read of the provider's agreement, its pseudonym map (and the pseudonym key, which is the stored one or an error, never one derived from the current key), the masked tokens of its values (the entry by token, which `restore` reads, is written first and mended when only the entry by value is found) and its row in the flow log (a refused read or write stops the flow with a 503 that says nothing was applied; a busy store is not logged as a refusal of the flow), the operator's host allow-list is known or the client sends nothing (a list that could not be read at start or after a save is not an empty list, which means "no restriction": the source is asked again at every send), erasing a subject, forgetting a connection, dropping its queued deliveries and revoking return an error when the store refused (the revocation is a partial request and its repeat finishes it; the erasure the worker does is noted in the store and done again at the next tick), a retry the store would not queue makes the run `failed` and the send an error, a pull whose lookup of the external id is refused does not add the record again and the link it writes is retried, a run that cannot be recorded does not start, the worker tries an event delivery that met a busy store at the gate again (nothing was sent) and reports it when it still cannot go, a refused read of the owner's groups or of the rows is "later" and not "the owner may not read", a masked value whose token the vault cannot store stops the flow (`egress::tests::a_token_the_vault_could_not_store_is_never_sent_and_the_flow_leaves_no_row`), and once records have been delivered a refused step (the end of the run) is retried and makes the request a partial one, the owner of a connection that cannot be looked up holds no groups, and ending an OAuth grant answers 503 when it could not be done. The 503 has the shape of its route (REST error; OAuth `temporarily_unavailable` on the token, registration and revocation endpoints; a JSON-RPC error on `/mcp`; a small page where HTML was coming), keeps the CORS and security headers the route set, and also covers the control plane and the community pages. The answers of every surface are made from that fault and not from the words of an error: `errors::classify_with` takes what the request did to the store (`Outcome`) and, when a fault was noted for it, the failure is the store's whatever the text says (the engine wraps the text of a refusal, `... failed, nothing was changed: ... storage_busy: ...`, and a refused lookup of a row is reported as an invalid row), with `applied` from `errors::applied`. A text that merely begins with a code word is never read as a storage fault: a name or an id the caller chose and an error echoes first is an ordinary 400 (and a refusal that begins with another code of the table, with the same `code: ` that the call's own input begins with, is set apart by `errors::unforge`, which the dispatcher applies, so input cannot choose the row of its own answer; the message of a package assertion is the author's text and is trusted). The command line and the stdio MCP server are requests too: `errors::requested` opens the same scope that `blocking` opens for a handler (one `obop call`, one `obop import`, one line of the stdio server), so the guard refuses changes after a refused call there as well and **they say `partial`** when a change had committed: REST answers 503 with `Retry-After`, `X-Obop-Applied` and the body; the tool error of the stdio server carries `code`, `hint`, `applied` and `retry_after` in its structured content; the command line prints `error: storage_busy: ... (nothing was changed; try again in 1 s)` (or `part of it was applied: do not repeat it blindly`) and **exits 75** (`EX_TEMPFAIL`, "try again later"), where every other failed call exits 1 as before. A result that rests on a refused read is the same failure there (`errors::storage_outcome`), as on the HTTP surfaces. The server logs the explanation of a refusal of a call (`storage fault on a call: ...`), which no caller sees. Threads without a request scope (the connector worker, provisioning threads) get the error text only: a refused lookup leaves a queued delivery queued, the instance policy reads as *closed* when it cannot be read (nothing leaves), and the final write of a provisioning is waited out (`fault::patiently`, up to a minute). `/healthz`, `/readyz` and `/metrics` decide for themselves; `/readyz` reports ready when the ping is refused as busy (tested: `a_saturated_pool_is_busy_not_down`, and end to end in `crates/obop/tests/pool.rs`) or when its probe ran out of time while the main pool was full (this second fallback has no test of its own).

*Locks.* A lock is a session advisory lock on a lock-pool connection; the locks one thread holds at the same time share one connection (a thread-local registry of weak references, per backend), so a request that holds an agent's vault lock, then the write lock, then a session lock needs one connection, never waits for a second while holding the first, and eight threads cannot deadlock a pool of eight (or of two) by each holding one and wanting another. A thread that wants its first lock while eight other threads hold theirs waits the acquire timeout and gets `storage_busy`. A lock call that fails poisons its session, which is closed (the server then releases what it still held) when the last lock on it is dropped. `OBOP_LOCK_DATABASE_URL` gives the lock pool a direct address (own `sslmode` rules); at start-up the backend connects both ways and refuses to start unless both report the same `current_database()` and `pg_postmaster_start_time()` (the pooler may alias the name; locks in the wrong database would exclude nobody). *Poolers.* `OBOP_DB_POOLER=transaction` is for PgBouncer (or a pooler like it) in transaction mode: every single statement then runs in a transaction of its own with `SET LOCAL` (about twice the round trips), because the server connection behind a client connection changes between transactions; prepared statements are made and used inside that transaction, so it works with `max_prepared_statements` on or off. **The mode needs `OBOP_LOCK_DATABASE_URL`**: configuration without it, or with the pooler's own address in it, is refused at start-up (a lock taken through the pooler is taken on one server session and released on another, so it excludes nobody). **Which address a command uses:** the two variables describe how the server reaches its database, `OBOP_DATABASE_URL`; a command given another address with `--db URL`, `--scratch URL` or `--record-in URL` (a maintenance command: `migrate`, `backup`, `restore`, `secrets`, `drill`, `storage`, `update`, `admin`) is a direct connection and ignores both (`PgPoolConfig::address_is_direct`, set by the command line), so it is not refused as "the same address as the database URL"; an address equal to `OBOP_DATABASE_URL`, and `serve` and `platform serve` wherever the global options stand in the command line (`obop --db URL serve` is `serve`), keep the variables and the refusal (docs/DEPLOY.md "Which address a command uses"; tested through the binary). Session-mode PgBouncer and direct connections need nothing. *Other databases.* A process that opens a second database for a moment (the control plane seeding a new instance's database) uses `PgPoolConfig::one_shot()` (one connection, none of the process's `OBOP_DB_*` or lock settings, which describe its own database). *Metrics* (`/metrics`, read from the pools, no connection needed): `obop_db_pool_max_connections`, `_in_use`, `_idle`, `_waiting`, `_acquire_seconds` (histogram, 0.5 ms to 2.5 s, refusals included), `_acquire_timeouts_total`, `_acquire_rejected_total`, `_connect_failures_total`, `_connections_opened_total`, `_connections_closed_total`, all labelled `pool="main|lock|blob"`, and `obop_db_statement_timeouts_total` and `obop_storage_faults_total{fault,answer}` (`answer` is `503` for applied none or unknown, `partial` for a 503 that says part was applied, which somebody should read, and `kept`). *Backoffice.* No command changes, so there is no new screen. `serverMessage.ts` shows the three code words as sentences in the six languages and in English (three strings, machine-drafted like the rest, D7); the sign-in form no longer shows a 503 as "Invalid username or password"; reads wait for `Retry-After` before they are repeated, and a statement that ran into its time limit is not run again at once. SQLite and memory are untouched: SQLite keeps its one connection (a second read-only connection in WAL mode would let readers run beside a writer for a file database and is not trivial for `scan`, which would need a read transaction held across the visitor; left out).

**Why.** One `postgres::Client` behind a `Mutex` meant that a list, an aggregate or a restore blocked every other request in the process for as long as it ran, that a stuck database call blocked all of them with no way out, that nothing bounded how long a request waited, and that a restarted database left a dead connection behind a mutex; the lock connections were an unbounded set (8 kept), and every `obop backup` opened a connection of its own. The hosted layout puts many instances on one PostgreSQL, so connections are the scarce resource and have to be counted; a pooler in front is the usual answer and had to be made to work with session advisory locks and `SET`. **Requirement mapping and where it differs from the requirement text:** the requirement says "default 8 per process"; the main pool is 8 and the process can also hold up to 8 lock and 4 blob connections (documented in the sizing rule). It asks that a long read "shall not hold a connection used by other requests": a long read holds one connection exclusively for its duration (that capacity is not available to others), capped by the lane. It asks for the trait to stay blocking (no async rewrite), which is what was done.

**Rejected.** *`r2d2` + `r2d2_postgres`, `deadpool-sync`, `bb8`:* each adds three to five crates (and, for r2d2, helper threads per pool) for a pool whose interesting parts are ours anyway: a bounded wait queue, a long-read lane, fault notes, per-pool metrics, connections opened through `PgConnector`, and a manager that tracks session state; r2d2 queues callers up to its timeout with no bound on their number and exposes no waiter count. About 700 lines of pool (comments included), tested without a database, was a smaller risk than adapting one. *An async pool and an async `Backend`:* moves the requirement to size L and rewrites every caller. *A session-level `SET` behind a transaction-mode pooler:* it lands on a session another client then uses. *`SET LOCAL` in a transaction for every statement in direct mode:* four round trips for a point read where two do. *A process-wide semaphore around long reads:* it would have kept the old behaviour for the second one. *Unbounded waiting, or waiting 60 s like a lock:* that is the queue the requirement forbids. *A typed error enum in the `Backend` contract:* the signature `Result<T, String>` is used by about forty files and by every concurrent branch, and callers swallow errors on the way up, so a type would be lost exactly where it matters; *an error-string prefix alone* has the same weakness (a session lookup that failed on a full pool would be answered 401 and a client would drop its token). The thread note and the guard survive swallowing because a handler's blocking part runs on one thread from start to end. *Answering 503 whenever any call of the request was refused (the first version):* it told clients "nothing was started, retry" for requests whose change had committed, and a retry then made a duplicate; the rule above is the true one. *Answering 200 (or a 400, 401 or 404) when a step the request needed was refused after it committed:* the client would believe the account was offboarded, the file attached, the webhook applied or the password reset complete, and not retry what a retry would finish; "kept" is only for steps nobody needs. *One advisory lock per record of a batch (the first version):* a thousand-row batch holds a thousand slots of a lock table shared by the whole server; a few at once refuse every statement; the stripes bound it at 128 and cost only some false waiting. *Failing every request after a fault in the same request, including its compensations:* the first version of the guard refused the revoke of an access token that the request had issued for a grant write that was then refused, which left the token live; undoing what the request did is let through. *Making `/readyz` fail while the pool is full:* a load balancer would take the busiest instance out and push its traffic onto the others. *One connection per nested lock:* deadlocks a small lock pool. *A connection per request or per user:* the database would hold as many sessions as there are users. *Row locks in the order of the steps with a retry as the only remedy:* two batches that deadlock cost a second each (the server's `deadlock_timeout`) before they are found; sorted advisory locks avoid that, the retry stays as the net.

**Threat model.** *Assets:* the availability of database connections (PostgreSQL's `max_connections` is shared with other instances), the integrity of what a connection carries from one caller to the next, the integrity of what a request writes after a refused read, the time limits, and the secrecy of the database address. *Pool exhaustion as a denial-of-service lever:* anyone who can make the server hold connections (a signed-in person or agent running aggregates, exports or large `bulk_create` batches, a flood of requests on a public route such as sign-in or the shop) can use up the pool. Bounded by: the acquire timeout and the waiter bound (a flood costs at most 256 parked threads and answers 503 in 2 s, not memory or time that grows with the load); the long-read lane (heavy reads cannot take more than half the pool); the time limits (no call holds a connection longer than its class allows; a stalled scan consumer loses its snapshot after two minutes); the separate lock pool (a flood of ordinary requests cannot stop a lock from being taken and the other way round, with the limit that a ninth concurrent lock-holder in one process is refused); and the answer being cheap and honest (`Retry-After`). *Not bounded:* per-person fairness (one person with many parallel requests can still occupy the pool; the per-IP, per-token and per-account rate limits that exist are counters in the same database, and they fail open when the database is busy, which is why a request that hit the full pool is refused as a whole before it changes anything), and the HTTP layer's own concurrency (requests beyond the blocking-thread limit of the runtime queue in the runtime before they reach the pool). Run a reverse proxy with connection and request limits in front of a public instance. *A refused read that feeds a write* (the review's most serious finding): a pool refusal is now routine, so every `read failed, carry on with a default` followed by a write is a way to corrupt state under load; the vault read overwrote the agent's stored mask vault so that earlier mask tokens resolved to other people (measured on a live server: 21 to 23 of 40 agents wrote a different person's data). Closed by the guard (no change after a refusal, in a request scope), by the vault read failing the call, and by the tests below; code that runs outside a request scope has no such guard and must treat a transient error (`Fault::is_transient`) as "later" (the connectors worker and the provisioning threads were changed to). *Statement-timeout bypass:* the limits are set by the pool and by nothing else; there is no SQL surface (values are bound, identifiers come from the registry and `ident()`), so no request can run `SET statement_timeout`; a transaction cannot raise its own limit, because `SET LOCAL` is issued by the pool before the caller's statements; the lock pool's sessions run only lock statements. Gaps: a limit of `0` (operator's choice) is the server's value; time in the consumer of a scan is bounded by the idle-in-transaction limit and not by a statement limit; time spent waiting for a row lock inside a batch counts against the batch limit (60 s), which is also how long a batch may hold a connection; the global write lock and same-key waits (`LOCK_WAIT`, 60 s) are a separate queue that this decision does not bound. *Connection leakage across requests:* a connection goes back only after its call finished; an open transaction is rolled back or the connection is closed; a panic discards the connection (also a panic while opening one); statements are closed by the driver; the only per-connection state is the tracked time limit, and the lock sessions return to their pool only when no lock is held on them (tested: `nested_locks_of_one_thread_share_a_connection...` ends with every key free for a fresh handle). A process serves one database (D6), so no tenant shares a pool with another; the pool never changes role or database on a connection. Later work that needs per-request session state (a role per caller, row-level-security settings) must use `SET LOCAL` inside the call's transaction or a pool per role, never a session-level `SET`. *`SET` state leaking between pooled sessions:* only `statement_timeout` is ever set; direct mode tracks it per connection and `RESET`s it for "server default" (test `time_limits_follow_the_command_class_and_never_leak_between_callers`: mixed classes on one connection each see exactly their own limit, also in a transaction and a snapshot of a class without one); transaction mode sets nothing on a session at all. *Locks that exclude nobody:* a pooler in transaction mode with the locks taken through it; the start-up refuses the configuration, and refuses a lock address that reaches another database; what it cannot see is a lock address that is another transaction-mode pooler in front of the same database (documented). *Dead or stale connections:* checked on checkout when idle, dropped with their neighbours on failure, never put back after an error that leaves their state unknown; a write is not repeated. *Disclosure:* the 503 bodies are fixed words and a hint; the driver's text goes to the error string (now with SQLSTATE and the server's message, no detail); configuration errors never echo a value that looks like an address; `/metrics` carries counts and the three pool names, no record, user or path; the lock URL is validated with the same rules as the database URL and its text is never echoed. *Out of scope:* a compromised database or pooler; a network that drops packets silently (keep-alive and a user timeout are set where the platform supports them); admission control above the pool.

**Acceptance tests (named).** Pool without a database (`crates/kernel/obop-storage/src/pool_tests.rs`): `connections_are_opened_on_demand_up_to_the_maximum_and_reused`; `exhaustion_answers_busy_after_the_acquire_timeout_and_notes_the_fault`; `the_wait_queue_is_bounded_and_the_surplus_is_refused_at_once`; `a_connection_that_comes_back_wakes_a_waiter`; `many_threads_share_a_small_pool_and_never_exceed_it`; `a_broken_connection_and_a_panicking_holder_are_never_put_back`; `idle_connections_are_closed_after_the_idle_timeout`; `a_connection_past_its_lifetime_is_closed_instead_of_reused`; `the_health_check_replaces_a_dead_idle_connection_on_checkout`; `a_recently_used_connection_is_not_pinged`; `an_unreachable_database_is_unavailable_before_the_timeout_and_the_pool_recovers`; `a_slow_connect_does_not_hold_the_caller_past_its_timeout` (and the connection that arrives late serves the next caller, no other is made); `a_panic_while_connecting_gives_the_slot_back`; `a_waiter_tries_again_when_the_pause_after_a_failed_connect_is_over`; `a_seeded_connection_is_checked_like_any_other_that_sat_idle`; `a_hanging_ping_holds_the_caller_no_longer_than_its_time_allows`; `long_reads_are_capped_and_leave_room_for_interactive_callers`; `purge_idle_closes_every_idle_connection_and_frees_the_room`; `a_seeded_connection_serves_first`; `pools_of_the_same_name_add_up_in_the_report`; `a_dropped_pool_leaves_the_report_and_lets_go_of_its_manager`. The fault rule: `fault::tests::{messages_start_with_the_code_word_and_are_recognised_again, the_first_fault_wins_and_take_clears_it, a_note_belongs_to_its_thread, a_request_scope_blocks_writes_after_a_fault_and_remembers_commits}`; `testing::tests::{after_a_refused_read_the_guard_refuses_changes_in_a_request_and_not_outside_one, commits_are_noted_except_counters_values_dedup_markers_and_the_security_log}`; the layer over a store that refuses calls (`obop-http` `ops::tests`): `a_swallowed_refusal_before_any_change_is_answered_503_with_retry_after`, `a_request_that_committed_a_change_keeps_its_answer`, `after_a_refused_read_the_request_writes_nothing_and_says_503`, `the_503_speaks_the_shape_of_its_route_and_keeps_its_headers`, `the_health_routes_decide_for_themselves`, `a_saturated_pool_is_busy_not_down`; the vault (`shared::tests::a_refused_read_of_the_vault_fails_the_call_and_leaves_the_stored_vault_alone`); a create or update whose read-back is refused (`engine_tests::a_change_that_committed_is_answered_even_when_its_read_back_is_refused`, on the guarded memory backend); the OAuth refresh (`obop-oauth` `tests::a_refused_lookup_never_ends_a_grant_and_a_refused_follow_up_never_loses_the_new_tokens`); the security log (`audit::tests::an_event_is_not_lost_to_a_store_that_is_busy_for_a_moment`); connectors (`model::tests::a_policy_that_cannot_be_read_closes_the_gates_instead_of_opening_them`, `connectors` module `a_store_that_refuses_a_lookup_does_not_dead_letter_a_queued_delivery`); the control plane (`obop` `the_control_plane_answers_503_when_the_store_is_busy_not_401_or_404`, `obop-platform` `a_busy_store_does_not_leave_an_instance_deleting_for_ever`). **The partial rule and what follows from it:** `fault::tests` (a needed step refused after a commit is partial and one before any commit is not; compensations undo a counted change; `patiently` does not wait for the guard's own refusal); `testing::tests::bookkeeping_is_allowed_after_a_refusal_and_the_log_and_dedup_markers_are_not_changes`; blobs (`blobs::tests::a_blob_hold_refused_for_a_moment_is_tried_again_and_a_lasting_refusal_is_a_partial_503`); the shop (`obop-site` `a_checkout_refused_by_a_busy_store_releases_its_key_and_the_resubmit_makes_the_order`); webhooks (`obop-payments` `a_completion_met_by_a_busy_store_is_retried_not_swallowed_and_not_taken_for_a_duplicate`, `connectors` `an_inbound_webhook_met_by_a_busy_store_is_not_swallowed_and_its_retry_is_applied`, `obop-connectors` `webhook::tests::a_store_that_refuses_is_neither_a_new_event_nor_a_replay_and_a_claim_can_be_given_back`); accounts (`obop-auth` `admin_tests::{offboarding_with_a_token_list_that_is_refused_fails_as_partial_and_can_be_repeated, offboarding_goes_on_when_the_store_refuses_for_a_moment, a_password_reset_whose_sessions_cannot_be_listed_is_a_partial_failure, a_lookup_that_is_refused_is_the_stores_trouble_and_not_a_400_or_a_404}`); the vault and the MCP session (`shared::tests::{a_vault_save_refused_for_a_moment_is_tried_again, a_vault_save_that_stays_refused_after_a_commit_is_a_partial_failure}`, `mcp::tests::{a_session_save_refused_for_a_moment_is_tried_again, a_session_save_that_stays_refused_after_a_commit_is_a_partial_failure}`); handler errors (`obop-http` `fault_answers::*`); the connector worker (`connectors` `a_retention_run_that_cannot_read_what_it_decides_on_deletes_nothing_and_is_repeated_soon`); OAuth (`obop-oauth` `tests::{a_rotation_the_store_refuses_leaves_no_live_access_token_behind_and_says_when_to_retry, a_code_exchange_the_store_refuses_gives_the_code_back_and_leaves_nothing_behind, ending_a_grant_is_never_reported_when_the_store_refused_it}`); the documents (`openapi::tests::every_operation_declares_the_503_with_its_retry_after_and_applied_marker`); the batch locks (`pg_pool_tests::{a_batch_takes_at_most_the_stripe_count_of_advisory_locks_whatever_its_size, the_locks_of_a_thousand_row_batch_in_the_server_are_the_stripes_not_the_records, thousand_row_batches_from_many_threads_do_not_fill_the_servers_lock_table, large_batches_that_name_the_same_records_in_opposite_order_do_not_deadlock}`, the second of which asks the server how many advisory locks the transaction holds); two uploads of one file (`blob::tests::the_same_bytes_uploaded_twice_at_once_are_stored_once_and_both_uploads_succeed`, which fails with a duplicate key without the lock); the address rule (`crates/obop/tests/pool.rs` `a_command_given_its_own_direct_address_is_not_refused_in_a_transaction_pooler_layout`, which also runs `obop --db URL serve` and `obop --dev-auth serve --db URL`; `ops::tests::every_flag_that_names_a_database_is_an_address_of_the_commands_own`). **Third round:** money and stock (`obop-payments` `a_refused_read_of_the_order_after_a_payment_is_never_taken_for_a_gone_order_and_never_refunds`, `a_late_payment_for_a_cancelled_order_is_refunded_once_even_when_the_store_refuses_a_call_on_the_way`, `a_cancel_whose_units_cannot_be_given_back_is_a_partial_503_and_the_sweep_finishes_it`, `a_refund_is_not_made_for_an_order_whose_payment_cannot_be_looked_up`, `the_sweep_never_cancels_an_order_whose_payment_it_could_not_look_up`; `obop-site` `a_claim_with_no_order_behind_it_is_taken_over_when_old_and_respected_while_young`, `a_claim_the_store_refused_is_not_a_claim_and_releases_nothing_of_the_submit_that_owns_the_key`; `obop-payments` `an_order_that_cannot_be_marked_paid_for_another_reason_is_never_answered_as_done`, `a_cancelled_order_that_never_reserved_units_returns_none_and_the_sweep_leaves_it_alone`, `a_cancel_of_a_pending_order_whose_payment_already_succeeded_returns_the_money`, `a_refund_whose_money_went_back_but_whose_record_was_refused_is_a_partial_503_that_says_so`, `a_refund_whose_units_could_not_go_back_is_finished_by_the_sweep_and_a_delivered_one_owes_none`, and the fifth round's `moving_an_order_with_an_update_returns_the_money_and_the_units_like_the_action`, `an_approved_plan_that_moves_the_state_runs_the_same_wrappers`, `the_sweep_returns_the_money_of_a_cancelled_order_whose_payment_succeeded`, `a_cancel_whose_refund_record_is_refused_never_leaves_an_order_the_sweep_can_mark_paid` and `a_refund_of_delivered_goods_never_leaves_units_owed_whichever_step_the_store_refuses` (each of the last two refuses the store from every call position in turn, for longer than a step is tried again), `a_refused_call_of_the_sweep_does_not_refuse_the_buyers_own_checkout`; `obop-site` `tests::{a_key_given_back_between_a_failed_claim_and_the_read_of_it_is_claimed_again, a_release_whose_own_read_is_refused_deletes_nothing_it_cannot_prove_is_its_own}`; `fault::tests::a_detached_part_keeps_its_faults_and_changes_to_itself`); `/mcp` and approvals through the real application (`obop` `mcp_tests::a_tool_call_the_store_refused_is_a_503_that_says_what_was_applied_never_a_200_tool_error`, `a_batch_of_tool_calls_that_met_a_refusal_after_a_commit_is_a_partial_503_too`, `an_approval_whose_plan_cannot_be_put_back_says_the_proposal_is_lost_and_logs_it`, `invoicing` `an_approval_refused_before_it_changed_anything_can_be_approved_again` (every position of a bundled `invoice.post` run), `an_approval_the_store_refused_before_it_changed_anything_can_be_repeated`, `every_generated_operation_of_a_real_instance_document_declares_the_503`); the repeat that finishes the work (`obop-auth` `admin_tests::{a_group_change_that_could_not_relimit_the_sessions_is_finished_by_repeating_it, turning_two_factor_off_that_could_not_end_the_other_sessions_is_finished_by_repeating_it, an_administrators_two_factor_reset_that_could_not_end_the_sessions_is_finished_by_repeating_it}`, `passkey_tests::{removing_a_passkey_that_could_not_end_the_other_sessions_is_finished_by_repeating_it, removing_a_passkey_with_a_refusal_at_any_call_never_panics_and_the_repeat_leaves_nothing_behind, an_administrators_reset_never_answers_done_while_the_passkey_list_cannot_be_read}`, `admin_tests::{a_disable_or_an_offboarding_that_could_not_end_the_tokens_does_not_bring_them_back_on_enable, a_password_change_that_could_not_end_the_other_sessions_says_what_the_person_can_do, a_note_for_the_repeat_is_not_a_change_so_a_refused_change_is_never_answered_partial, offboarding_goes_on_when_the_store_refuses_for_a_moment}`, and the fifth round's `admin_tests::{a_note_that_is_kept_or_taken_back_leaves_the_notes_of_the_other_changes_alone, a_pending_offboarding_survives_a_later_reset_so_enabling_still_finishes_it, a_note_is_kept_when_the_outcome_of_the_change_is_unknown_so_the_repeat_still_ends_the_sessions, a_reset_that_was_refused_before_it_removed_anything_leaves_no_note_behind, a_second_step_of_the_sign_in_gives_no_session_to_an_account_disabled_in_between, a_password_change_whose_limit_could_not_be_lifted_says_to_sign_in_again_not_to_sign_out_everywhere}` and `passkey_tests::{a_reset_that_stopped_after_the_passkeys_went_is_finished_by_repeating_it_recovery_codes_too, a_passkey_second_step_gives_no_session_to_an_account_disabled_in_between, a_passkey_enrolment_whose_limit_could_not_be_lifted_is_never_answered_as_done}`;
