the sixth round's `admin_tests::{a_repeated_offboarding_refused_at_its_own_write_leaves_the_note_of_the_first_one, a_note_that_existed_is_put_back_and_not_removed_when_the_repeat_of_its_change_is_refused, a_two_factor_enrolment_whose_limit_could_not_be_lifted_is_never_answered_as_done, a_password_change_never_frees_a_session_that_owes_enrolment_because_a_policy_read_was_refused, a_session_made_while_its_account_is_being_disabled_is_revoked_and_not_left_to_wake_up, a_lockout_counter_the_store_would_not_give_is_a_lockout_and_not_no_attempts}`); the security log (`audit::tests::an_event_after_a_refused_call_of_the_same_request_is_also_tried_four_times`, `audit::tests::an_entry_that_stays_refused_is_reported_to_its_caller_which_may_be_its_only_trace`); an approved plan that is put back or not (`obop-commands` `tests::an_approved_plan_comes_back_when_nothing_was_applied_and_stays_gone_when_the_outcome_is_unknown`); an outbound flow that leaves only with its trace (`connectors` `tests::records_leave_only_with_a_trace_and_a_delivery_is_never_answered_as_nothing_applied`, which refuses the store from every call of a manual send in turn, once and for good, and `tests::an_event_met_by_one_refused_call_is_delivered_once_not_lost_and_not_twice`); a connection that died under a deadlocked transaction (`pg_pool_tests::a_connection_that_died_under_a_deadlocked_transaction_is_not_given_to_its_rerun`); the sixth round: connectors (`egress::tests::{a_mask_refused_between_its_two_writes_never_sends_a_token_that_cannot_be_restored, a_refused_read_of_the_agreement_is_busy_and_never_logged_as_no_agreement, an_erasure_the_store_refused_is_an_error_and_a_repeat_finishes_it}`, `net::tests::an_operator_list_that_cannot_be_read_lifts_nothing_and_is_asked_again`, `connectors` `tests::{a_host_list_the_store_could_not_give_at_start_lifts_nothing, a_revocation_the_store_refused_is_a_partial_request_and_a_repeat_finishes_it, an_erasure_the_worker_could_not_finish_is_noted_and_done_again_at_the_next_tick, a_retry_the_store_would_not_queue_is_a_failed_run_and_an_error_never_retrying, a_delivery_whose_run_cannot_be_closed_is_a_partial_request_and_never_nothing_applied}`), payments (`tests::{payments_that_can_never_be_refunded_do_not_starve_the_ones_that_can, a_stranded_payment_that_fails_before_the_provider_costs_nothing_against_the_few_per_sweep}`), and the class sweep (`secretbox::tests::a_pseudonym_key_the_store_would_not_give_is_an_error_and_never_a_different_key`, `queue::tests::a_job_leaves_the_queue_only_once_it_is_in_the_dead_letters`, `oauth::tests::a_state_that_could_not_be_consumed_is_not_used_and_one_the_store_would_not_give_is_not_invalid`, `connectors` `tests::{a_pull_never_adds_a_record_again_because_the_store_would_not_say_it_was_imported, a_pull_keeps_the_link_of_every_record_it_made_whichever_call_the_store_refuses}`, `gates::tests::a_rollback_does_not_skip_the_identity_check_when_the_id_of_this_database_cannot_be_read`, `backup::review_tests::a_backup_is_not_made_without_the_id_of_the_database_because_the_store_would_not_say`, `update_tests::{a_count_the_store_would_not_give_is_never_read_as_no_records, the_settings_a_change_is_compared_with_are_never_nothing_because_the_store_would_not_give_them}`); OAuth (`obop-oauth` `tests::{a_code_exchange_refused_at_any_call_is_never_a_final_invalid_grant_and_its_retry_works, a_grant_ended_while_its_access_token_could_not_be_revoked_finishes_when_the_client_comes_back}`); the code word only at the start (`fault::tests::messages_start_with_the_code_word_and_are_recognised_again`); the answer after a commit (`obop-http` `ops::tests::a_redirect_after_a_commit_is_an_answer_that_worked_and_a_partial_503_is_counted_apart`); the blob store (`pg_pool_tests::{processes_that_open_the_blob_store_together_on_a_fresh_database_all_start, a_bulk_write_makes_the_small_writes_and_reads_of_its_process_wait_and_never_busy}`, `blob::tests::the_same_bytes_uploaded_twice_at_once_are_stored_once_and_both_uploads_succeed`, now with a pool of two and 300 ms to acquire). Settings (`crates/kernel/obop-storage/src/pg_pool_tests.rs`, no server needed): `the_defaults_are_the_documented_ones` (every default as a literal); `every_setting_is_read_from_its_variable`; `a_bad_value_stops_start_up_and_names_the_variable_without_echoing_a_url` (also: a pooler in transaction mode without a lock address, or with the pooler's own address, is refused); `a_database_opened_for_a_moment_does_not_take_the_process_settings`. Against PostgreSQL (the same file, `OBOP_TEST_POSTGRES_URL`; each in a database of its own): `a_five_second_read_does_not_block_a_concurrent_get` (a `pg_sleep(5)` read on a pooled connection beside `get`: the median get is under 50 ms or five times the median of an idle get, whichever is larger; a machine busy with other work makes every get slow, with or without the read); `a_streaming_scan_holds_one_connection_and_nothing_else` (pool `in_use` is 1 and the lock pool's 0 during the scan); `a_scan_sees_one_snapshot_from_its_first_row_to_its_last` (an insert, a delete and an update from other connections between two fetches of 1,300 rows are not seen); `two_hundred_requests_on_a_pool_of_eight_refuse_the_surplus_within_the_acquire_timeout`; `a_full_queue_refuses_without_waiting_and_parks_no_more_than_its_bound` (the memory figures are in the HTTP test below); `the_lock_pool_does_not_take_interactive_capacity_and_the_other_way_round`; `nested_locks_of_one_thread_share_a_connection_so_a_small_lock_pool_cannot_deadlock`; `time_limits_follow_the_command_class_and_never_leak_between_callers` (single statements, transactions and snapshots, and the class without a limit); `a_cancelled_statement_is_recognised_by_its_code_not_by_the_words_of_the_server`; `a_statement_over_its_limit_is_cancelled_and_the_connection_stays_in_the_pool`; `a_slow_consumer_is_not_cut_off_by_the_statement_limit_but_a_stalled_one_is_ended`; `a_connection_killed_by_the_server_is_noticed_and_replaced` (health check; a read repeated once; a write fails once and the pool has healed); `batches_that_name_the_same_records_in_opposite_order_do_not_deadlock` and `a_transaction_the_server_rolled_back_for_a_deadlock_is_run_again` (with `shared_tests::concurrent_batches`, which also runs on memory and SQLite in `shared_contract`: opposite-order batches, a counter created by whoever comes first, six callers inserting one new record); `a_lock_address_that_reaches_another_database_is_refused_at_start_up`. With Docker (containers of the test's own, `obop-pool-pg-<pid>-<n>`, removed afterwards; skipped with a message when Docker or an image is missing): `after_a_server_restart_the_pool_serves_again_without_restarting_the_process` (`docker restart` of a throwaway `postgres:17` under a running pool: calls during the outage answer within 5 s with `storage_unavailable`, the pool serves again within 30 s of the database accepting connections, then writes, counters, a batch, a lock and a scan work); `behind_pgbouncer_in_transaction_mode_everything_works_with_locks_on_a_direct_address` and `pgbouncer_without_protocol_level_prepared_statements_works_too` (PgBouncer, pinned by image digest, which the test asks `SHOW VERSION` (1.26.1); `pool_mode = transaction`, two server connections, `tests::contract` plus the counter, value, lock, batch, import and export checks across two handles; the full `shared_contract` is not run through the pooler because it lets values live 50 ms, which three round trips through a container network cannot honour). A real server (`crates/obop/tests/pool.rs`): `an_exhausted_pool_answers_503_with_retry_after_never_a_401_and_the_server_recovers` (a pool of two, a table lock held by another session, 200 signed-in requests, then 800 more: all 503, `storage_busy` with `Retry-After: 1` within the timeout, `storage_timeout` with `Retry-After: 5` for the ones that held the connections, never 401 or 400; the server's memory read throughout; `/healthz` up; `/readyz` ready while both connections are held; the series on `/metrics`; served again after the lock is released, without a restart); `a_create_is_never_answered_503_after_it_committed_and_never_stored_after_a_503` (a pool of one and no queue, 360 creates beside a background reader: every 200 is stored, every 503 `none` is not; that a refusal after a commit keeps its answer is not forced over HTTP, where it depends on who gets the one connection, but in `ops::tests::a_request_that_committed_a_change_keeps_its_answer` and `a_redirect_after_a_commit_is_an_answer_that_worked_and_a_partial_503_is_counted_apart`); and `a_bad_pool_setting_stops_the_server_at_start_up_and_names_the_variable`. Backoffice: `serverMessage.test.ts` (the three code words as sentences in every language and in English, and `storageFaultMessage` for the sign-in form), `net.test.ts` (`Retry-After` is waited for; a timed-out statement is not read again at once). **Unchanged and green:** `tests::postgres_meets_contract_when_available`, `shared_tests::postgres_shared_contract_between_two_connections` (apart from the timing note below), `shared_tests::a_panicking_holder_releases_its_lock`, `blob::tests::postgres_bytes_meet_the_contract_when_available`, the TLS tests (one opens its backend with a longer acquire timeout, because containers started in parallel on a busy machine need more than two seconds for a first TLS handshake) and `crates/obop/tests/multi_process.rs`. The visibility of `tests::contract` and `shared_tests::shared_contract` changed (`pub(crate)`), so the pooler tests can call them. *Added with the error table (`obop-commands/src/errors.rs`):* `obop-commands` `errors::tests::` `the_storage_rows_are_the_faults_of_the_pool`, `a_noted_fault_makes_the_failure_the_stores_whatever_the_text_says`, `a_text_alone_never_makes_a_storage_fault_and_input_cannot_choose_a_row`, `applied_follows_what_the_request_did_to_the_store`, `a_storage_fault_says_what_became_of_the_changes_in_every_shape`, `engine::tests::no_handler_builds_its_own_unit_or_engine`; `obop-http` `error_tests::a_storage_word_is_a_503_with_a_wait_and_what_became_of_the_changes`, `a_bare_error_text_with_a_storage_word_answers_as_a_call_does`, `ops::tests::the_three_outcomes_are_said_alike_on_rest_and_json_rpc` (none, unknown and partial, as the REST body and headers and as the `data` of the JSON-RPC error); `obop-mcp` `tests::a_storage_fault_is_a_tool_error_with_the_wait_and_what_was_applied`; `obop` `error_seam_tests::` `a_refused_rest_call_is_a_503_that_says_nothing_was_changed`, `a_refusal_after_a_commit_is_a_partial_503_and_the_word_is_true` (REST), `a_refusal_after_a_commit_says_partial_on_the_command_line_and_the_stdio_server`, `a_wrapped_refusal_is_the_stores_on_the_command_line_and_the_stdio_server`, `input_that_begins_with_a_code_word_cannot_forge_a_fault_or_a_row` (REST, the command line and the stdio server), `a_refused_tool_call_of_the_stdio_server_is_a_tool_error_with_the_wait_and_applied`, `a_refused_command_line_call_prints_the_word_and_exits_with_75`, `the_exit_status_and_line_of_every_failed_call_come_from_the_table` (a real app over a store that refuses on demand); and with the real binary over a SQLite file whose store refuses the batches named in `OBOP_TEST_FAULTS` (`obop-storage` with `test-support` only, never a release build) `crates/obop/tests/error_seam.rs`: `a_refused_call_of_the_binary_exits_75_and_says_what_was_changed`, `a_refused_import_of_the_binary_exits_75_and_creates_nothing`, `a_refused_tool_call_of_the_binary_says_what_was_applied`.

**Consequences.** *Measured on one Windows 11 machine with Docker Desktop, PostgreSQL 17, a debug build of obop with optimised dependencies, other work running on the machine (numbers vary by a factor of ten with that load):* a `pg_sleep(5)` read beside `get`: 49 to 58 gets in 3 s, median 2 to 6 ms (idle median 1 to 12 ms), worst 3 to 160 ms. Pool 8, 200 concurrent 3 s requests: 8 served, 192 `storage_busy` after 2.01 to 2.19 s. Queue bound 5, 300 callers on 2 busy connections: peak waiting 5, 295 refused at once. Over HTTP (pool 2, acquire timeout 0.5 s, every statement blocked by a table lock): 193 to 198 `storage_busy` (the slowest after 0.5 to 0.9 s), 2 to 7 `storage_timeout`. The server process (`obop serve`) was 21 MiB resident idle, 25 MiB at the peak of a 200-request stampede on a pool of two and 27 to 29 MiB at the peak of one with 800 requests. A restarted PostgreSQL container: the first refused call 0.9 to 14 s after the restart began, refused calls answered within 2.0 s (the acquire timeout) at most, and the pool served within 0.2 s of the database accepting connections again (the container itself needed 6 to 44 s). Sixteen threads taking three nested locks five times on a lock pool of two: 0.6 to 1.0 s. Behind PgBouncer eight client connections shared two server connections. *Timing in the existing tests:* `shared_tests::shared_contract` lets counters and values live 50 to 120 ms and expects two consecutive calls to fall inside that; each call is a round trip to the container, so on a machine where a write takes 100 ms or more (measured here during heavy builds: a `counter_add` took 0.2 to 1.0 s on the shared test database while a `SELECT` took 1 ms, with or without the pool) it fails; the pooled backend adds no round trip to a call. *Costs.* One more `SET` when the class changes between single statements; two more round trips per call in transaction-pooler mode; up to 20 connections per process against `max_connections`; the idle-connection ping adds one round trip to a call that follows a pause of a second or more (`OBOP_DB_HEALTH_CHECK_IDLE_MS`); an atomic batch takes one advisory-lock statement before its steps (at most 128 locks, and a bulk write makes the other atomic batches of the database wait while it runs); the scan limit is per fetch, so a scan over a very large model is bounded by the idle-in-transaction limit and not by the 15 s class (an aggregate that needs longer than 15 s per 500 rows is cancelled). *Behaviour that differs from before, deliberately:* a storage fault (full pool, unreachable database, statement over its limit) answers 503 with `Retry-After` instead of a 400 with the driver's text or a 401 from a swallowed error, **saying whether anything had been changed (`applied`); a request that committed and lost only something optional keeps its answer, and one that lost a step it needed is a 503 `partial`** (see the rule above; a request can still succeed while its audit entry is lost after four tries, which is counted and logged); after a refused call a request makes no further change; a call that used to wait behind a long read now runs beside it; writes of one process run at the same moment instead of one after the other (see "Batches"); a scan or an export is one repeatable-read transaction (before: one statement, which also saw one snapshot, but on the connection everyone else waited for); a ninth simultaneous lock-holding thread is refused after two seconds (before: a new connection); error texts of the database carry SQLSTATE and message (which also makes `explain_db_error`, which matches `obop_uq_` in the text, work against PostgreSQL). Command semantics are otherwise those of the contract tests, which pass unchanged on memory, SQLite and PostgreSQL.

*Not delivered.* **Left open after the third review, with reasons:** a person's own password change cannot be finished by a repeat (the repeat needs the old password) and keeps no note, so the log says to sign out everywhere; the refusal of a plain claim of the shop's key is answered at once and the buyer submits again, there is no queue for it; a plan that cannot be put back within two seconds is lost (logged, and the answer says so; the plan id goes to stderr when the log is refused too); the cross-process queue behind a bulk write (above). **Left open after the fifth round (the fourth re-check), each with its sequence and why it is rare:** (1) a plan put back after a refused run whose own put-back write failed with `storage_unavailable` may in fact have been stored, so the log says "lost" while a live copy waits for its time limit (the approver is told to ask again, and the old copy stays approvable until its time limit, so approving both the old and the new plan would run the action twice; it needs the put-back to fail with an unknown outcome right after a refused run, and both approvals by people who were told the first is lost); (2) a flow row for a flow that never left (the row is written before the run starts, so a run that cannot be recorded stops the flow but leaves a `released` row that counts toward the rate and volume caps: conservative); (3) a stale `user_disable` note after a disable whose outcome was unknown and which did not commit (enabling an account that was never disabled then signs out its sessions older than the note, once); (4) a stranded payment whose refund record stays refused for more than a day (the provider's idempotency key lives one day, so the sweep's repeat may be refused as "already refunded" and the payment stays `succeeded`, and no action records a refund that was made in the provider's dashboard, so the record is corrected in the database; a payment that failed is left alone for an hour and five provider calls at most are made per sweep, inside the checkout that started the sweep); (5) the recovery codes of a first passkey enrolment whose session limit could not be lifted are in an answer that became a 503 (they exist and work; the person makes new ones under Security); (6) a `refund` or `refund_delivered` whose money went back and whose payment record was refused leaves the order paid and the payment succeeded until staff repeat it (the repeat is safe), while a cancel is order-first: the two are not the same order of steps because a paid order cannot be marked paid again, which a pending one can. **Left open after the sixth round (the second re-check and the class sweep):** (1) the sweep's refund of a stranded payment is per process (the hour of waiting is kept in memory): two processes may each try a payment that cannot be refunded once an hour, and a restart tries it again at once; (2) `installed_manifests` and the list of installed packages at start-up read a refused list as "no packages installed": a command line or a start that meets that registers nothing until the next start (fail closed for the commands, but nothing says so; a request scope answers 503); (3) `limit::hit`, the rate limiter, lets a hit through when its counter cannot be written (a limiter must not take the service down with the database): in a request scope the layer then answers 503 anyway, so this matters only to code that calls it off a request, and none does; (4) the in-memory `Auth` reads that answer a plain value (`is_disabled`, `must_change_password`, `mfa_enabled`, `security_setting`, `passkeys_of`) read a refusal as "no" and are protected by the guard in a request scope and by `settled` where they feed a write; a command that called them off a request would not be, and none does; (5) the community's forum and status caches keep their old data when a refresh is refused (a page can be stale for the length of the refusal); (6) `external_change` after a delivery cannot be isolated by a test, because the flow-log row is already a change of the request: it is defence in depth, and the test asserts the `partial` that the end-of-run step gives. **Closed after the seventh round ("fail-closed II"), changing the lists above:** the sign-in that began before a password change, a reset, new groups or a removed factor or passkey no longer leaves a live session (item "a person's own password change" above stays: a repeat still needs the old password, but the other sessions, including one a sign-in in flight was about to write, are ended); the sweep's refund of a stranded payment no longer starts its hour for a refused store call (sixth round (1) keeps the hour for a refund the provider refused); `modules uninstall` no longer reads a refused list as "nothing depends on it" (sixth round (2) keeps `installed_manifests` for the plan preview and the start); the lockout no longer counts a refused account read as a wrong guess, answers a guess it could not count as the store's trouble and writes its log entry only for a count that was read (sixth round (4) keeps the plain-value reads of `Auth` off a request scope). **Left open after the seventh round, each with its sequence and why it is rare:** (1) a record that an inbound flow created (a pull, a webhook) and whose link to the sender's id or whose origin entry is still refused after the needed retries (five tries, 150 ms apart) is a partial request and its item says "the next run may add it again": the sender's retry of the batch or the next pull adds it once more; it needs a store that refuses for over a second right after a create. (2) A delivery that failed and waits for its retry, or lies in the dead letters, keeps the treated rows made before an erasure until it is sent or ages out (dead letters now leave with the retention of their connection): masked values are tokens whose vault entries the erasure drops and pseudonyms cannot be reversed, but a field the owner allowed in clear stays in the sealed body; finding a person's jobs would mean opening every body, so the erasure does not touch them. (3) The word of the 503 follows the first fault and what the request wrote, not `in_flight`: after `storage_unavailable` with nothing sent (no connection could be had) the answer says "unknown" while the approved plan was put back, and a plan lost behind a break that came after a first `storage_busy` is answered "none"; both need a refusal at connect level, or a break behind a busy moment, during an approval, and then both copies of the plan approved by people told the first is lost. `blocking()` still merges fault, wrote and partial of a part and not `in_flight` (latent: approve is one part). (4) Input that reaches the text of a refusal through a decoding step of its producer (URL-decoding, base64) is not seen by `unforge`; the producer that echoed input first was changed and the effect of the rest is a status, a code and a hint on the caller's own answer. (5) A batch on `/mcp` over HTTP is still answered as one request (the layer answers a whole payload once); the stdio server answers a batch line call by call. (6) The flow-log row of an inbound flow is written once, without a retry, after the records exist; a lost row leaves the sync run as the trace, and nothing decides on an inbound row. (7) A wrong guess whose count is refused three times in a row (about 0.2 s) is answered with the store's error and not counted; the answer is a 503, so the guesser learns nothing from it. Per-person fairness or admission control above the pool; a FIFO queue (waiters wake in the operating system's order); a SQLite reader connection; an asynchronous `Backend`; pools for the control plane's single-shot connections (the cell admin connection and the status probe still open one connection each through `PgConnector`); a lease limit on a held connection (a consumer that never returns holds its connection until its process ends, visible as `obop_db_pool_in_use`; a scan's snapshot is ended by the server after two minutes of idleness). **Deferred after the review, with reasons:** (a) a lock pool larger than 8 (the requirement says at most 8; an MCP request holds one lock connection for its whole duration, so a ninth concurrent MCP session request in one process gets `storage_busy`; raising the cap or not pinning a session for the whole request is the owner's call); (b) a 503 for the operations of the OAuth endpoints and the public pages in OpenAPI (they are not in the documents; the OAuth endpoints answer an OAuth error with `Retry-After`, the pages a small page); every operation that the two documents (control plane and instance) list declares it, with `Retry-After`, `X-Obop-Applied` and the `applied` field; (c) a read-through path for post-commit work other than the create and update read-back (observers of the dispatcher, workflow actions' follow-ups) beyond the guard's rule that a committed request keeps its answer; (d) the `bulk_create`-style idempotency key for single creates and updates, which would make the one remaining case (`storage_unavailable` after a change in flight) safe to repeat. **Not tried** against a managed PostgreSQL service or pooler, PgBouncer in session mode, any PgBouncer other than 1.26.1, PostgreSQL 18, Linux (the tests ran on Windows with Docker Desktop, where `TCP_USER_TIMEOUT` has no effect), a server with a non-English `lc_messages` (the classification by SQLSTATE is tested with a raised error that carries a German message), a network partition that drops packets silently, a failover to a replica, a sustained load of minutes or a pool of 64, or pool and TLS together beyond the one D66 test; and the memory figure is one process observed on one machine, not a proof of absence of growth.

**Review.** The branch went through an adversarial review (43 findings; 26 confirmed by two verifiers, 11 disputed, the rest refuted). What changed: the 503 rule and the guard (hygiene-1, faults-1, poolcore-7, pgbackend-4); the vault read (faults-3, pgbackend-3); transaction mode needs a lock address (poolcore-1, pgbackend-6, hygiene-3); batches order their locks and deadlocks are rerun (pgbackend-1, pgbackend-2); OAuth refresh (faults-2); control plane and community routes (faults-4, hygiene-2); background threads and egress (faults-5); the 503's shape and headers (faults-7); a limit of 0 in transactions and snapshots (poolcore-2, pgbackend-5); SQLSTATE for cancelled statements (poolcore-6); a panic while connecting (poolcore-4); retry after the backoff (poolcore-5); the seeded connection (poolcore-8); the bounded ping (poolcore-9); the identity check by simple query (poolcore-3); `/readyz` when the pool is full (regression-2); the sign-in form and the retry timing (faults-6, faults-8); `OBOP_DB_*` and the lock address no longer reach other databases opened by the control plane (regression-4, hygiene-7); URL options win over the pool's defaults (pgbackend-8); the PgBouncer image is pinned and asked for its version, the site wording says what was tried (hygiene-6); the test hang (regression-5) and the tests that claimed more than they asserted (poolcore-10, pgbackend-9, hygiene-5). **Second round.** Three reviewers found that the 503 rule of the first revision still let a request finish half done without saying so: a webhook answered 200 after its event was not applied, blob holds lost after a create, the revoke of a token the request had issued refused by the guard that was meant to protect it, offboarding that reported "0 sessions ended" when the token list was refused, a refused account lookup answered "no such user", a retention run that deleted by an empty list of connections, OAuth 503s without `Retry-After`, a thousand-row batch that took a thousand locks, two uploads of one file that failed on the primary key, and a maintenance command refused in a transaction-pooler layout. The rule is now the three cases above (none or unknown; partial; kept only for what nobody needs), each of the fixes has a test that fails without it (listed under the acceptance tests above), the testing backend (`Flaky`) is behind the `test-support` feature of `obop-storage` and not in the production crate, and the readiness test waits for the pool to be full instead of sleeping a guessed 400 ms. **Fifth round.** A fourth re-check of the money, revocation and approval fixes confirmed twenty findings, all but the ones above fixed with a test that fails without the fix: an `update` of a governed state ran the bare action and skipped the refund, close-payment and stock wrappers (money-1); the sweep never looked at a cancelled order whose payment succeeded (money-2); a refund of delivered goods left a window in which the sweep would shelve the goods (money-3); a refused refund record left a pending order that could be marked paid (money-4); a release that guessed after a refused read, and a claim that took a vanished key for its own (money-5, money-6, fault-6); the sweep's refusals in the buyer's request (money-7); a passkey-only person's repeated reset left the recovery codes, one note slot overwritten by a later change, and a note taken back after an unknown outcome (revocation-1, -2, -3); a second step of a sign-in for an account disabled in between (revocation-4); a log line that named the wrong remedy and a swallowed enrolment-limit refusal (revocation-5, -7); a plan lost where the layer says "none", and put back where the outcome is unknown (fault-2, -3, revocation-6); a reset's note left after a refusal that changed nothing (fault-7); a connection whose rollback failed handed to the rerun after a deadlock (fault-5); and connector records that left with no trace and an answer of "none" after a delivery (fault-1). **Sixth round.** A second re-check of the fifth round confirmed fifteen findings, fixed with a test that fails without the fix: a repeated offboarding refused at its own write erased the note of the first (the earlier note is put back); a half-written mask vault entry was reused on the retry and its token sent with nothing to restore it (the entry restore reads is written first and mended); the provider's agreement, the operator's host allow-list, erasure and revocation read a refusal as "no agreement", "no restriction" and "done"; a retry the store would not queue left a run "retrying" for ever; two-factor enrolment threw away a refused lift of its session's limit; a password change wrote a limit computed from a policy read that had been refused; a sign-in without a second step could issue a session after a disable had run; five payments that can never be refunded starved the sixth; a plan was dropped when no connection could be opened at all; and two tests could not fail for the reason they claimed. **Class sweep.** The grep of the pattern (a refused call read as a default) over the store layer and the kernel crates, and what it found, are in the rule above. **Seventh round ("fail-closed II").** A third re-check of the fixes found 32 more (a few of them low or already recorded) and the review of the error table (A3) six; they were fixed with a test that fails without the fix, or recorded above. *Revocation:* every change that ends sessions writes a new credential epoch (`epoch` on the account, random, in the same atomic write as the change: password change, reset, disable, offboarding, groups, removed factor, removed passkey); a sign-in reads it with the credential it checks, a half-finished sign-in carries it to its second step, a passwordless sign-in checks the passkey again after reading it, and the session is compared once written (an unequal epoch ends it and refuses the sign-in); the refusal of a finished sign-in is no longer answered as a store fault (signin-1, signin-2; tests `epoch_tests::*`). *Lockout:* a refused account read is not a wrong guess, a guess that cannot be counted is the store's error, the log says "lockout" only for a count that was read (signin-3, signin-4, tests-docs-9; `lockout_tests::*`). *Connectors:* a consent is withdrawn after its connection is revoked, so a partial request can be repeated; revoking reads the consents with the lookup that reports a refusal; an erased party is noted in the call that erased it (also on the command line, where nothing was registered) and the entries and the `erased` row of the flow log go in one atomic batch; the links of an imported record are needed steps; an insert error that is not a duplicate is no longer taken for one; dead letters follow the retention; a refused policy read is not shown or stored as the closed policy; a host list saved by another process applies within five seconds; refreshed tokens, a finished delivery and the package list of an uninstall are no longer lost to one refusal (connectors-1 to -7, -10, sweep-1, -4, -5, -6, -9, tests-docs-1 to -3; the tests are named in docs/ISSUES.md K11 to K16 and in `crates/modules/connectors/src/tests.rs`). *Operator:* `obop site set` reads the settings with the lookup that reports a refusal (sweep-2); `migrate run` opens the uploaded files only when it takes the backup, and the strict gate no longer reads a refused `archive_mode` or a database size as "off" and zero (sweep-7, sweep-8); the payments sweep stops at a refused store call and does not start the hour (money-pool-2, tests-docs-4). *Pool:* a connection found dead before the caller's own statement was sent (the limit of the session, `BEGIN`) is unavailable with nothing in flight (tests-docs-8; `pool_tests::a_connection_that_could_not_be_had_is_unavailable_and_nothing_was_in_flight`, `pg_pool_tests::only_a_statement_on_a_connection_that_broke_is_in_flight`, money-pool-3). *Error table:* the token of `obop call`, `import`, `mcp` and `commands` is looked up in a request scope (scope-2), the stdio server answers a batch line call by call (scope-1), `unforge` sets a code apart when any text or name of the input contains it, in any case, and leaves storage texts alone (forgery-1, forgery-2), the REST log prints only the store's own explanation, cleaned (forgery-3), and the binary test was made able to fail (tests-1). *Tests of the backup sites:* the schema label of a backup, the status and ledger of a rollback, the module gate and the scheduled backup (tests-docs-6). The status line said "Revised twice" in an earlier copy; it counts the rounds now (tests-docs-10). **Evidence.** D44, D59, D66, D67, docs/DEPLOY.md "Connection pool and poolers", the PostgreSQL documentation for `statement_timeout`, `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` and advisory locks, the PgBouncer documentation for transaction pooling, the tests above.

#### D75 System fields, row version and record history
**Status** PROPOSED (owner-directed in chat, 2026-10-25/26, as part of item 1 "storage foundations", second wave; requirements OBR-DAT-018 and 019 and the tombstone and `updated_since` need of 009; not yet ratified as binding). **Reversibility** two-way door for the code: the planner is additive, a model that is not tracked runs the old code byte for byte, an N-1 binary writes without stamps and entries and the next tracked write repairs the record. One-way doors once an instance holds data in them: the six reserved key names (the install gate already refuses them, see "Built today"), the change format `{b, a, t}` and the `record_change` rows of a backup, the spelling of the reserved actors, and the history rows themselves. Nothing of that is stored today, because no model is tracked yet. **Owner action** yes, four questions (Q44 to Q47: grandfathering, the visible spellings, the history of `connection`, retention), none of which blocks starting work: Q44, Q45 and Q46 are needed before the first model is tracked (the PostgreSQL history package tracks none), Q47 before an instance holds personal data in tracked models. This record asks for no CLI-only exemption: the maintenance command is an ordinary admin command (Decision 8).

**Source.** The requirements are OBR-DAT-018 (system fields and the row version), 019 (record history) and the tombstone and `updated_since` need of 009 item 5 of the data-storage requirements of a project that builds on obop (kept outside this repository). Where the requirement text contradicts the repository, the repository wins and the last paragraph of Decision says where. The design was made by three independent designs and a judge and was then reconciled with the three sibling storage designs (D76, D77, D78) in one integration plan (the wave-2 plan, `wave2-plan` of the session notes in docs/HANDOFF.md, which is a working document and is not in this repository). The rulings of that plan are followed here and are written "plan K1" to "plan K27" in D75 to D78, because docs/ISSUES.md has its own rows K1 to K27 with other content, and those are written "ISSUES K..". Where a single design said otherwise the ruling wins. Read with D44, D48, D49, D58, D72, D73 and D74.

**Built today, and not built.** Four packages are on main, and a fifth, the PostgreSQL side, arrives with pull request 25. (1) `ModelExt` on every `ModelDef` (`history`, `indexes`, `guard`), the constants `SYSTEM_FIELDS` (6), `RESERVED_FIELDS` (8: the six, `id`, `if_version`), `RESERVED_MODELS` (`record_change`, `storage_event`, `storage_index`, `data_guard`, `device`) and `GENERATED_COMMANDS` (8, with `history`). (2) The seams of `obop-storage`: `ctx.rs` (`WriteCtx` with `by`, `via`, `batch_id`, `run_id`, `primary`, `actions`; a thread-local scope that ends by token), `tracked.rs` (the `Tracked` wrapper that `open` returns around every store, `apply_ctx`, `apply_tracked`, `track`, `untrack`; a test fails when `Tracked`, `Guarded` or a test double forgets to forward a `Backend` method; writes to the pseudo-model `record_change` are refused), `schema.rs` (parts, `Ddl`, `OpenOptions`), `PgPool::read_tx`, and `Engine::unit()` as the one constructor of a unit of work. (3) The first slice of the record itself (pull request 14): the install gate refuses a field named like a system field or `if_version` or `id`, a model named like a reserved model and a custom action named `history`, each with a rename hint, and a model that wants `history = "none"` needs a `history_reason` and may not be sensitive or governed (manifest keys `history`, `history_reason`, `history_retention_days`); a package installed before the gate is grandfathered (it loads at start, rolls back and is put back after a failed upgrade; installing or upgrading one that keeps a reserved name is refused); `module.list` and `module.get` show `upgraded_at` where they showed `updated_at`, and `connection.created_at` is dropped from the connectors module. `obop-storage` has the planner that memory, SQLite and PostgreSQL share (`sysfields.rs`, called from the one `run_ops`): it strips supplied system keys, stamps `row_version` and `updated_at` once per record per transaction, writes back a record that ends as it began, drops a write that changes nothing, appends one history entry per changed record, keeps class `secret` and undeclared keys out, seals the sensitive classes, marks a value over 32 KiB, and lets `Op::Patch` require `row_version` (an absent version reads as 1, a mismatch is a `Conflict` with the reason `stale_version:<current>`). `Op::EraseHistory` scrubs the values of a record from its entries and appends an `erase` entry without values. Memory and SQLite keep the history (the SQLite table is created inside the first transaction that writes history, so the schema of a database that has none is unchanged); `Backend::history`, the backfill pass and the export and import of history exist for them, and history rows travel in a backup as `record_change` rows marked `$kind = history.v1`; an old backup restores as before. The `Backend` methods that read or scrub history return an error by default, never an empty answer. (4) The list of tracked models is empty, so **no model is tracked and the product writes the same bytes as before**; a golden test compares memory, SQLite and PostgreSQL with bytes written on unmodified main. (5) The PostgreSQL side (pull request 25, `crates/kernel/obop-storage/src/pg_hist.rs`; operators: docs/DEPLOY.md "Change history and system fields on PostgreSQL", ISSUES K28 to K33): a schema part `history` that is made on first use and not at start, so a database installed before it opens as it did under any role (the table `obop_record_change`, partitioned by month with a default partition, its sequence and three indexes, the append-only guard as row and truncate triggers, and the three `SECURITY DEFINER` functions that make a month, drop an old one and erase the values of a record's entries); the stamps (the database's clock, read after the record's lock in one statement) and the entries (one flush per batch) written in the transaction of the write; `Backend::history`, `changes` (the feed in the order of the transaction number and the entry number, below the horizon of the oldest open write transaction, with cursors bound to the epoch and to the cluster and its time line), `history_stats`, `history_ensure_partition`, `history_drop_partition`, `ensure_history_schema` and `backfill_sysfields`; the export and import of the entries, with the partitions and the sequence made by the restore (ISSUES K23 (c) and (d) are closed). It is tested against PostgreSQL 18 in CI. It tracks no model, so nothing creates the part in the shipped binary yet. **Not built:** the timer that makes and drops partitions and applies `history_retention_days` (the primitives exist; D75 WP9); tracking by `Dispatcher::register_crud` and the write context in `Dispatcher::run`; `if_version` in `update`, `delete` and the actions, `If-Match`, `ETag` and the answer 412 (the error table has the row `precondition_failed`, and nothing produces it); the `<model>.history` command (registered, off on every model); the global feed, `GET /api/history`, the group `audit.reader`; `record_change.maintain`; the ledger step that installs the history part for a role that cannot create it, and the grandfathering message at start-up for PostgreSQL; the Change log page, the conflict banner and the read-only system section of the record panel; the re-sealing of sealed history by `secrets rotate`; the write-path bench. Everything in Decision below that depends on those is the design, not the product.

**Decision.** *1. Where the fields live.* Six reserved keys inside the document of every tracked model: `created_at` and `updated_at` (`YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MM:SS.ffffffZ`, UTC, microseconds, the bytes of D72's `datetime`), `created_by` and `updated_by`, `row_version` (a whole number counting the changes, from 1) and `sys_backfilled` (true only on a record whose `created_at` is an estimate). A model is tracked when `Dispatcher::register_crud` registers it; internal tables (`auth_*`, `oauth_*`, `obop_*`, `sequence_counter`, `bulk_idempotency`, blob metadata, connector logs, `security_event`) are never tracked, because they hold hashes, counters and integer-second `created_at` of their own and a global stamp would overwrite them. The keys are plain fields for every reader (plan K10): the storage layer treats `updated_at` as an ordinary JSON key, no index is made for it automatically (D78 makes one for `created_at` only), and a later typed layout lifts the six keys to columns with the same names. Until D76's work package for the D72 kinds is in, the timestamps are declared `Text` in `system_defs` (their canonical text sorts as the instant) and the pushdown treats them as a `Finish` key. *2. The write context.* The principal reaches storage as an explicit argument, `Backend::apply_ctx(&WriteCtx, ops)`. `Dispatcher::run` installs the context as an RAII scope; the legacy shorthand writes (`insert`, `update`, `delete`, `insert_many`, `apply_checked`) on a tracked model read the scope and fall back to `system:internal`. Memory, SQLite and PostgreSQL implement a hidden `apply_tracked`, `apply_checked` is it with the system context, and only `Tracked` implements `apply_ctx` (plan K14). One mechanism covers the engine and the custom handlers (`party.erase`, connector handlers) without changing their signatures. *3. The planner (built on memory, SQLite and PostgreSQL).* Inside `run_ops`, for tracked keys only: strip supplied system keys, compute the new record, compare canonical forms (system keys and nulls ignored), and write nothing when they are equal. The first effective write of a record in a transaction stamps it (`row_version + 1`, `updated_at = max(clock, previous + 1 microsecond)`); later writes of the same transaction change data only. A record that was stamped and ends the transaction as it began (A to B to A) is written back with its original system keys and leaves no entry. Memory and SQLite stamp with a strictly increasing process clock, which is the host clock and is said so. PostgreSQL takes the instant from `clock_timestamp()` returned by the same statement that locks the row (built; no extra round trip). *4. Actor values.* `created_by` and `updated_by` hold the login of the person (for an agent, its human owner) or a reserved id that contains a colon, which no login can: `system:internal`, `public:anonymous`, `connector:<id>`. A record that predates the tracking has no actor and `sys_backfilled = true`. An agent's token is recorded as `via.token_ref`, the first 12 hex digits of the stored token hash, which is what the Security page already shows as a session id. The requirement's `via` list mixes two axes and is split into `surface` (`rest`, `mcp`, `cli`, `connector`, `public`, `system`) and `path` (`direct`, `batch`, `effect`, `migration`, `erase`). *5. The history.* One table, `obop_record_change` on PostgreSQL, appended once per transaction by the same `run_ops` inside the same transaction, for every write path that reaches storage (one `INSERT ... SELECT FROM unnest(...)`: the system fields add no round trip, the history adds one). One entry per record per transaction, except update-then-delete, which writes two; a record created and deleted in one transaction leaves none. The entry is `seq` (global, unique, the entry id), `txid` (`xid8`, for the feed), `at` (equal to the record's `updated_at`, so the feed and `updated_since` agree), `model`, `record_id`, `op` (`create`, `update`, `delete`, `transition`, `erase`, `import`), `actor`, `via`, `row_version`, `changed`, `sealed`, `batch_id`, `run_id`. `changed` is `{field: {"b": before, "a": after, "t": marker}}` for the fields that changed: `b` is present iff the field existed before (a null counts as absent), `a` iff it exists after; a `create` carries `a` for every non-null field, a `delete` carries `b`; the system keys never appear. The marker `redacted` (class `secret`, an undeclared key, a sensitive value when no key ring is configured) carries neither `b` nor `a`; `sealed` (class `special_category` or `confidential_hr`) carries `KeyRing` envelopes sealed with the purpose `history.v1:{model}.{field}`; `erased` is written by the erase; `large` (a value over 32 KiB, `HISTORY_VALUE_MAX`) carries its length. Folding the `a` sides from the `create` reproduces the stored canonical fields of every unmarked field. *6. Append-only.* On PostgreSQL, row and truncate triggers on the parent and on each partition refuse any change unless `obop.history_maint = on` and `current_user` is the table owner; three `SECURITY DEFINER` functions (erase, ensure a partition, drop a partition, which re-checks the age floor itself) are the only way through (the re-wrap of sealed history by `secrets rotate` would be a fourth, which is not designed yet; see sealed-key loss under Threat model). This stops code accidents today, because the serving role is the owner of the table; it becomes a boundary when D77 revokes UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE from the runtime role. Memory and SQLite are append-only by API only (D44). *7. Partitions.* Monthly by `at` (UTC) plus a default partition that is a fail-open net, made by `obop_history_ensure_partition(month)`, which also re-homes rows that landed in the default; nothing depends on the typed layout. *8. Retention and maintenance.* The manifest key `history_retention_days` per model; the default keeps for ever. A partition is dropped only when every model present in it has a retention and `partition_end + max(retention)` is past; a model that is not registered counts as keep for ever. Maintenance is `record_change.maintain {dry_run}`, an ordinary admin command on REST, MCP, CLI and the Change log page; the status (`history_stats`: rows in the default partition, the lag of the feed horizon) is shown on that page and by `obop storage check`. There is no `obop history` command and no CLI-only exemption (plan K22), because dropping a partition goes through a definer function that the runtime role may call. *9. Row version.* `if_version` (input), `If-Match` (REST), `ETag` (output). The authority is `Op::Patch{expect: row_version}` under the row lock; the command layer compares first for a clean early error; a governed transition always requires the version it read. A mismatch is `CallError::PreconditionFailed{current}`, HTTP 412 with the current version in the body and the `ETag`; a `Conflict` with the reason `stale_version:n` is mapped to it by `Unit::commit`. A missing version means last write wins. An `update` that echoes a system field unchanged (an agent that sends back the record it read) is accepted and ignored, as computed fields are today; any other value is refused with 400 naming the field; create, bulk rows, CSV headers, effect targets and the MCP and CLI input refuse any system name; `If-Match` on get, list, search, create, bulk_create and history is 400 and never silently ignored. A stale approval gives 412 to the approver (the plan is consumed) and the agent proposes again; capturing a version when a proposal is made is not done. *10. Reads.* The generated command `<model>.history {id, limit <= 200, after}` (the 8th generated command) answers newest first under the row rules and the field groups: the history of a record the caller cannot see is "not found", the same as an unknown id; a deleted record is gated on its last values (`Gate::visible_deleted`, shared with D78); a field the caller may not read is withheld; an agent sees the mask token for `actor`. The global feed is `Backend::changes`, paged by `(txid, seq)` below the snapshot horizon so that a transaction that commits late is not skipped, served as `GET /api/history` (an alias of the feed command), the Change log page and the group `audit.reader`. **Tombstones are the `delete` entries**: a delete stays a hard delete. *11. Backup, restore and import.* `Backend::export` emits the history after the records, in the same repeatable-read snapshot, as pseudo-rows of the model `record_change` (on main marked `$kind = history.v1`), paged by `(at, seq)`; `import` routes them to the history table with their `seq` and `txid = 0`, ensures each month first and finishes with `setval('obop_record_change_seq', max(seq))`; the wipe drops the feed epoch (`kv` key `obop.history.epoch`), so a cursor from before a restore answers 410. The wipe runs with the maintenance setting and so needs the owner role; D73's restore opens the owner URL. A backup of format 1 restores to schema 2 and the backfill step stamps it. *12. Migration.* The history schema is a part of `schema.rs` (`history`), installed by `install_all` and ensured under the existing schema lock; ledger steps **0004 `history_schema`** and **0006 `sysfields_backfill`** (numbers fixed at merge; 0003 is D77's, 0005 D76's; each DDL step carries the digest of its schema part and a test fails when the SQL changes without a new step). The backfill is an optimisation, not a prerequisite: a legacy record (no `row_version`) is normalised lazily on its first write, reads show the system fields absent until then, the UI sends no `if_version` for it and the server treats a missing version as 1. The step walks an allow-list of the tracked models (never a deny-list: `auth_token.created_at` is an integer that a blanket pass would overwrite) in batches of 5,000 with a pause of 20 ms, taking `SKIP LOCKED`; its resume point is the predicate itself (`NOT (data ? 'row_version')`), so no cursor is stored; the fallback instant for ids that are not UUIDv7 is stored once (`kv_put_new`) so that a `kill -9` and a rerun end in the same state; it fails with "run obop migrate run" when it is not told the tracked models. *13. Installed modules that use a reserved name.* They are **grandfathered**: the model is not tracked, a loud message is logged at start, its own field keeps working, its history command is off, and the next upgrade of the module is refused by the gate until it renames the field (a `[[migration]] rename_field` step exists). An instance is never blocked from upgrading by a third-party module (Q44 asks whether that is right). The two built-ins that used a name were changed (see "Built today"). *14. Where the requirement text loses to the repository.* REST is `POST /api/{model}/{command}` only, so `If-Match` and `ETag` ride on that POST. The requirement says no bundled package declares the reserved names; two did, and are changed. `personal_data` is not a data class (`obop-types::DataClass`), so `created_by` and `updated_by` are class `internal` with the personal-data flag and agents see mask tokens (D40). There is no separate UUIDv7 entry id: `seq` is the id of the entry, the table is `obop_record_change` (the repository's prefix) and `record_change` is the model name of the feed and of the backup rows. It is the field-change history that docs/ARCHITECTURE.md ("Audit and history") and docs/CONVENTIONS.md (the import row of the rename map in section 8.1 and the open item in section 9.2) call `audit_change`: one entry per record per transaction with the changed fields and their old and new values, not one row per field; it sits beside the append-only `security_event` log (whose hash chain is design, not built: its rows are written plainly by `obop-commands/src/audit.rs` and `obop audit verify` does not exist) and does not replace it. It is kept per tracked model, not opted into per field as ARCHITECTURE says of `audit_change`: a model keeps the full history unless it declares `history = "none"` with a reason (the manifest gates under Built today and the cost note under Consequences). Those documents and docs/spec/security-privacy.md 4.4 keep the older name until the package that tracks models conforms them (the PostgreSQL package leaves them as they are). D49's "no tombstones before the audit log exists" is superseded for a model with history (this record amends D49 once built; the text of D49 stands until then). "Both layouts" has no meaning: only the JSON-document layout exists.

**Threat model.** Which defences below exist today is said under "Built today, and not built"; the others are the design. *Assets:* the truth of who changed what and when for every record of a tracked model; the integrity of the version that optimistic concurrency relies on; the secrecy of the values history copies (history is a second copy of every change, so a sensitive value that entered it would outlive the record); the availability of the write path, because the entry is written in the same transaction. *A forged actor:* the actor never comes from input. The context is built from the authenticated principal; `_actor*` and `_caller` keys in input are stripped in `call` as today; an agent's write is attributed to its owner with `via.agent` and `via.token_ref`; an approval records the proposer as `by` and the approver as `via.approver`; a write on a thread with no scope (a detached thread, a leaked scope) is attributed to `system:internal`, never to a person, and the scope is restored on a panic; a switch (`OBOP_STRICT_WRITE_CTX=1`, designed, not read by any code yet) turns an unscoped write to a tracked model into an error so the whole suite can be run once to list the unscoped writers. The reserved ids contain a colon, which `valid_name` of `obop-auth` does not allow in a login, so no person can be named like one. *Not bounded:* a holder of the runtime database credentials writes SQL directly and can name any actor. *Forged system fields:* a body that names a system field is refused (Decision 9); storage strips the six keys from every op as the last line of defence, which is built and tested today. *History tampering:* Decision 6; until D77 revokes privileges the append-only trigger is an accident guard and the record says so; a hash chain (OBR-DAT-025) is not built, and a column for it can be added instantly. *Secret leakage into history:* class `secret` and undeclared keys are never stored; the sensitive classes are sealed, or marked `redacted` when no key ring is configured, never kept in plain text; a test plants a canary in a secret field and in a token and looks for it in every entry. *Erase resurrection after a restore:* the erase (`party.erase`) is one transaction that scrubs the record's values from all its entries and appends an `erase` entry with names and counts only; a restore from a backup older than the erase brings the erased values back, because the backup holds them, until the erasure ledger replays (OBR-SVC-056, not built); this is documented in docs/DEPLOY.md with the PostgreSQL package. *Sealed-key loss:* a sealed value cannot be read without the key that sealed it; rotation keeps the previous keys and `secrets rotate` re-wraps sealed history (a work package; the re-wrap is an update of append-only history rows, which the trigger of Decision 6 allows only to the table owner under the maintenance setting and which the runtime role of D77 cannot make, so it needs the owner role or a definer function of its own: design, decided with the sealed-history package, and until then listed among the online writes that D77 and Q53 say are refused under split roles); if every key is lost the entries stay and still say that a value changed, but the values are gone, as they are for the sealed backups of D73. *Existence oracle:* history of an invisible record is indistinguishable from an unknown id; the feed needs `audit.reader`. *Privilege confusion by approval:* Decision 9 and 4. *Write-path denial:* a history insert that fails fails the write (fail closed); a missing partition cannot cause it, because the default partition takes the row.

**Acceptance tests (named).** *On main today* (`crates/kernel/obop-storage/src/sys_tests.rs`, on memory and SQLite, each name a test): `a_tracked_record_is_stamped_and_an_untracked_one_is_not`; `a_write_that_changes_nothing_leaves_no_version_and_no_entry`; `a_record_that_ends_as_it_began_is_written_back_as_it_was`; `a_record_is_stamped_once_per_transaction`; `the_stamps_of_a_record_only_grow_whatever_the_clock_does`; `a_record_made_and_changed_in_one_transaction_has_one_entry_with_the_final_values`; `a_record_changed_and_then_deleted_leaves_two_entries`; `a_bulk_batch_is_one_transaction_with_one_entry_per_row_in_order`; `an_entry_says_who_and_how_and_a_governed_move_is_a_transition`; `the_old_entry_points_stamp_a_tracked_model_with_the_context_they_find`; `a_secret_is_never_stored_and_an_undeclared_field_is_treated_like_one`; `a_sensitive_value_is_sealed_under_the_key_ring_and_redacted_without_one`; `a_value_over_32_kib_is_a_marker_with_its_length`; `a_model_without_history_is_stamped_and_logs_nothing`; `a_patch_can_require_the_version_and_an_absent_version_reads_as_one`; `two_writers_with_the_same_version_cannot_both_win`; `a_record_from_before_the_tracking_is_stamped_when_it_is_first_written`; `the_backfill_stamps_old_records_in_passes_and_nothing_else`; `a_refused_batch_leaves_no_record_no_stamp_and_no_entry`; `tracked_and_untracked_models_in_one_batch_each_do_their_own_thing`; `the_history_of_a_record_is_read_newest_first_in_pages_and_by_operation`; `an_erasure_removes_the_values_from_the_history_and_says_so_without_values`; `a_sealed_value_is_erased_like_any_other`; `the_history_rides_the_export_and_comes_back_with_its_numbers`; `a_backup_with_records_of_a_model_called_record_change_and_with_history_restores_both`; `a_database_from_before_the_history_opens_without_a_schema_change_and_gets_the_table_when_needed`; `the_history_always_adds_up_to_the_records` (seeded random operations, folding the entries gives the records); `a_store_without_a_history_refuses_a_model_that_wants_one_and_stamps_one_that_does_not`; in `obop-commands`, `the_registry_and_the_storage_layer_name_the_same_system_fields_in_the_same_order` and `the_timestamp_of_the_storage_layer_is_the_canonical_datetime`; in `golden_tests.rs` the golden script that compares the bytes written by memory, SQLite and PostgreSQL with those of unmodified main; the seams of `seams_tests.rs` (`the_tickets_of_a_write_are_visible_to_the_database_in_its_transaction_and_only_there`, `read_tx_is_one_snapshot_and_read_only`). *On PostgreSQL* the package has tests of its own under other names than the design's (`pg_hist_tests.rs`, `sys_tests.rs` and `seams_tests.rs` of `obop-storage`, run in CI against PostgreSQL 18): the append-only guard on the parent, on a month and on the default partition, for the owner and for another role; the making, re-homing and dropping of partitions, and the refusal of a young one; the feed, its horizon and its order; the backfill; backup and restore with the partitions and the sequence; the part made twice and by several processes at once; a lost connection and a refused commit leave neither record nor entry. The design's `pg_history_atomic_under_kill9` (50 kills of the process, then both or neither) is not built. *Of the work packages that are not built (the tests do not exist yet):* in `obop-commands`, `six_paths_refuse_system_fields`, `update_echoing_unchanged_system_fields_is_accepted`, `if_version_flow`, `transition_compares_the_version_it_read`, `agent_write_records_owner_and_via`, `approval_records_approver`, `bulk_entries_share_batch_id`, `connector_inbound_actor_is_connector_id`, `public_checkout_and_payments_have_reserved_actors`, `history_visibility_follows_row_rules`, `history_none_is_refused_for_sensitive_or_governed_models`, `grandfathered_module_is_untracked`, `party_erase_is_one_transaction_and_history_has_no_canary`, `strict_write_ctx_mode`; on REST, MCP, CLI and OpenAPI, `rest_etag_and_if_match`, `rest_412_body_and_etag`, `if_match_on_create_is_400`, `mcp_if_version_and_history_tool`, `cli_if_version_is_typed`, `openapi_documents_readonly_etag_412_history`; for migration and backup, `step_history_schema_is_idempotent`, `backfill_step_requires_tracked_models`, `backfill_step_resumes_after_kill`, `restore_format1_then_migrate_backfills`, `n_minus_1_write_is_repaired_lazily`; for sealed history, `sealed_history_readable_only_with_field_group` and `reseal_rewraps_sealed_history`; in the browser, `history.spec.ts` (edit twice shows two entries; a stale save shows the conflict banner and keeps the draft; the system section is read-only and hidden on create; erased and sealed markers render); and the ignored bench `perf_sysfields_vs_untracked` (10,000 single-record updates and a batch of 500: tracked without history within +10 percent of untracked, with history within +30 percent of without, statements per transaction printed) with the combined write-path bench that the integration plan asks for (untracked, system fields, history, guard triggers and eight expression indexes on 300,000 rows).

**Why.** An obop record has no author, no time and no version, and a delete leaves nothing: two people who edit one record overwrite each other without a warning, a client that syncs cannot tell a deleted record from an unchanged one (D49 refused tombstones until a log exists), and the only history is the security log and a few command audits. A system that is meant to be the one place of a business has to say who did what and when, refuse a save made on an old state, and keep a history that the code that writes the records cannot rewrite. The fields live as keys of the existing document because every read path, the pool (D74), the kinds (D72), the pushdown (D76) and the indexes (D78) already work on that layout, and because one pure planner then serves three backends and gives one behaviour; the history is one extra table in the same transaction because a history that can disagree with the records is worse than none.

**Rejected.** *Dedicated columns on `obop_record` for the six fields:* they touch every read path, collide with the pool, the pushdown and the typed-layout work, and need a new `Op::Expect`; the typed layout lifts the keys later. *A stage-then-flush rewrite of `run_ops`:* risk for payments, OAuth and the installer for no gain. *A setting alone as the append-only guard:* anyone who can run SQL sets it; the trigger plus the owner test plus the definer functions is the guard that D77 turns into a boundary. *Tracking every table:* it would overwrite the integer `created_at` of `auth_token` and stamp counters. *An entry per field or per statement:* more rows, no more truth; one entry per record per transaction is what a reader asks for. *`record_change` as an ordinary `obop_record` model:* a write through `Backend::insert` would collide with the exported rows (plan K27), so storage refuses writes to the reserved pseudo-models. *A CLI-only `history status|prune` with an exemption* (the design's first proposal): the definer function makes the exemption unnecessary (plan K22). *Blocking an instance's upgrade when an installed third-party module declares a reserved name:* grandfathering keeps the instance upgradable and refuses only the upgrade of that module (Q44). *Putting D77 first:* the entry is useful against defects today, and D77 turns the guard into a boundary later; the record says which it is today. *Capturing the version when an approval is proposed:* it changes how approvals work and is not done in this wave.

**Consequences.** *Cost.* The budgets are targets with a named bench, not measurements: tracked without history within +10 percent of untracked, with history within +30 percent of without; if the second is exceeded the last record write and the history insert are fused into one statement for single-record transactions. Nothing was measured at DP-M scale. *Behaviour changes of the first slice* (two, both visible to clients): `module.list` and `module.get` show `upgraded_at` where they showed `updated_at`, and `connection.created_at` is dropped (old connection records keep a stored `created_at` that is withheld, not reported as denied). *Behaviour changes of the packages still to come:* the six names are refused as input; `update` echoing a system field unchanged is accepted; a stale `if_version` is 412; `connection` (built-in, governed, written by the connector worker) keeps full history, which yields `system:internal` entries on every sync (Q46); a hot-counter model written by `Op::Add` writes one entry per sale and may set `history = "none"` with a reason when it has no sensitive class and no governed field. *Rolling deploys.* An N-1 binary writes without stamps or entries, its `Put` drops the system keys (the next tracked write repairs the record) and it lists the keys as withheld fields (cosmetic); a new ledger step blocks a downgrade by schema version (D73), so rollback means restoring the backup taken before the step. *Sync.* A consumer that reads by `updated_at` must overlap its window by the longest transaction (the lock wait plus 5 s, 65 s; D78 sets the default) or read the feed, because a record is stamped when it is written, not when it commits. *Growth.* The default keeps history for ever; the retention defaults per data class are not set (Q47). *Docs.* docs/MODULES.md 3.2 (the history keys, the reserved names and the generated names) says what the gate does today; docs/CONVENTIONS.md 9.1 carries the open question about the reserved actors and the feed group; docs/DEPLOY.md says how history is installed, backed up and partitioned on PostgreSQL (pruning waits for the timer); the rest is this record.

**Not delivered, and why.** Preserve-history import with an `imported` run id (OBR-DAT-021; `op = 'import'` is allowed by the table's check); the hash chain (025); real role separation (D77: until then the append-only guard is an accident guard and the restore needs the owner role); field encryption of the record itself (057; sealed history is interim); replay of the erasure ledger after a restore (OBR-SVC-056); pseudonymising `actor` when a person is erased (OBR-SVC-052; the `created_by` of an offboarded login is kept for accountability); read audit and MCP-call audit; promotion of the six keys to typed columns (OBR-DAT-011); the `Datetime` kind for the timestamps and aggregation by month on `created_at` until D76's work package for the D72 kinds; automatic version capture on an approval proposal; the measured budgets; the PostgreSQL package and everything listed under "Built today, and not built". The open items of the review of the first slice are ISSUES K23 and K24 (an export that panics on a number near `f64::MAX`, the PostgreSQL routing of `record_change` rows (closed by the PostgreSQL package), a seven-name row of the error table in docs/MODULES.md, a test whose "no table behind" check cannot fail, an overflow of an imported entry number in memory, an `UNSTAMPED` that can disagree with `row_version` above `i64::MAX`).

**Not tried.** The PostgreSQL package runs as code against PostgreSQL 18 in CI (the design's SQL was first run by hand on PostgreSQL 17). Not tried at all: partitions at the scale of millions of entries (the design names a manual gate of a 200-entry read over 7.5 million entries), the feed under a real long transaction in production, PgBouncer in transaction mode with the maintenance setting, managed PostgreSQL, the sealed history through a key rotation, the Change log page and the conflict banner in a browser, and any measured cost of the stamps.

**Evidence.** The packages on main were merged after adversarial review and with the project's gates: the seams (pull request 11, head afe48fc, 16 findings in the first review and one low item after the check, ISSUES K22; what the review left open is ISSUES K21) and the first slice (pull request 14, head db037f9, 16 findings in the first review, 3 medium and all fixed with tests, then five low items recorded as ISSUES K23 and K24) passed GitHub Actions rust (Linux, PostgreSQL 18), web, e2e and lockfiles; locally cargo fmt, clippy `-D warnings` and the workspace against PostgreSQL 18, 64 suites with 1,747 tests passed and none failed at the merge of the first slice. The PostgreSQL package (pull request 25) was reviewed adversarially twice after it was built. The first review confirmed 23 findings and refuted 8. The findings about code and SQL (the order of the feed, which was an unexplained flake until this review, ISSUES K33; the start under a role that cannot create tables; the binding of cursors to the cluster; the grants of a serving role; the age floor of the drop; the dependence of tests on the calendar and the clock; the swallowed failure of the install; the schema name in the SQL; the `CHECK`s; the built-in functions called by the definer functions) are fixed, each with a test. The findings about wording and text (what holds the feed back, the change of owner, the longest retention, the stale statements here, in `CLAUDE.md` and in comments) are fixed in the text only, and no test pins them. The second review (four reviewers, two skeptics for each finding) confirmed 16 further findings, 3 of them medium, and refuted 5: entries that carry the transaction numbers of another cluster after a dump and a load (counted apart, warned about, and a renumbering in docs/DEPLOY.md that a test runs as written), a check at start that was prepared outside a transaction (a test now reads the wire), and an age floor that an interval of mixed signs lowered (tested as an instant); the other code and SQL findings of that review have a test each, the rest are text only. This paragraph first said that all 23 findings were fixed with tests, which was not true. Its SQL is in `crates/kernel/obop-storage/src/pg_hist.rs`.

#### D76 Query pushdown to the database, with compiled row rules
**Status** PROPOSED (owner-directed in chat, 2026-10-25/26, as part of item 1 "storage foundations", second wave; requirement OBR-DAT-007; not yet ratified as binding). **Reversibility** two-way door for the code: every new `Backend` method is defaulted to "not supported", memory and SQLite keep the in-memory evaluator as they are, `OBOP_QUERY_PUSHDOWN=off` restores today's path, no data is migrated and nothing is stored but eight collations and the rows of one log model. Not reversible once an instance relies on them: the behaviour changes of Decision 12 (two of them, the refusal of NUL in a text filter and the new splitting of a `shared_with` list, are already live). **Owner action** yes, four questions (Q48 to Q51: the accepted differences between the database's text order and the in-memory order, the behaviour changes, the default switch, the event retention and the PostgreSQL floor), none of which blocks starting work: Q48 and Q49 are needed before pushdown is switched on by default, Q50 before the first release that contains the pushdown, and Q51 when `storage_event` is built. This record amends D49 and one sentence of D58 once the code is built (Decision 13); until then both stand as written.

**Source.** The requirement is OBR-DAT-007 (search and list evaluated in the database, with row rules) of the data-storage requirements of a project that builds on obop (kept outside this repository); 008, 009, 010 and 011 are touched only as contracts (D78 owns 008 and 009). Where the requirement text contradicts the repository, the repository wins: the budgets and personas of the requirement belong to another company and are replaced by generic synthetic models of 10,000, 100,000 and 1,000,000 rows and principals named by the kind of rule; "`EXPLAIN` shows no sequential scan" is restated as "a query whose filter, order and rule columns are indexed in the shapes of Decision 14 uses those indexes, and everything else is bounded by the 3 s rule", because all models share one heap; "no command reads more rows than the page" is restated for `list`, `search`, `get`, uniqueness, references, referrers and aggregates (the computed-field preload that reads a whole child model is fixed by `find_in`, the other whole-model reads are listed under Not delivered). The design was made by three designs and a judge and reconciled with D75, D77 and D78 in the wave-2 integration plan (see D75 Source; its rulings are written "plan K.." and are not the rows of docs/ISSUES.md); its rulings (plan K1 to K27) are followed, in particular that the typed SQL expressions have one writer (`keyexpr.rs`, plan K3), that text order is pushed down by D76 (plan K4) and that the member tokenizer is D78's (plan K5). Read with D10, D49, D57, D58, D72, D74, D75, D78 and D79.

**Built today, and not built.** *Built* (packages on main): (1) `obop-storage/src/keyexpr.rs`, the one writer of the typed SQL expressions for a field of a record stored as a JSONB document. Its slots are `Str` (collated `"C"`), `Raw` (uncollated, for ICU order and `contains`), `Int`, `Bool`, `Date`, `Instant`, `Dec`, `SelPos`, `Members`, `Lower` and `Id`; each is NULL exactly where the in-memory `stored_key` is empty and none can raise a cast error on hostile stored data; the semantics are the fuzzed ones of this design (an integer is `scale(v::numeric) = 0`, not `v = trunc(v)`; a date must exist on the calendar; a decimal mirrors `Decimal::parse`). `EXPR_VERSION` is 2; a pinned digest with a version history fails when any text changes without a new version; a lint over every crate fails on any other read of a field out of a document, except two whole files (`ALLOWED_FILES`: the writer itself, which spells the patterns, and `obop-commands/src/keyexpr_parity.rs`, which spells the earlier wrong expressions on purpose) and six listed lines: the whole-document `CHECK` of the base schema, `Pg::find` (to be converted by D78), the two texts of the D57 unique index (which must stay as deployed databases hold them), `swap_fields` (a compare and swap under a bound field name), and one test. Nothing in the product calls the writer yet. (2) A parity test on PostgreSQL (`obop-commands/src/keyexpr_parity.rs`): 20,229 hostile documents, 404,580 comparisons, 0 differences, and a test of six mutants (the mistakes the designs first made) that it must catch. (3) Changes of the in-memory evaluator that the pushdown needs: a `shared_with` list is split on the eight ASCII separators and nothing is trimmed (a list separated by a tab, a lone carriage return, a form feed or a vertical tab now works; before, only comma, semicolon, space and line feed separated and every piece was trimmed with the Unicode `trim`, so a name padded with a non-breaking space no longer matches); a stored datetime counts in a filter, sort, bucket, aggregate group or computed read only in its canonical form; NUL in a text filter value is refused on every backend (`filter on M.f: text cannot contain NUL`); the access cache key is (model, id, hops); and a non-administrator's aggregate on a model whose row rule follows a parent no longer hangs (the engine loads the records and applies the rule outside the scan when the rule reads the store, `Gate::reads_store`). (4) The seams it uses: `PgPool::read_tx`, `Fault::Timeout` and the mapping of SQLSTATE 57014, `Tracked` forwarding, `schema.rs` parts. *Not built:* the `QuerySpec` IR and the defaulted `Backend` methods (`query`, `scan_query`, `query_caps`, `find_in`, `ensure_query_support`); `pg_sql.rs`, `pg_query.rs`, `explain.rs`, `collation.rs`; the lowering, the strategies and the row-rule compiler in `obop-commands/src/pushdown/`; the eight collations and their probe; `storage_event` (the name is reserved, nothing registers it) and its page; `accepted_differences.json`; the differential harness; shadow mode; the settings `OBOP_QUERY_PUSHDOWN`, `OBOP_QUERY_CANDIDATE_CAP`, `OBOP_QUERY_SHADOW_PERCENT` and `OBOP_QUERY_STRICT` (no code reads them); `find_in`; `Pg::find` in the slot form (D78's package). So **no query is pushed down today**, and nothing in this record's Decision except the items above runs.

**Decision.** *1. Grammar and policy stay in `obop-commands`.* `Query::parse` is still the only parser (kinds, limits, the D10 `usable` check). A `pushdown` module lowers a parsed `Query` and the actor's compiled row rule into `QuerySpec`, a registry-free IR defined in `obop-storage`: storage never sees the JSON grammar, `ModelDef` or `Actor`. `QuerySpec` is `non_exhaustive`: `after: Option<keyset::Bound>` and `total: Total::{Exact, Capped(u64), Estimate, Skip}` are in it from the first version (plan K9), and `Page` is `{rows, total, total_is_estimate, last, overflow}`. *2. `Backend` gets defaulted methods that mean "not supported".* `query`, `scan_query`, `query_caps`, `find_in` and `ensure_query_support`, all in `Tracked`'s forward list. Memory and SQLite keep the defaults, so `Query::apply` with `Gate` and `Computer` stays their implementation and is the **oracle** for PostgreSQL. Only `Pg` implements them. *3. One pure emitter.* `pg_sql.rs` turns a `QuerySpec` into SQL for the JSONB table behind a `Layout` trait (the typed layout of OBR-DAT-011 adds a second `Layout` and nothing else); `DocLayout` calls `keyexpr`. Identifiers enter SQL only as `Ident` (`[a-z0-9_]{1,64}`), the model name as a literal (needed to prove partial-index predicates and robust against statement caches), values only as `$n`; the statement text is a function of the shape of the spec and not of values or list lengths (`in` is one array parameter); every guard is an inline `CASE`; there are no SQL helper functions, so no function DDL and no version skew. *4.
