Four strategies*, chosen per call and counted in a metric. `Push`: everything in SQL, one statement for the rows, one for `total` when it cannot be derived, one repeatable-read snapshot. `OverFetch`: the row rule has a branch that cannot be compiled (a computed `public_field` outside the compiled subset); SQL relaxes the branch to TRUE and filters, orders and pages a superset, `Gate::visible` runs on the rows, and the probe grows by a factor of four up to the cap. `Finish`: a filter or order key is computed, a condition has a `bucket` (D72), the `lang` has no managed collation or `contains` cannot run in SQL; SQL applies every condition it can plus the rule, returns at most the cap in candidates, and the existing `Query::apply` (the oracle itself) sorts and pages them. `Oracle`: memory, SQLite, `OBOP_QUERY_PUSHDOWN=off`, a name that is not an identifier, an SQLSTATE class 22 error. A candidate set above the cap is an error that asks for a narrower filter ("too many candidates"), never a truncation. *5. Text order is a deterministic ICU collation per language.* `CREATE COLLATION obop_l1_<tag> (provider = icu, locale = '<tag>-u-kn-ks-level1', deterministic = true)` for `und en de fr pl fi sv es`. PostgreSQL's bytewise tie-break of a deterministic collation is exactly `TextOrder::compare`'s `then_with(a.cmp(b))`, so one `ORDER BY e COLLATE obop_l1_sv` equals the oracle ascending and descending. A `lang` maps to a tag only when its language subtag is in the set and it has no script, variant or extension (a region is allowed); otherwise the key is not pushable. A probe at connect orders about 40 strings per tag under the collation and compares them with the order of `icu4x`; a mismatch clears that tag, logs once and sets `obop_query_collation_ok{tag}` to 0. Text, long text and select keys therefore push down, and keyset arms on a text key compare under the same collation (not served by an index in this wave). The collations are a schema part (`query_support`, plan K13) installed by the owner side; the runtime only probes, and a missing collation or `pg_trgm` becomes a `QueryCaps` flag and a doctor finding, not a failed start. *6. Row rules (D58) are compiled into the same `WHERE`* by `compile_rule`, an exact mirror of `Gate::grants` that folds the actor's facts (administrator, held groups, empty id) at compile time and is default-deny. `via` becomes a nested `EXISTS` of up to four hops (the oracle's cut-off), "parent first" (`fk = ANY(ARRAY(SELECT ...))`) where few parents are visible. A computed `public_field` in a small own-record boolean subset (S0, which covers the bundled `documents.open_to_all`) is compiled; everything else is `Residual` and uses `OverFetch`. D79 hands this record the `Residual` form, `Pred::Member`, and a vector file that the compiler must also pass; `find_in` is D76's. *7. Transactions, timeouts, plan classification.* Every `query` and `scan_query` runs in one `read_tx` (repeatable read, read only, a normal lease) with `SET LOCAL statement_timeout` for its class and `SET LOCAL plan_cache_mode = force_custom_plan`; only `SET LOCAL` is used, so it is safe behind a pooler in transaction mode. For a model of more than 20,000 rows one `EXPLAIN (GENERIC_PLAN, FORMAT JSON)` is cached per statement shape and size bucket (no values by construction); a scan that uses only the base indexes with a filter, or a big sort, runs with a **3 s** statement timeout. SQLSTATE 57014 on a `query` returns `storage_timeout` (503 with `Retry-After`, D74's `Fault::Timeout` and thread note, a message that asks to narrow the filter or have an administrator index the named fields) and writes a `query_timeout` event; an unindexed query that takes more than 1 s writes a `slow_query` event. *8. The events are a model, not a bus.* The repository has no event bus, so `storage.slow_query` becomes an append-only read-only admin model `storage_event` (the pattern of `security_event`): model, the names of the columns used in filter, order and rule (never values), elapsed ms, rows, a hash of the shape and the generic plan truncated to 4 KB; at most one per shape per ten minutes and 200 a day; a failure to write is logged and never fails the query. It reaches REST, MCP, CLI and a Customize page with no new command. *9. Errors and settings.* SQLSTATE class 22 gives `Ok(None)` and the caller runs the oracle (counted as `obop_query_fallback_total{reason="data_exception"}`; with `OBOP_QUERY_STRICT=1`, which the tests set, it is an error so the differential test fails on it); any other server error becomes "query failed" with the SQLSTATE and the first 200 characters of the message logged and never returned (a PostgreSQL message can quote data). `OBOP_QUERY_PUSHDOWN=on|off|shadow`; `OBOP_QUERY_CANDIDATE_CAP` (default 20,000, 0 means unbounded) drives the candidate cap here and the refusal of an unbounded list in D78; `OBOP_QUERY_SHADOW_PERCENT` samples shadow mode, which runs the oracle too (whole-model reads, for staging and canaries) and logs the model and the first differing id, never values. The default is `on` once the 10,000-query differential run passes (Q50). *10. Proof.* An exhaustive differential property test (pushdown against the oracle on nine kinds of principal, with a hand-written SplitMix64 generator, 1,500 queries per principal in CI and 10,000 for the acceptance run, 4 percent of the stored values dirty, a shrinker), metamorphic checks (pages concatenate to the unpaged result, a total does not depend on the page, an added condition never adds a row), a mutant test that must catch thirteen deliberate mistakes within 2,000 queries, and a coverage guard that fails when fewer than 95 percent of the queries over supported kinds take `Push` or `OverFetch` (so an all-fallback run cannot pass). *11. Accepted differences* between the database's order and `icu4x` (version 2.3.1 in `Cargo.lock`), to be recorded as PROPOSED in `crates/kernel/obop-storage/tests/accepted_differences.json` with the versions and the counts (the file does not exist yet), and to be pinned by the test `accepted_differences_are_current`, which will fail when any of it changes: AD-1 Han (the CJK ideographs, radicals, circled and parenthesised ideograph symbols, Hangzhou numerals and the Tangut supplement order differently; about 57,000 code points); AD-2 code points that are unassigned in the server's ICU; AD-3 the lower-casing used by `contains` (28 code points, U+A7CE, U+A7D2, U+A7D4 and U+16EA0 to U+16EB8, which are Unicode 17 additions that a server on Unicode 16 does not know); AD-4 any other ICU or CLDR skew, which the probe and the golden test detect. Not differences but limits: a tag outside the set, or a server without ICU, takes `Finish` (exact, capped). *12. Behaviour changes*, all listed here: the `shared_with` list is split on eight ASCII separators and not trimmed (live; it changes who matches a list, Q49); NUL is refused in a text filter value on every backend (live); the access cache key includes the hops (live); a stored datetime counts only in its canonical form (live, an amendment of D72); the aggregate on a model that follows its parent no longer hangs (live); a candidate set above the cap is an error instead of an unbounded read of memory; a query stopped after 3 s answers 503 `storage_timeout`. *13. Amendments.* D49 and docs/MODULES.md 4.1 reject pushing predicates into JSONB and say aggregates are not pushed down; this record supersedes that for PostgreSQL only, answering the risk D49 names (a wrong whitelist exposes data) with default-deny, the differential test, shadow mode and the switch, and amends both texts when the code is built. The sentence in D58 that "aggregates and computed totals ignore the rules" is stale: `crud::search` already passes the visibility test into the aggregate (the test `aggregates_count_only_the_records_the_caller_may_see` says so), so aggregates see visible rows only; child aggregates inside computed fields (D57, for example `sum(lines.amount)`) still ignore the rules. *14. The index contract for D78.* Indexes are non-partial and composite, `(model, (expr), ...)` through `keyexpr`, so that the planner has statistics (plan K7); partial indexes only for a manifest `where`, GIN member indexes and trigram indexes per model; a test (`pg_explain_uses_index_for_indexed_filters`) builds them through `index_expr` and asserts the plan.

**Threat model.** Of the defences below only these exist today: the refusal of NUL in a text filter value, the guarded key expressions (no cast can raise on a stored value) and their parity proof. Every other mitigation is the design of the packages that are not built, and every test named here is a test of those packages, which do not exist yet (see "Acceptance tests"). *Assets:* what each principal may see (a wrong compile exposes or hides rows), the database against injection, the existence of fields a caller may not read, availability, the correctness of text order. *A wrong query exposes rows:* the rule compiler is to be an exact mirror of `Gate::grants`, default-deny (anything it cannot compile is `Residual` and is re-checked by the gate), tested against the gate on 100,000 random records and rule shapes (`rule_compile_matches_gate`) and against the whole oracle by the differential test, with a kill switch and a shadow mode that compares in production-like data; actor facts are to live only in the SQL text or its parameters, there is no session state and no row-level security, and no cache is keyed without the full `Actor`. *Injection and hostile values:* text reaches SQL only as validated identifiers, enum-derived fragments, constants and `$n` (200 hostile operand sets, including 1 MB strings, give one identical statement text; no operand bytes appear in the text); NUL is refused at parse; no cast can raise on any JSON value (nested guards, a generator in which 4 percent of values are dirty). *Unreadable field used to probe:* filter and order columns are a subset of the caller's usable columns (`query_guard` fails before storage with a text identical to an unknown field; the IR test `ir_columns_are_readable`), and rule columns appear only in the rule. *Existence oracle:* an invisible record answers "not found" before any guard runs, as D58 says. *Slow-query denial:* the 3 s rule, the candidate cap, the pool limits of D74, `Retry-After`; not bounded: a principal who can run many cheap queries, which D74 states. *ICU skew:* the probe at connect, the metric and the golden test against the CI server (all to be built); the failing tag degrades to `Finish`. *Events leaking data:* an event holds names and a hash, never values; the plan is generic. *Mixed versions:* specs are compiled per request from the live registry and nothing is cached across processes, collations are additive.

**Acceptance tests (named).** *On main today:* `expression_texts_are_pinned`, `expressions_are_golden_for_the_simple_slots`, `names_and_aliases_are_validated_and_values_never_enter`, `the_member_class_is_the_constant`, `member_tokens_split_on_eight_ascii_separators_and_trim_nothing`, `like_patterns_escape_the_three_special_characters`, `one_writer_lint` and `the_lint_finds_what_it_is_meant_to_find` (`obop-storage/src/keyexpr.rs`); `keyexpr_parity_on_postgresql`, `the_slots_cover_every_field_kind_the_parity_test_checks` and `the_parity_check_finds_the_expressions_that_were_wrong_before` (`obop-commands/src/keyexpr_parity.rs`; the first already runs every slot in a second session with non-default `DateStyle`, `TimeZone`, `standard_conforming_strings` and `extra_float_digits`, which is what `pg_expr_parity_<kind>` of the design was for); `the_cache_keeps_one_answer_per_depth_and_the_order_of_questions_changes_nothing` (`engine/access.rs`); `a_nul_in_a_filter_value_is_refused_on_every_backend` and `an_aggregate_over_a_model_that_follows_its_parent_returns_and_counts_only_visible_records` (`engine_tests.rs`, the second on memory, SQLite and, with `OBOP_TEST_POSTGRES_URL`, PostgreSQL, with a 20 s deadline that the old code missed). *Of the work packages that are not built (the tests do not exist yet):* no database: `ident_accepts_only_registry_names`, `collation_tags_are_a_closed_set`, `emit_golden_<case>` for each operator and shape, `sql_text_is_value_independent`, `no_operand_bytes_in_sql`, `count_params_are_a_prefix_of_select_params`, `plan_is_unindexed_on_canned_plans`; PostgreSQL: `pg_collations_installed_and_probe_passes`, `pg_lower_matches_rust_to_lowercase`, `pg_statement_timeout_does_not_leak_to_the_next_statement`, `pg_unindexed_large_model_times_out_and_records_event`, `pg_explain_uses_index_for_indexed_filters`, `pg_parent_first_equals_exists`, `pg_find_uses_the_unique_index`, `pg_find_in_matches_find`, `pg_scan_query_is_one_snapshot`, `pg_class22_falls_back_unless_strict`, `shared_contract_find_in` (all three backends); commands: `pushdown_differential` (the acceptance test), `pushdown_metamorphic`, `pushdown_mutants_are_caught`, `pushdown_coverage_guard`, `rule_compile_matches_gate`, `unreadable_field_error_parity`, `referrers_message_identical`, `unique_check_identical`, `aggregate_input_identical`, `computed_children_preload_reads_only_the_page`, `overfetch_bounds`, `finish_bounds`, `shadow_mode_counts_a_mismatch`, `kill_switch_off_uses_the_oracle`, `oracle_is_backend_independent`; collation: `collation_fixtures_json_through_pushdown`, `collation_random_corpus_matches_icu4x` (eight tags, ascending and descending, 5,000 strings each, Han excluded, 0 differences), the nightly `collation_single_code_point_differential_is_pinned` and `accepted_differences_are_current`; registry and packages: `pushable_bool_accepts_only_s0` and `manifest_warns_on_unpushable_public_field`; ignored scale tests `bench_pushdown_scale` (10,000, 100,000 and 1,000,000 rows, twelve queries per kind of principal, p50 and p95, thresholds to be set in this record after the first run) and `rss_1000_pages`.

**Why.** Today a list or search with a filter, a sort or a page reads the whole model into memory and filters it there (D49 chose that on purpose while the data was small), and a row rule is applied record by record by `Gate`. That is the cost class of a demo, and the hosted layout puts many instances on one PostgreSQL. The risk that D49 named is real: a database filter that disagrees with the engine exposes or hides records. The answer is to keep the engine's evaluator as the oracle, compile the rules into the same statement, default to deny, and prove the equality with a differential test on hostile data, with a switch back. The text order has to be proven too, because a user sees it: it was measured, not assumed.

**Rejected.** *Putting the grammar and the policy into storage:* storage would learn the registry and the actor. *A non-deterministic collation plus a `"C"` second key* (two of the three designs): it orders the same in a `SELECT ... ORDER BY` but PostgreSQL drops the second key inside an aggregate `ORDER BY` (measured: `string_agg(s, '|' ORDER BY s COLLATE nd, s COLLATE "C")` returned insertion order for ties), which would bite the aggregation requirement. *"Text never pushes down"* (D78's first design, withdrawn by plan K4). *SQL helper functions:* function DDL, version skew and an owner job at every release. *Row-level security as the row rule:* it would need one role or one session setting per principal and would not cover `via` chains and `shared_with` as the oracle does. *A single `OBOP_QUERY_PUSHDOWN` that is on without proof:* the default waits for the differential run (Q50). *Pushing down on SQLite:* SQLite indexes serve `find` only. *Raising on dirty data:* a bare cast raises on `{"f":"x"}`; every expression is guarded and a class 22 error falls back to the oracle. *A queryable `storage.explain` command:* it would need REST, MCP, CLI and UI; the event model carries the plan instead.

**Consequences.** *Behaviour changes* are listed in Decision 12. *Costs.* One more log model and eight collations per database; the shadow mode reads whole models; a query of a model of more than 20,000 rows costs one cached `EXPLAIN` per shape. *Measured on PostgreSQL 17.11 (ICU 16.0) in the design, not in the tree:* deterministic `<tag>-u-kn-ks-level1` collations equal `icu4x` 2.3.1 on 34,446 random strings for eight tags and both directions with 0 differences (region tags equal the language; `de-u-co-phonebk` with 4,954 lines and `fi-u-co-trad` with 1,505 do not, so any script, variant or extension is unmapped); of all 1,112,061 single code points 61,785 sit in different relative positions (56,573 `Lo`, 4,803 unassigned in ICU 16.0, 409 Han-derived symbols and numerals; among assigned code points nothing else differs); `lower()` against Rust `to_lowercase()` differs on 28 code points; a partial expression index gets no planner statistics, and with 300,000 tasks `expr = 'p77' ORDER BY seq LIMIT 50` took 1,250 ms against 0.11 ms with a non-partial `(model, expr)` index, which is why D78 builds those; a principal who sees 5 percent of 20,000 parents over 300,000 children: `EXISTS` 1.8 s, a materialised CTE 0.78 s, `fk = ANY(ARRAY(...))` 139 ms. *D79* hands `Residual` and the vectors to this record. *The aggregation requirement* (OBR-DAT-010) later pushes `GROUP BY`; only the aggregate input is pushed here.

**Not delivered, and why.** The typed-layout emitter (OBR-DAT-011); declared and automatic indexes and keyset pagination (D78: only their contract is here); `GROUP BY` pushdown (010); SQL for computed fields beyond S0 (arithmetic, `if`, `concat`, child aggregates stay `Residual` or `Finish`); a queryable `storage.explain`; pushdown on SQLite; a single-statement `get` for `via` rules and batched reference checks (an optional work package); the whole-model reads that are not list, search, get, uniqueness, reference, referrer or aggregate: the public site's `find_by`, the token lookup and `server_records` (`public.rs`), the sequence backfill (`seq.rs`), the idempotency scan (`bulk.rs`); the retention of `storage_event` (Q51; 90 days through the purge command is the proposal, not built); everything under "Built today, and not built".

**Not tried.** PgBouncer (the design uses `SET LOCAL` and explicit transactions only), managed PostgreSQL without ICU, ICU versions other than 16.0, a server whose ICU is newer than `icu4x` 2.3.1, tables of 1,000,000 rows (the numbers above are 300,000 children and 20,000 parents), and the interplay with the D72 kinds and the D74 pool beyond the parity test. The design measured on PostgreSQL 17.11; the merged parity test ran in CI on PostgreSQL 18 and not on 17 (D44 says 17 or 18; the collation work has not been run on 18).

**Evidence.** The preparation package (pull request 5, head 506bb28) went through an adversarial review (9 confirmed findings, none high, all fixed or documented) and passed GitHub Actions rust (Linux, PostgreSQL 18), web, e2e and lockfiles; locally cargo fmt, clippy `-D warnings` and the workspace tests with PostgreSQL 18 passed, with one run under heavy machine load that had 14 `storage_timeout` failures that pass when rerun alone. The deadlock fix is its own merge. The measurements in Consequences are the designer's, taken on one machine against PostgreSQL 17.11 in a scratch database; they were not repeated for this record.

#### D77 Database-enforced immutability and PostgreSQL role separation
**Status** PROPOSED (owner-directed in chat, 2026-10-25/26, as part of item 1 "storage foundations", second wave; requirements OBR-DAT-022 and 023; not yet ratified as binding). **Reversibility** two-way door for the code: every switch is off by default, memory and SQLite behave as before, an old binary runs on the new schema because the triggers do nothing without guard rows, and rollback of a split is switching the database URL back. One-way doors once an instance holds data in them: the stored guard `Spec` (JSON, version 1, with its hash), a guard that protects frozen records (it can then be replaced only by the database owner), the three roles of a hosted instance, and the ledger step that installs the guards. None of that exists today. **Owner action** yes, two questions (Q52: the CLI-only exemption entries and the names of the production switches; Q53: whether a guarded model may refuse every online write of the kinds listed in Consequences); neither blocks the work packages, and no exemption is taken until the owner approves it. The operator's later action, at deployment time and not before: `obop migrate run` as the owner, then (optionally) `obop storage split-roles`, `OBOP_PRODUCTION=1`.

**Source.** The requirements are OBR-DAT-022 (role separation: an owner role, a runtime role without DDL, a backup role) and 023 (immutability enforced by the database) of the data-storage requirements of a project that builds on obop (kept outside this repository). Where the requirement text contradicts the repository, the repository wins: "triggers generated from the declarations" is met by one generic trigger driven by the declared spec rows (a `CREATE TRIGGER` per model would need DDL and a `SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE` lock on `obop_record` at every package install); "the installer creates indexes under the owner role" cannot hold for the runtime installs of D45, so uniqueness uses a definer door; "two servers can race" (D48) is stale after D59, so the dispatcher's write lock covers guarded models (`has_rules()` becomes true for them) and the compare-and-set and the trigger are defence in depth; "the full test suite and the restore drill pass with the runtime role" cannot hold, because a restore writes frozen rows and package tests create databases, so the storage contract, the engine guard tests, a mini end-to-end run and the restore drill (bootstrap and restore as owner, serve as runtime) stand for it; there is no schema called `obop`, everything is in `public` (or the `{schema}` of the connection, plan K23); seeds and direct writes bypass the command layer (D44, D47, D48), which on PostgreSQL the trigger covers, so a seed of a guarded model must start in its initial state. The rulings of the wave-2 integration plan (see D75 Source; written "plan K..", not the rows of docs/ISSUES.md) are followed (plan K1, K2, K12, K13, K14, K15, K19, K20, K21, K23, K24, K26). Read with D44, D45, D48, D53, D57, D58, D59, D73, D74 and D75.

**Built today, and not built.** *Built* (packages on main): (1) the types `Guard`, `StateSet`, `Exit` and `LockDate` in `obop-registry/src/ext.rs`, held by `ModelDef.ext.guard`; nothing reads or enforces them and the manifest has no `[model.guard]` key. (2) `obop-storage/src/refusal.rs`: the typed refusal of the database's own guards (`Refusal`, `RefusalCode`; SQLSTATE `OBG01` for a refused change and `OBG02` for a refused guard change), accepted only when the whole error text is one, so a value echoed into another error cannot make a refusal; `pgpool::describe` maps the two SQLSTATEs to it and an unrelated server error keeps its old text. (3) `schema.rs`: the base schema and the byte store's table as parts with a `{schema}` placeholder, `ensure`, `install_all`, `owner_install` and a fallible `inspect`; and in `obop-storage/src/lib.rs`: `Ddl::{IfMissing, Never}`, `OpenOptions`, `open_options` and `set_production` with the rule of `production_from`. A serving role that may not create tables opens an installed database; one that may not create tables and finds no schema is told to create it once with a role that may (`schema::owner_install`, a Rust function; no command installs the schema as the owner yet, ISSUES K21 (b)). **`OBOP_PRODUCTION` is not read by any code and `set_production` has no caller** (ISSUES K21 (b)). (4) The tickets: `WriteCtx.actions` and the transaction-local setting `obop.ctx` (`set_config(..., true)`, only `actions` serialised), visible to the database inside the transaction and nowhere else (tested through the pool). (5) The rows `immutable_record`, `state_conflict` and `guard_change_refused` (409) in the error table of `obop_commands::errors`, with no producer. (6) The reserved model name `data_guard`, `Tracked` forwarding (a test fails when a wrapper forgets a method), `testing::TestDb` (a scratch database per test). (7) From D73: the migration ledger, the DDL cost classifier, the policy and the window rule that this record's DDL component uses. *Not built:* the pure `Spec` and its check (the design calls the module `obop_storage::guard`, a name the tree already uses for D74's request guard; the package chooses another), the manifest key and the gate rules, the compiler, the tables `obop_guard` and `obop_guard_log`, the trigger and the definer functions (`obop_guard_register`, `obop_ensure_unique`, the protect functions), the roles and `grants_sql`, `split-roles`, `check-roles`, `guards ...`, `modules tamper`, `serve --production`, `--owner-db`, the `data_guard` commands, the Integrity page, the Protection section of the install plan, the built-in guards, the ledger step, the control plane's three roles, and the DDL component (`ddl_run.rs`). **No guard is enforced anywhere today**, on any backend.

**Decision.** *1. A guard is a row and one generic trigger enforces it.* `[model.guard]` in a package manifest (and `ModelDef.ext.guard` in a Rust module) compiles to one JSON `Spec` per model, canonical (compact `serde_json`, sorted keys, optional parts omitted, edges sorted by field and action), hash = SHA-256 of that text. The same `Spec` is interpreted twice: by a pure Rust `check` called from `Unit` on every backend (this is `guard_level = command_only` on memory and SQLite) and by ONE generic plpgsql trigger function on `obop_record` that reads the spec from `obop_guard` (PostgreSQL). Registration is an INSERT through the narrow `SECURITY DEFINER` function `obop_guard_register`, so a runtime package install needs no DDL and no owner credential. The manifest keys, each refused by the gate with a message that names `model.guard.<key>`: `immutable_when = {field, values}`, `mutable_fields`, `exit_to = [{to, action, may_change}]`, `no_delete_when = {field, values}`, `lock_date = {field, setting}`, `children_of` (a ref field of this model whose target model is guarded; at most four hops, no cycle) and `append_only` (alone). Gate rules: at least one key; `immutable_when.field` is a governed `select`, its values are options and none is the first option (a record is born unfrozen); each `exit_to.action` is a declared transition of this model with the same `to` and an explicit `from`, `may_change` fields are stored fields, and every transition on the immutable field that starts in an immutable state or has no `from` is listed (completeness); `mutable_fields` are stored fields only (not computed, sequence, governed, the lock field or the `children_of` field) and **every field that `obop secrets rotate` re-seals (the inventory `obop_keys::reseal::FIELDS`) and that a guarded model holds must be listed**, because the rotation writes the re-sealed text into the record and would abort its batch on a frozen row (no model of that inventory is guarded today; a `secret`-class field of a package model is masked and denied, not sealed, and the rotation does not read data classes or touch it, so the gate does not require it to be listed; the design's first rule, "every `secret` field", rested on a wrong picture of the rotation and is withdrawn); `lock_date.field` has kind `date` (a datetime is refused, because its UTC date can differ from the date in the platform's zone) and `setting` matches `^[a-z][a-z0-9_]*(\.[a-z][a-z0-9_]*){0,3}$`; a warning for an action that moves a record into an immutable state and then creates a child of it, which fails at run time. *2. Semantics.* A record is frozen when its state field is in `immutable_when.values`, or its lock-date field is on or before the lock date (the setting `obop_setting/instance`, read `FOR SHARE`; a value that is set but is not `YYYY-MM-DD` raises), or its parent exists and is frozen (the parent row read `FOR SHARE`; a depth over four counts as frozen). INSERT must carry the initial value of each governed field, the parent must not be frozen, the date must not be on or before the lock date. DELETE is refused by `append_only`, on a frozen record, and in a `no_delete_when` state. UPDATE is refused by `append_only`; the system keys are ignored; a change of a governed field needs a declared edge whose `from` matches (or is null) and whose action has a ticket, and on a frozen record only an exit edge; a change of another field on a frozen record is allowed only for `mutable_fields` and the `may_change` of the used edge; the new parent must not be frozen; a new date must be after the lock date. Only the old row decides frozenness, so a post that also edits fields is fine. A violation raises SQLSTATE `OBG01` with the message `immutable_record` and a DETAIL of names only (model, id, rule, field names, never values; a state value only when it is a string of at most 40 characters). The rules are `initial`, `transition`, `immutable_when`, `lock_date`, `children_of`, `no_delete_when`, `append_only`, `identity` and `truncate`. *3. Transitions and tickets.* `Unit::transition` queues a compare-and-set `Op::Patch` and records a ticket `(model, id, action)`. `Backend::apply_ctx` sends the tickets as the transaction-local `obop.ctx`, and the trigger accepts a change of a governed field only along a declared edge whose action has a ticket for that record. This **stops defects and unaudited paths, not a holder of the runtime credentials**, who can forge a ticket for a declared edge; the report says so in `guard_form = document_ticket`, and the typed layout (a column privilege plus a definer transition function) and HMAC tickets are not built. They still cannot use an undeclared edge, another record's ticket, skip `from`, edit a non-mutable field, delete, truncate, change identity or weaken the guard of a model that holds frozen records. *4. Trigger order and identity.* `obop_00_ident` first, the names `obop_10_` to `obop_89_` reserved, then `zzz_obop_guard_*` last, so that the guard validates the final row image that no other trigger can still change (plan K24; D75's triggers are on `obop_record_change`, not on `obop_record`). A guarded record can never change model or id (`obop_00_ident`, for guarded models only). A runtime role may register a new guard and replace a guard while no record of the model is frozen under the current one; otherwise `OBG02`, and only the owner can replace it (`obop storage guards replace`). *5. Roles.* The existing database role stays the **owner** (`obop_<slug>`: all DDL, migrations, restore, guard replacement), a new `obop_<slug>_rt` is the **runtime** role (DML only; on the history and other append-only tables SELECT and INSERT only), and an optional `obop_<slug>_ro` is the **backup** role (SELECT only, `default_transaction_read_only = on`); all `LOGIN NOSUPERUSER NOCREATEDB NOCREATEROLE NOREPLICATION NOBYPASSRLS`; nothing is re-owned. The target is that the serving path issues no DDL: `Pg::connect_options` asks the catalogue (`schema::ensure`) and creates the base schema only under `Ddl::IfMissing`; once `serve --production` sets `Ddl::Never` and the definer door replaces the runtime `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS` of `Pg::ensure_unique`, nothing on the serving path creates an object. **Today it still does:** `Ddl::for_process()` is `IfMissing` in every process, because `set_production` has no caller, and `ensure_unique` runs that statement at runtime. The only two things the runtime role may ask the owner side to do are the two definer doors with regex-validated arguments, `obop_guard_register` and `obop_ensure_unique` (the owner-owned unique index of D57; the existing `obop_uq_*` indexes stay as they are). Functions pin `search_path = pg_catalog, pg_temp` and qualify names; TEMP is revoked. *6. Switches and the production start-up.* The owner URL is `OBOP_DATABASE_OWNER_URL` or `--owner-db` (plan K12), read only by `migrate`, `storage`, `restore`, `modules tamper` and `platform`; `serve` and `mcp` ignore it and, with `--production`, refuse to start when it is set. `OBOP_PRODUCTION=1` or `--production` applies to every command that opens the app, not to operator commands (which open the store with `IfMissing` themselves). The production start-up runs in order: `Ddl::Never` (a missing schema is an error that names the command); an owner URL in the environment is refused; `Backend::role_report` with any error finding exits 1 and prints code, object and the privilege found (for example `runtime_owns_relation obop_record`); a pending migration with DDL and no owner URL is refused with the command; failures to register a package's guards exit 1; then one line `database: postgres; roles: split; guard_level: trigger_separated; guard_form: document_ticket`. The levels are `command_only` (memory, SQLite), `trigger_same_role` (PostgreSQL, one role: the trigger stops accidents, the role could drop it) and `trigger_separated`. *7. The guards step and DDL.* Ledger step **0003 `guards_v1`** (plan K15; the schema part `guards`; it runs as the same role on a single-role database and as the owner via `obop migrate run --owner-db` on a split one; PostgreSQL's auto-migrate is off by default, so `serve` refuses with the existing "needs 1 migration step" message until it is run; every hosted instance sets `OBOP_AUTO_MIGRATE=1` (docs/DEPLOY.md), and there a single-role instance runs step 0003 itself after the verified backup, while a split instance still needs `obop migrate run --owner-db`); DDL runs through one component (`ddl_run.rs`), the single DDL executor that D73 left to this record. The comparison with the live catalogue (indexes, triggers) that D73's `migrate verify` leaves to this record is one inspector, `schema::inspect`, with the finding codes of D77 and D78, shared by `Migrator::verify`, the doctor, `check-roles` and `storage indexes` (plan K26); today `inspect` covers the base parts only and returns a result that can fail instead of a bare list. A fresh database is bootstrapped by `install_all` under `IfMissing`. *8. Surfaces.* `data_guard.report` and `data_guard.verify` (admin read, no approval) on REST, MCP, CLI and a Customize page `Integrity`; the 409 body is `{error, code, model, record, rule, fields, actions, mutable, hint}` with `code` one of `immutable_record`, `state_conflict`, `guard_change_refused` and never a value; an invisible record answers "not found" before any guard runs (D58), so there is no existence oracle; `/api/meta` gains `guard`; the install plan gains a Protection section (rules, level, form, frozen records now, the number of negative tests, a blocker when a package upgrade would change a guard that protects frozen records, and one when a guarded package is installed on PostgreSQL without the guards step); the record panel locks the fields by state. CLI: `obop storage check-roles`, `grants`, `split-roles`, `guards list|verify|replace|remove`, `obop modules tamper FILE`, `obop migrate run --owner-db`, `obop restore --owner-db`, `obop serve --production`, `obop platform split-roles SLUG`. **Exemption proposal (Q52):** `storage grants`, `storage split-roles`, `storage guards replace|remove`, `modules tamper` and `platform split-roles` are CLI-only (they lack the UI, REST and MCP; docs/spec/cli.md 1.1 lists them as a proposed group) because they need credentials the web server must never hold; everything else is reachable in the UI through `data_guard` and the plan dialog. Nothing is exempt until the owner approves it. *9. Built-in guards,* only after the verification of O7 (the full PostgreSQL suite shows that nothing updates `security_event` or rewrites an applied `obop_migration` row): `security_event` as `append_only` and the ledger `obop_migration` (a status may move from `partial` to `applied` only; applied, failed and adopted rows are immutable; a `partial` row cannot be deleted), adjusted to D73's final writes. *10. Restore.* The owner connection runs `ALTER TABLE obop_record DISABLE TRIGGER USER` inside the import transaction only (`schema::import_begin` and `import_end`, plan K20); no maintenance setting exists to forge. The runtime role may restore only into a schema whose `obop_guard` is empty and without a wipe, and the guards are registered again at the next start from the restored package records. `restore --force` over guarded rows needs the owner. *11. Hosted instances.* The control plane creates the three roles and the database, bootstraps and stamps as owner, sets `OBOP_PRODUCTION=1`, and passes the runtime URL to the service and never the owner or the backup URL; the owner password is not stored and is rotated when a later DDL step needs it (refused for an instance that is not split yet); a slug whose `_rt` role name exceeds 63 bytes is refused; `platform split-roles SLUG` converts an existing instance and is never automatic. Whether Sliplane can change the environment of a running service is unverified, so applying the two changes is manual until it is shown. *12. Negative tests derive from the spec.* `Spec::negative_tests` returns named cases from the spec alone (edit a frozen field, edit a mutable field, delete a frozen record, a state jump, an edge without a ticket, a ticket for another record, a non-initial insert, a no-delete state, the lock-date cases, the child cases, append-only, an identity change, truncate); the install plan prints their number and `obop modules tamper FILE` runs them as the runtime role against scratch models and refuses when the database holds records of the named models or the lock setting is non-empty.

**Threat model.** *Assets:* records that must not change once posted or locked, the append-only logs (`security_event`, the history of D75, the ledger), the schema and the guard rows, the database credentials, the restore path. *A holder of the runtime credentials:* as the runtime role, DROP TABLE and TRIGGER, TRUNCATE, `DISABLE TRIGGER ALL` or by name, CREATE TABLE, INDEX, SCHEMA or TRIGGER, `CREATE OR REPLACE` or `ALTER FUNCTION`, UPDATE or DELETE of `obop_guard`, INSERT into `obop_guard_log`, `SET ROLE` to the owner, `session_replication_role`, `COPY ... PROGRAM`, ALTER OWNER and ADD COLUMN all fail with 42501 when the roles are split (tested as a real login, not as `SET ROLE` from a superuser session, which is a false test); `GRANT ... TO self` returns OK with a warning and grants nothing, so the test asserts the ACL. With one role (`trigger_same_role`) the privilege cases succeed and the tests say so, so that the level is honest. *Forged tickets:* Decision 3. *A ticket that leaks to the next caller through the pool or PgBouncer:* only `set_config(..., true)` is used, inside the transaction, and a test reads `current_setting('obop.ctx', true)` on every pooled connection; statement-level pooling is unsupported. *Shadowing:* a temp table named `obop_guard` or `SET search_path = pg_temp, ...` do not defeat the guard (functions pin the path and qualify names; TEMP is revoked). *Moving a record in or out:* a change of model or id of a guarded record is refused. *Born-frozen:* an INSERT must be initial and `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` onto a frozen row fires the UPDATE trigger. *Two processes post one document:* `Op::Patch` compare-and-set under the row lock lets one win; the loser's whole unit (counter, effects) rolls back and it answers 409 `state_conflict`, so a gapless number stays gapless. *A child insert races the parent's post:* the trigger reads the parent `FOR SHARE`, so either the child exists before the post commits or the insert fails. *The owner drops or disables a trigger:* detected, not prevented: `trigger_missing`, `trigger_disabled`, `function_drift` in `check-roles`, `data_guard.verify` and the production start-up. *A bad guard release that blocks all writes of a guarded model:* the owner reinstalls with `obop migrate run` or restores; no suspend switch is built, because it would be a bypass. *Restore as a bypass:* only the owner path or an empty `obop_guard`. *GDPR erasure of a frozen record:* refused with 409 (interim: list the personal fields in `mutable_fields`; the gate warns when a personal field of a guarded model is not listed; an erasable ticket kind is a follow-up and a legal question). *The control plane:* the owner and backup URLs never reach the service's environment (an assertion in the Sliplane tests). *Not bounded:* `LOCK TABLE` and long locks by the runtime role (a denial of service any DML role can cause), and a holder of the owner credentials.

**Acceptance tests (named).** *On main today* (the seams, `obop-storage/src/seams_tests.rs` and `refusal.rs`): `a_write_the_databases_guard_refuses_comes_back_as_a_typed_refusal_and_not_as_a_fault`, `other_server_errors_keep_their_old_text`, `the_tickets_of_a_write_are_visible_to_the_database_in_its_transaction_and_only_there`, `ensure_creates_what_is_missing_only_when_allowed`, `a_missing_object_is_reported_and_put_back`, `a_serving_role_that_may_not_create_tables_opens_an_installed_database`, `a_role_that_may_not_create_tables_is_told_how_to_install_the_schema`, `processes_that_start_together_on_an_empty_database_all_start`, `a_catalogue_query_that_fails_is_an_error_and_never_a_missing_part`, `the_byte_stores_table_is_created_or_required_like_the_rest_of_the_schema`, `production_is_a_flag_or_the_variable_set_to_one`, `a_process_that_is_not_a_production_process_creates_the_schema_whatever_the_environment_says`. *Of the work packages that are not built (the tests do not exist yet):* pure and command layer: `guard::tests::check_each_rule`, `guard_spec_golden`, `guard_command_only_<rule>` on memory and SQLite, `transition_is_compare_and_set`, `mixed_update_refused_whole`, `agent_update_and_delete_of_frozen_record_refused_without_plan`, `rules::check_guard_refusals`, `fault_wire_roundtrip`, `fault_message_has_no_values`; PostgreSQL in a database of its own: `roles_five_required_attacks` (the protected-table list comes from `inspect`, so the history table of D75 is covered unchanged), `guard_tamper_runtime_role` (about 35 statements, expecting `OBG01`, `OBG02` or `42501`, with positive controls that must pass), `roles_check_clean_after_install`, `roles_check_flags_each_drift` (one finding code each for a granted TRUNCATE, a grant to PUBLIC, a missing default ACL, a disabled trigger, a wrong trigger order, a partition with UPDATE, an owned table, membership of the owner, a superuser, BYPASSRLS, an invalid index), `connect_never_issues_no_ddl`, `ensure_unique_definer_door` (injection in a name, model or column gives 22023, duplicates 23505), `schema_install_idempotent`, `guard_register_rules`, `identity_change_refused`, `truncate_refused_with_guards`, `import_as_owner_restores_frozen_rows`, `import_as_runtime_refused_when_guards_exist`, `ctx_not_leaked_through_pool`, `append_only_partitions_protected`, `guard_oracle_rust_and_trigger_agree` (5,000 seeded random cases, the same allow or deny and the same rule from the Rust check and the trigger), `transition_race_1000_backend` (memory, SQLite and PostgreSQL), `raw_sql_transition_race_1000`, `child_insert_vs_parent_post_blocks`, `child_insert_vs_post_stress_500`, and the ignored `guard_overhead_gate` (single-row units on an unguarded model with the triggers against `DISABLE TRIGGER USER`, ratio at most 1.10, best of three, plus a bulk create of 5,000 children under one parent); dispatcher and surfaces: `guard_dispatch.rs` (1,000 trials of `invoice.post` raced with the write lock on and off: one winner, one effect row, gapless numbers), `split_roles.rs` (bootstrap as owner, serve as runtime, `serve_production_refuses_owning_role`, `serve_production_refuses_owner_url_env`, `migrate_run_uses_owner_url`, `restore_round_trip_with_guards`), the package tests (`manifest_guard_parse_and_unknown_key`, `plan_lists_guards`, `upgrade_changing_guard_with_frozen_records_is_blocked`, `uninstall_keeps_guard`, `guarded_package_refused_on_postgres_without_guards`, `migration_over_frozen_records_is_blocked`), `rest_409_immutable_shape`, `rest_409_state_conflict_shape`, `mcp_is_error_carries_code`, `openapi_documents_409`, `meta_has_guard`, `guards.spec.ts` in the browser, and for the control plane `pg_cell_creates_three_roles_and_database`, `pg_cell_failure_drops_everything`, `sliplane_env_has_production_and_no_owner_url`, `slug_too_long_refused`, `split_roles_existing_instance`.

**Why.** A "posted" invoice is immutable today because the command layer says so. Any bug, any custom handler, a seed, a script with the database URL or a package installed with a wrong rule can change it, and the server connects as a role that owns every table, so it can also drop or truncate them and remove the history that would show what happened. For books that must stand up to an auditor, a lock date, a payroll run or a ledger, "the application refuses" is not enough; "the database refuses the application's own role" is a stronger statement, and this record says exactly how far it goes: it stops defects and unaudited paths today, and with separate roles it stops a stolen runtime credential from rewriting what is frozen, though not from forging a ticket for a declared edge.

**Rejected.** *A generated trigger per model:* it needs DDL and a heavy lock at every package install. *A new crate `obop-schema` and a schema `obop_sys`:* storage would depend on it circularly. *`ENABLE ALWAYS` triggers:* they would break logical replication and disaster-recovery copies. *HMAC-signed tickets:* designed, not built; they need a key the runtime role would have to hold. *`Op::Actions`:* a `WriteCtx` parameter is what D75 needs too. *A maintenance setting for restore:* anything a setting can switch off, a caller can switch off; the owner disables the triggers in the import transaction. *Re-owning the objects, or renaming the role,* to make the old role the runtime role: nothing is re-owned, rollback is switching the URL back, and the control plane's stored credentials stay valid. *An insert-only registration rule* (every guard change an owner job): the runtime role may register and replace while nothing is frozen. *An identity trigger on every model:* it would touch unguarded writers for no gain. *Triggers that fire before D75's:* the guard must see the final row image. *A suspend switch for a bad guard release:* it is a bypass. *Per-model SQLite triggers:* SQLite stays `command_only` (D44). *A rule that every `secret`-class field of a guarded model be listed in `mutable_fields`* (the design's first form): `secrets rotate` re-seals a fixed inventory and never reads data classes, so the rule had no cause for package models; the gate requires listing only the inventoried fields a guarded model holds.

**Consequences.** *Overhead.* Measured on a loaded shared host, noisy: a bulk update costs 11.6 to 12.4 microseconds per row without the triggers, and 4 to 11 more with the `WHEN` form of the trigger; the 10 percent gate is a ratio test that has not been run. A hot parent with thousands of children costs `FOR SHARE` multixact churn; a bulk create of 5,000 children is measured in the perf test and its ceiling is stated in this record afterwards. *Online writes that a frozen record then refuses* (all end in 409, all intended; Q53; the last item is not about a frozen record but is refused under split roles in the same way): the sequence backfill, `secrets rotate` of an inventoried sealed field that a guarded model holds and does not list in `mutable_fields` (none today), package `[[migration]]` data steps (a blocker in the install plan), GDPR erasure of personal fields, edits of records on or before the lock date, and the re-wrap of sealed history entries by `secrets rotate` (D75: an update of append-only history rows that the runtime role, which has SELECT and INSERT only there, cannot make; it needs the owner role or a definer function of its own, decided with D75's sealed-history package). The owner path for the frozen-record writes is `guards replace` and restore. *Upgrading.* Where auto-migrate is off, the new image's `serve` refuses to start on PostgreSQL until `obop migrate run` (a hosted instance migrates itself, a split one needs the owner command); migrate-then-swap rolling upgrades for DDL steps on split hosted instances are not built, so the rolling upgrade stops at the first instance that does not come up. An old binary on the new schema works; a package with `[model.guard]` does not parse in an old binary (`deny_unknown_fields`, and `requires_core` carries the minimum). *With D75.* The history table is protected by the same functions (`obop_protect_table(..., 'insert_only')` after each partition it creates, called only `IF to_regprocedure(...) IS NOT NULL`, and `grants_sql` repairs partitions afterwards, so either order of merging works); with the roles split, D75's append-only trigger becomes a boundary. *Docs.* docs/DEPLOY.md (roles, `--production`, the owner URL), docs/RUNBOOK-RECOVERY.md and docs/MODULES.md will say what is built.

**Not delivered, and why.** The typed-layout form (a column `REVOKE UPDATE` plus a definer transition function; OBR-DAT-011) and HMAC tickets; SQLite triggers (D44); a database-enforced monotonic lock date (lowering the date reopens a period and is only audited); datetime lock fields; an erasable-field ticket kind for GDPR erasure of frozen records; owner-role data migrations over frozen rows; the sequence backfill and package data steps on frozen rows (blocked, not enabled); the re-wrap of sealed history under split roles (D75); a hash chain over history (OBR-DAT-025); the replication role and an EXECUTE-only connector account (022 items 6 and 7); WAL and archiving roles (documented only); migrate-then-swap rolling upgrades for DDL steps; a suspend switch; live Sliplane runs; the full workspace test suite as the runtime role. Still to settle with D73: the exact writes of `obop_migration` for the built-in guard and whether `obop backup` writes a ledger row under the read-only role (it must then use a writer URL).

**Not tried.** The design's SQL was run by hand as a prototype on PostgreSQL 17 in a scratch database (insert, update, delete, child, lock date, append-only, identity, upsert, registration, unique index and partition scenarios, and the runtime-role attack list). **The races, the 5,000-case oracle and the overhead gate were not run.** Not tried at all: PgBouncer (transaction mode is expected to work, statement mode is unsupported), managed PostgreSQL, PostgreSQL 18 for the trigger work, several server processes, tables above a million rows (the cost of the trigger, of the `OBG02` scan and of the `CREATE TRIGGER` lock wait), and the real Sliplane cell.

**Evidence.** The seams on main (pull request 11, head afe48fc) were reviewed adversarially twice (16 findings in the first review, 4 medium: a failed schema check read as "missing", a test that hung, the byte store outside `Ddl`, the production flag; one low item after the check, ISSUES K22; what the review left open is ISSUES K21) and passed GitHub Actions rust (Linux, PostgreSQL 18), web, e2e and lockfiles; locally cargo fmt, clippy `-D warnings` and the workspace against PostgreSQL 18, 64 suites with 1,682 tests passed and none failed. The guard SQL, the roles and the attack list were run only as the designer's prototype; the files of the package are not in the tree.

#### D78 Declared and automatic indexes, keyset pagination and incremental reads
**Status** PROPOSED (owner-directed in chat, 2026-10-25/26, as part of item 1 "storage foundations", second wave; requirements OBR-DAT-008 and 009; not yet ratified as binding). **Reversibility** two-way door for almost everything: an index is derived data (dropping one changes speed and never a result; it is rebuilt from the registry), a cursor is sealed and dies after 24 hours, the manifest keys are optional (an older core refuses a manifest that uses them because of `deny_unknown_fields`, and `requires_core` carries the minimum), and a model that declares nothing behaves as before except for the limits below and the member tokenizer, which is already live (Decision 12 of D76 and "Built today"). One-way doors once clients rely on them: the member tokenizer (live), the caps (an unbounded call is refused above 20,000 records, an offset above 10,000), the default order by id of a call that pages, the names of the error codes, and the `next_since` contract. **Owner action** yes, three questions (Q54: the amendments of D49; Q55: whether `list` should also return `next`; Q56: the order of ties under a descending sort), none of which blocks starting work; Q54 to Q56 are needed before the keyset package merges. The tokenizer change is Q49 (it came with D76).

**Source.** The requirements are OBR-DAT-008 (declared and automatic indexes) and 009 (keyset pagination, totals and caps, incremental reads) of the data-storage requirements of a project that builds on obop (kept outside this repository). The design was made as the second half of the pushdown design and is recorded here separately (the wave-2 integration plan fixed the id, plan K25). Where the requirement text contradicts the repository, the repository wins: `list` stays a bare array (D49 rejected changing its shape), so `after`, `total`, `updated_since` and `include_deleted` belong to `search` and `list` refuses them with `use_search`; the unbounded default of `limit` (D49) now refuses above 20,000 records and changes nothing below it; `total = exact` means "exact up to 50,000, then the planner's estimate, flagged `total_is_estimate`" (which is how the requirement's acceptance reads), and a true big count is `search` with the aggregate `count`, which streams; "exactly once under concurrent writes" holds only for records whose order keys do not change. The rulings of the wave-2 integration plan (see D75 Source; written "plan K..", not the rows of docs/ISSUES.md) are followed: plan K3 (one writer of expression text), K4 (text order is pushed down by D76, so this record's first design, which said text never pushes down, is withdrawn), K5 (the member tokenizer is this record's), K7, K8, K9, K10, K11, K12, K15, K21 and K26. Read with D49, D57, D58, D72, D73, D74, D75, D76, D77 and D79.

**Built today, and not built.** *Built:* (1) the member tokenizer: `keyexpr::member_tokens` splits a `shared_with` list on eight ASCII separators and trims nothing, `Gate::grants` and the in-memory evaluator use it, and the `Members` slot has the same rule in SQL (`regexp_split_to_array`, index-servable by a GIN index); one constant for Rust and SQL, and it replaced the earlier rule of four separators (comma, semicolon, space, line feed) followed by a Unicode trim of every piece, so it is a behaviour change that is already live (Consequences); it is tested by `member_tokens_split_on_eight_ascii_separators_and_trim_nothing` and the parity test. (2) `keyexpr.rs`, the one writer of expression text with its `EXPR_VERSION` (2), which the index definitions will hash. (3) The types `IndexKind`, `CondOp`, `IndexCond` and `IndexDef` in `obop-registry/src/ext.rs`, held by `ModelDef.ext.indexes`; nothing reads them and the manifest has no `index` key. (4) The rows `cursor_expired` (410) and `cursor_invalid`, `cursor_key_too_long`, `offset_too_large`, `limit_required`, `use_search` (400) in the error table of `obop_commands::errors`, tested for their status (`each_row_maps_as_the_table_says`, `the_table_says_what_the_plan_says`) and without a producer. (5) The reserved model name `storage_index` and the seams that the package uses (`PgPool::read_tx`, `Engine::unit*`, `Tracked` forwarding). *Not built:* the manifest keys and the gate; automatic indexes; `IndexSpec` and its names; the builder, the reconcile worker, `index_inventory`, `index_build`, `index_drop`, `analyze`, `count_upto`, `try_lock` and `clock_ms`; the `storage_index` model and the Customize Storage tab; `obop storage indexes|index build|index prune`; the keyset arms, the cursor, the total modes, the offset cap, the refusal of an unbounded list, the default order by id for paged calls, `updated_since`, `next_since`, `include_deleted`; the `Pg::find` slot form and the delete check through `find`; `OBOP_SYNC_OVERLAP_MS`, `OBOP_INDEX_BUILD` and `OBOP_DDL_STATEMENT_TIMEOUT_MS` (no code reads them); the pager in the browser. **Today no model has a declared or automatic index, `list` and `search` page by offset exactly as before, and an unbounded call is not refused.**

**Decision.** *1. Declared indexes.* In a package manifest: `index = true` on a field (one ascending btree), and `[[model.index]]` with `fields` (one to four stored fields, `-` for descending; the id, bytewise ascending, is always appended), `kind` (`btree` by default, or `trigram` on exactly one text or long text field), `unique` (btree only, not with `where`; sugar for D57 `unique` and `unique_with`, desugared at parse time so one uniqueness path remains) and `where` (at most three conditions with the operators `eq`, `ne`, `in`, `is_null`). Caps: 12 declared per model, 60 per package, 24 per model counting the automatic ones; a declared index equal to an automatic one is not counted and not built twice. The gate refuses an unknown, computed, image or file field, a btree on long text, a duplicate column, a trigram that is not alone, and a `where` over a decimal; `where` values are typed to the kind and match `^[A-Za-z0-9 _.@:+-]{0,64}$`, and that grammar is the injection defence: DDL is built only from validated tokens, `ident()` names and quote-doubled literals. `check_json` refuses a value over 1,000 bytes in an indexed text field (a btree row above 2,704 bytes makes an INSERT fail; this also protects the D57 unique indexes). *2. Automatic indexes* (counted in 24, not in 12 or 60): a btree `(expr, id)` for every stored `ref` field (`via` included), for `access.own`, and for every governed field; a GIN `Members` index for `access.shared_with`; a non-partial btree `(model, E_bool, (id COLLATE "C"))` for a stored boolean `access.public_field`; `created_at` of a tracked model (D75; `updated_at` only by explicit declaration, plan K10); and one system index `(model, (id COLLATE "C"))` on the whole table. A computed `public_field` has no index. The default shape is non-partial and composite, `(model, expr..., (id COLLATE "C"))`, because the planner keeps no statistics for a partial index (plan K7; D76 measured 1,250 ms against 0.11 ms); a partial index is made only for a manifest `where`, and the GIN members and trigram indexes are partial per model (the same contract as D76 Decision 14; the stored boolean `public_field` index is non-partial like the rest). *3. Expressions and names.* Every expression comes from `keyexpr`; typed values are fixed-width canonical text under `COLLATE "C"` (dates, datetimes), guarded `bigint` (number, money), guarded `numeric` (decimal), guarded boolean; raw `::date` and `::timestamptz` indexes fail (they are STABLE) and a raw `::bigint` makes an INSERT throw on a poisoned row, so every guard returns NULL for a wrong JSON type, which is what the engine already calls "empty". The name is `obop_ix_{model[..20]}_{hex(sha256(canonical))[..16]}` (at most 45 characters; the system index `obop_ix_sys_{hash}`), the canonical text begins with `v{EXPR_VERSION}`, so any change of expression, direction, predicate or version is a new name, and the old index shows as `orphan` and is never silently ignored by the planner; the `obop_uq_*` indexes of D57 are not adopted or renamed. Model and field names are inlined as validated literals and only values are bound (with a bound `model = $1` and a generic plan the partial index is lost; a literal keeps it). Ids order bytewise everywhere (`id COLLATE "C"` in SQL, `String::cmp` in Rust), because the primary key `(model, id)` uses the database collation and cannot serve that order. *4. The builder* (`Pg::index_build`, one statement per call): `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` on a dedicated direct session (`PgConnector`; the owner URL `OBOP_DATABASE_OWNER_URL`, else the lock URL, else the runtime URL; never the pool, whose batches are limited to 60 s; behind a pooler in transaction mode with no direct URL the outcome is `NeedsDirect`), with `lock_timeout = 0` for `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` and `DROP INDEX CONCURRENTLY` only, `statement_timeout` as the ceiling (30 minutes, `OBOP_DDL_STATEMENT_TIMEOUT_MS`) and `client_connection_check_interval = 1000` so that the server aborts a build whose client died. **This is a deliberate deviation from the 5 s `lock_timeout` of D73's policy:** a concurrent build waits for older snapshots as lock waits (seen as `Lock/virtualxid`) and does not block writers, so a 5 s limit would abort every build that starts behind a long export and leave debris; everything else (`CREATE EXTENSION`, `ANALYZE`) runs under D73's policy. A build runs under the cross-process `try_lock("obop.indexes")` (the loser skips and retries), after a check that the session's role may use the owner of `obop_record` (otherwise `NeedsOwner`, nothing attempted) and, for trigram, that `pg_trgm` and the ICU collation exist (otherwise `Unsupported`); the validity is re-read afterwards because `IF NOT EXISTS` silently skips an INVALID index, and an INVALID leftover is dropped at once (a killed build leaves an index that is not valid but is still maintained by every write); `ANALYZE obop_record` runs once per pass; messages carry the model, the index name, the kind and the SQLSTATE and never the server's DETAIL (unique and value errors echo data). A package install returns at once; queries stay correct, unindexed, until the index is valid. *5. Who builds.* The in-process reconcile worker (at start and every 30 s, a ledger row `index/{name}` per build with module `storage`, so `drift()` and `verify`, which read only `core/NNNN` rows, are not disturbed) builds only when its role owns `obop_record` (a single role, development, SQLite); under split roles (D77) `index_build` returns `needs_owner` and the operator runs the same command with `--owner-db` (plan K12). It is one command on REST, MCP, CLI and the UI, so no exemption is asked. The model `storage_index` (list, build, prune; admin; builds need approval from an agent) and `obop storage indexes [--json]`, `index build`, `index prune` report declared, automatic, valid, invalid, building, orphan, unmanaged, `protected` (ref, own, unique), `unused_30d` and the write-amplification ratio; an orphan is reported and dropped only by `prune`; the one inspector `schema::inspect` (plan K26) supplies the findings `index_missing`, `index_invalid`, `index_orphan`, `index_unmanaged`. *6. Keyset pagination.* The order is `[plan K1 d1, ..., Kn dn, id asc]` with `n <= 5` and empty values last in both directions (as `Query::apply` does). The rows after a bound `(v1..vn, bid)` are the concatenation, in this order, of the tie arm `T` (all keys equal and `id > bid`) and, for `j` from `n` down to 1 where `vj` is not null, the arm `B_j` (the keys before `j` equal, `Kj` strictly after `vj`) and `N_j` (the keys before `j` equal, `Kj` null); each arm is executed with the full `ORDER BY` and a `LIMIT` of what remains until the page holds `limit + 1` rows, the extra row deciding `next`. Each arm is an equality prefix plus one range on an index ordered the same way. The first page is one arm; the default order is one arm `id > bid` on the system index; for `n <= 1`, ascending, with a non-null bound, `T` and `B` merge into the row comparison `(E, id) > ($v, $id)`. An OR chain and an inclusive bound were measured and removed (96,667 rows removed by filter, 151 ms; the OR forms with NULL scan from the start). `keyset.rs` is pure (`OrderCol`, `Bound`, `Arm`, `order_by`, `arms`) and `Query::page_rows` pages the rows that the engine path (memory, SQLite, and PostgreSQL when a call is not pushable) returns. *7. Totals, caps and the default order.* `total` is `exact` (default), `estimate` or `none`: on the pushed path `SELECT count(*) FROM (SELECT 1 ... LIMIT 50001)` over filter and visibility is exact up to 50,000, above it `EXPLAIN (FORMAT JSON)` gives `Plan Rows`, returned as the larger of the two with `total_is_estimate: true`; the engine path is always exact. `offset` above 10,000 is `offset_too_large: ... continue with after ... (use search)`; `offset` with `after` and `after` without `limit` are refused. A call with no `limit` and no aggregate on a model with more than 20,000 records (`Backend::count_upto(model, 20001)`) is `limit_required: ... pass limit (1 to 5000)`. One setting, `OBOP_QUERY_CANDIDATE_CAP` (20,000, 0 = off), drives that refusal and D76's candidate cap (plan K9). A call that has `limit`, `offset` or `after` orders by `order` followed by `id` ascending; a call with none of them and no `order` keeps insertion order exactly as today (plan K8; a model whose ids are not time-ordered, such as a slug-keyed singleton, lists in id order when paged; models with UUIDv7 ids are unchanged). *8. The cursor.* `after` is a `KeyRing` seal (purpose `cursor:v1:{model}:{caller}`, ChaCha20-Poly1305, 24 hours from the engine clock) of a small JSON object that holds the logical key values (not SQL, not `seq`), the last id, a 16-hex digest of the request (model, filter, order, `lang`, `updated_since`, `include_deleted`; not `limit` or `total`) and, for incremental reads, the phase and `next_since`. So it survives a restore, works on every backend and path, and cannot be forged or replayed across users (a person and that person's agent are different callers), models or queries. The checks run in a fixed order so that nothing says which one failed: more than 8 KiB, not sealed, does not open, the payload does not fit the request all give one text, `cursor_invalid: the cursor does not belong to this request; start again without after`; only an authentic cursor past its time is `cursor_expired` (410). A text order key is at most 256 bytes or the call fails with `cursor_key_too_long`. Row rules, the `usable` check and masking run on every page; the cursor holds only values of fields the caller may order by. Guarantees: (I1) every record present and visible throughout whose key tuple does not change appears exactly once, ties included; (I2) a record inserted, deleted or whose visibility changes mid-iteration appears iff it sorts after the boundary when read; (I3) a record whose order key changes mid-iteration may appear 0, 1 or 2 times and the response never claims otherwise; (I4) a record that commits late with a smaller id (UUIDv7 skew) did not exist throughout and may be missed. *9. Incremental reads.* After D75: `updated_since` adds `updated_at >= X`, fixes the order to `(updated_at, id)` (an `order` input is refused), and every response with `next = null` carries `next_since`, the database clock at the first page minus `OBOP_SYNC_OVERLAP_MS`, **65,000 ms by default** (the lock wait plus 5 s, plan K11; the design's first value was 5 s), carried in the cursor so that one iteration reports one value. A write is stamped before it commits, so a commit can land behind a reader's watermark: clients resume from `next_since` and upsert by id, delivery is at least once, and nothing is lost if no write transaction outlives the overlap. `include_deleted` needs `updated_since` and a model with history; the tombstones are the `delete` entries of D75 (`Backend::changes` with the operation `delete`; there is no `deleted_since`), the cursor phase `ph = 1` embeds D75's `(txid, seq)`, and a tombstone is returned only if the caller could have seen the record on its last stored values (`Gate::visible_deleted`, written by D75 and used here; D75 must keep the fields `own`, `shared_with`, `public_field` and `via` in the delete entry) or is an administrator or holds `see_all`, because tombstones would otherwise reveal that hidden records existed; `deleted_by` is personal data and follows D40. Until D75 is in, `updated_since` answers "needs the system field updated_at, which this model does not have yet (D75)" and `include_deleted` "needs the change history (D75)". *10. `find` and the delete check.* `Pg::find` renders `model = '{m}' AND {slot expression} = $1::t` with the slot taken from the JSON type of the value, and for a non-empty string also the D57 expression `NULLIF(data ->> 'F', '') = $1` so that either index serves it (the D57 write-time uniqueness check calls `find` per unique field and is O(model) today); `guards::referrers` stops reading the whole model per referring model and calls `find` per reference field; computed child lookups get the reference index for free. *11. Surfaces.* The arguments of `search`; REST 410 with a `code` in the body; the MCP and CLI messages begin with the code; the `storage_index` list; the install plan gets an index section; in the browser a Customize Storage tab and a pager (numbered pages to 10,000, then Previous and Next, "about N").

**Threat model.** *Assets:* what each principal may see, the database (DDL in the serving path), write throughput, the confidentiality of tombstones. *A forged, replayed or stolen cursor:* sealed, bound to the model, the caller and the request, 24 hours; a flipped bit in any byte is `cursor_invalid`; a cursor from another user, agent, model or query is the same one text; a stolen cursor is useless to another principal; the cursor holds no rule inputs and no value of a field the caller may not order by. *Hidden rows in pages, `next` or `total`:* the row rule and visibility run on every page and in the total (the estimate is of the visible query). *Injection through `where` and `fields`:* the grammar above; DDL text is built only from validated tokens; a fuzz test of 1,000 manifests. *A package that makes the database slow or its writes expensive:* the caps 12, 60 and 24, the write-amplification ratio shown, `unused_30d`, and the 3 s rule of D76 for unindexed queries; a build never blocks writers (it is concurrent) and is bounded by the statement timeout; a second process does not start a second build. *A build that fails or is killed:* the INVALID leftover is dropped and rebuilt at the next pass (every 30 s), and an INVALID index costs writes until then, so the pass runs at start and on a timer. *Poisoned stored values:* guards return NULL, so an INSERT and a build never throw; the API refuses a value over 1,000 bytes and data that bypasses it makes a build fail as `ValueTooLong` without the value in the message. *Tombstones that reveal existence:* the visibility rule of Decision 9. *The builder's privileges:* it needs the owner role (D77) and a direct session; it never uses the pool, and `needs_owner` is an answer, not an error. *Not bounded:* a descending sort on a low-cardinality key can cost up to the size of the tie group per page (the planner mis-estimates the tie arm; measured 41 to 128 ms for 33,333 remaining rows against 0.4 ms), so `obop storage indexes` flags indexes whose leading key has fewer than 1,000 distinct values; and a record whose order key changes mid-iteration (I3).

**Acceptance tests (named).** *On main today:* `member_tokens_split_on_eight_ascii_separators_and_trim_nothing`, `the_member_class_is_the_constant`, `expression_texts_are_pinned`, `one_writer_lint` (`obop-storage/src/keyexpr.rs`), `keyexpr_parity_on_postgresql` (`obop-commands/src/keyexpr_parity.rs`), `each_row_maps_as_the_table_says` and `the_table_says_what_the_plan_says` (`obop-commands/src/errors.rs`). *Of the work packages that are not built (the tests do not exist yet):* manifest and registry: `index_gate_refuses_13_per_model_and_61_per_package`, `index_gate_names_model_and_package`, `index_gate_ceiling_counts_automatic`, `automatic_indexes_cover_ref_own_shared_state_public`, `declared_equal_to_automatic_is_not_counted`, `unique_index_desugars_to_d57`, `where_grammar_refuses_injection` (1,000 fuzzed manifests), `old_core_refuses_index_keys`; storage on every backend: `index_name_is_definition_hashed_and_45_chars`, `canonical_changes_with_every_part`, `create_sql_golden`, `find_matches_memory_semantics`, `count_upto_caps`, `try_lock_does_not_wait`, `member_tokens_equal_pg_split`, `arms_golden`; PostgreSQL: `poisoned_rows_never_break_insert_or_build`, `explain_matchable` (200,000 rows; for each slot and shape the plan must name the expected `obop_ix_` index, with and without a generic plan) with its mutation twins `explain_mutation_missing_collate_fails`, `explain_mutation_bound_field_name_fails`, `explain_mutation_bound_model_generic_plan_fails` (they prove the EXPLAIN tests can fail), `concurrent_build_under_200_writes_per_second` (three indexes on 300,000 rows, the longest write under 500 ms, all valid), `killed_builder_leaves_invalid_then_next_pass_rebuilds` (the test re-executes its own binary as the builder, kills it with SIGKILL or TerminateProcess and with `pg_terminate_backend`), `if_not_exists_does_not_hide_invalid`, `two_builders_race`, `idle_in_transaction_blocks_build_and_names_blocker`, `needs_owner_when_not_table_owner`, `trigram_unavailable_degrades`, `orphan_is_reported_not_dropped_until_prune`, `ledger_rows_partial_then_applied`, `keyset_dsc_tie_group_cost_is_bounded_by_group`; commands: `cursor_roundtrip`, `cursor_expires_after_24h_410`, `cursor_bitflip_every_byte`, `cursor_cross_user_agent_model_query_all_invalid`, `cursor_after_key_rotation`, `cursor_1mib_rejected_before_decode`, `cursor_never_contains_invisible_keys`, `cursor_key_too_long`, `keyset_pages_equal_oracle_property` (random filters, up to five order keys, both directions, nulls and ties: pages chained through `next` equal `Query::apply`), `ties_straddling_page_boundary`, `keyset_stable_under_concurrent_writers` (20,000 records, pages of 500, a writer inserting 1,000 a minute, deleting 100 and changing keys: every untouched id exactly once), `offset_cap_names_after`, `offset_with_after_refused`, `total_modes_and_estimate_flag`, `unbounded_list_20000_ok_20001_refused`, `list_refuses_after_with_use_search`, `default_order_unchanged_without_limit`, `hidden_rows_never_in_pages_or_next`, `updated_since_refused_until_d75`; surfaces and browser: the 410 body and `code`, the MCP and OpenAPI snapshots, `storage.spec.ts` and `paging.spec.ts` (10,001 or more rows seeded through `bulk_create`).

**Why.** A list reads the whole model and pages by offset; a sync client has no way to resume or to learn about deletions; an unbounded call on a big model is an outage waiting for a user; and the speed of everything else depends on indexes that nobody declares (D57 builds only its unique ones). The three needs share one piece of machinery, the expression text that the engine, the indexes and the pushdown must agree on, so they are one record: indexes that the planner can match, a pager that costs the same on page 1,000 as on page 1, and a feed that does not lose a write.

**Rejected.** *Wrapper functions and custom collations in the database* (one design): nothing for the owner role to version and nothing to restore. *An opt-in envelope for `list`, a store epoch, `CallError::Gone`, `transaction_timeout`, an OR-chain keyset* (other designs): the repository keeps `list` a bare array, 410 is a row of the error table, and arms are the measured form. *Partial indexes by default:* no statistics (Decision 2). *A `lock_timeout` of 5 s for a concurrent build:* aborts builds behind a long export (Decision 4). *A 5 s overlap for incremental reads:* a transaction can outlive it (65 s, plan K11). *Automatic indexing of `updated_at`:* every write changes it, so it would double the write cost of every model (plan K10). *Adopting the `obop_uq_*` indexes into the new naming:* they stay as they are. *An index builder in a DEFINER function:* `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` cannot run inside a function, so there is no door; the owner runs it (plan K12). *Typed `date` or `timestamptz` columns in the index now:* that is the typed layout (OBR-DAT-011); here dates are canonical text under `COLLATE "C"`.

**Consequences.** *Behaviour changes* (Q49 for the tokenizer, Q54 for the rest): the member tokenizer is live and one-way: a `shared_with` list written with tabs, carriage returns, form feeds or vertical tabs now grants access, and a list whose entry is padded with Unicode spaces (a non-breaking space, an em space) no longer names the person it used to name, because nothing is trimmed any more; an unbounded call on a model of more than 20,000 records is refused; an offset above 10,000 is refused; a call that pages is ordered by id after its own order; `list` refuses the new arguments with `use_search`. *Costs.* Every declared or automatic index costs every write of its model (at most 24 per model; the HOT-update loss is a known PostgreSQL behaviour that was not measured); a build scans the whole shared `obop_record` table, because there are no per-model tables, so plans estimate on the total row count. *D75 interplay.* `updated_since` and tombstones wait for D75; the default sync overlap is 65 s. *D76 interplay.* D76 emits the same default order (plan K8) and compares keyset arms on a text key under the same collation, not served by an index in this wave; the page interface is D78's `Query::page_rows`, so D76's Finish and OverFetch page through one function. *D77 interplay.* Under split roles the build is an owner command. *Docs.* docs/MODULES.md (the manifest keys), docs/DEPLOY.md (the settings), the OpenAPI document and docs/spec/* will say what is built.

**Not delivered, and why.** Typed `date`, `timestamptz` and `numeric(p,s)` columns and the real "typed by kind" index (OBR-DAT-011); a text sort index (text order is pushed down by D76 but not index-served); the order of a select by option position in SQL; trigram for patterns under three characters; `where` beyond `eq`, `ne`, `in`, `is_null` over stored fields, and `unique` with `where`; SQLite pushdown (SQLite indexes serve `find` only); the D57 uniqueness through the concurrent builder; `updated_since`, `next_since` and tombstones until D75; true big exact counts (use the aggregate); automatic dropping of orphans; per-model tables.

**Not tried.** A C-locale database (the claim that `COLLATE "C"` then needs its own index is reasoned from collation OIDs); `lock_timeout` aborting a waiting build; managed PostgreSQL (`pg_trgm`, an ICU build, `client_connection_check_interval` off Linux); PgBouncer in transaction mode; a Windows builder against a Linux server beyond the reset path; the HOT-update loss; 200 writes per second over 1,000,000 rows (the test is designed, not run); PostgreSQL 18 for the builder.

**Evidence.** The expression writer and the tokenizer on main (pull request 5, head 506bb28) passed GitHub Actions rust (Linux, PostgreSQL 18), web, e2e and lockfiles and the parity run described in D76. Everything else in Decision is the design: its measurements were taken on PostgreSQL 17.11 with a 500,000-row probe table (since dropped) and were not repeated for this record: the arms are ordered index scans of 0.1 ms on 300,000 rows with a three-valued key; the row comparison is an index condition; a bound `model = $1` with a generic plan loses the partial index; the primary-key order differs from the bytewise order (`0, a, a-b, ab, B, _x, Z` against `0, B, Z, _x, a, a-b, ab`); a killed concurrent build leaves an index that is not valid but is still maintained.

#### D79 Persons, organisation, grants and scoped row rules
**Status** PROPOSED (owner-directed in chat, 2026-10-25/26, as item 2 of the plan, "org units and scoped row rules, decision record first"; not yet ratified as binding). **Reversibility** the code is a two-way door (two new built-in modules, optional manifest keys, new commands, additive fields on existing records; a manifest, an account or a user that uses none of it behaves as before).
