Four things are one-way doors once an instance holds data in them: (1) a minted person id, because it is also the account key and the value that `own`, `shared_with` and grants hold; (2) the stored shapes of `access_role` and `access_grant` (options may be added, a field is never retyped or removed); (3) the manifest keys a package starts to use (a process older than this record cannot load such a package and treats the module as absent, which fails closed); (4) the three security fixes that change behaviour by default (blob reads, computed totals and effect children follow row visibility). **Owner action** yes, four small items, none of which blocks starting work: (a) name the reviewer for the security paths (`obop-auth`, `obop-oauth`, the authority and gate code), so `.github/CODEOWNERS` can be written; (b) confirm the sign-in alias design, which touches the sign-in path; (c) accept before the gate and channel work packages merge that a readable-but-not-allowed refusal becomes HTTP 403, that effects, blobs and computed totals follow visibility, and that an approval re-resolves its owner live; (d) read the 25 waiver texts for bundled models in the work package that adds them. At deployment time (no deploy is approved by this record) the operator sets `OBOP_ADMIN_BYPASS=0` and `OBOP_DISCLOSE_WITHHELD=0` on new hosted instances; both default to 1, today's behaviour.

**Source.** Derived from the requirement set of the first downstream project, kept outside this repository (identity, tenancy and authorisation: persons and organisation, scoped grants and their administration, row rules and their side channels, the install-time proof), and from the defect register and two architecture records of that project's first module, which show what obop could not yet express. Where the requirement text contradicts the repository, the repository wins and Consequences lists the deviations. Nothing here names that project, a customer or an industry; examples use generic units and sites. Read with D10 (the algebra this record implements: grants OR, restrictions AND, default-deny), D18, D40, D47, D48, D53, D56 to D58, D64, D65, D69, D70 and D72, and with the step 1 records D73 to D78 (see Consequences for the order of merging).

**Decision.**

*1. Shape.* One decision, three topics, one set of shared contracts. Two new built-in modules: `org` (persons and organisation) and `access` (roles, grants and the explorer, in the new kernel crate `obop-access`). An extension of the existing `[model.access]` table. A person-aware account path in `obop-auth`. One test kit crate. Every command is generated or declared in the registry and therefore on REST, MCP, the CLI, OpenAPI and `/api/meta` with a screen in the backoffice in the same change (UI parity is literal); the only hand-written routes are the D69 account routes, which gain fields and two siblings.

*2. Who is a person.* A person is a record of `org_person`. Its id is the principal id everywhere: the `auth_user` key, `Principal.id`, `Actor.id`, the value in `own` and `shared_with`, and `access_grant.person`. An id is minted server side (`PersonId::mint()`, a lower case UUIDv7; an `id` sent by a client is ignored) or adopted once from an existing account key (frozen from then on; one source file may call `PersonId::adopted`, which refuses a `bg-`, `svc-` or `connector:` prefix and any id with a `/`). Nothing derives an id from an e-mail address, a principal name, a name or an IdP subject. Credentials stay in `auth_user`; the link is key equality; a person with no account (a contractor, a mailbox, a historical person) and an account with no person (a legacy account until it is adopted) are both legal. People type a sign-in alias, not the id: `auth_login/<alias>` maps a lower case alias (`[a-z0-9._@-]{1,64}`, never UUID-shaped, never equal to any account key or alias) to the account key, is resolved in `login_step_impl`, the one door that takes typed text, and the lockout counter is keyed by the resolved key. Renaming an alias changes no token, grant, passkey or `own` value.

*3. Creating, adopting, leaving.* D69's path stays the only way to make an account. `create_user_as`, `set_groups_as`, `offboard_as` and the other creation paths first become one `apply_checked` batch of the change and its security event (today the change and the event are separate writes); person-aware creation then composes, in that one batch, the ops of the person row (built by the `PersonRegistry` port that `org` implements), the account insert, the alias insert and the event. There is no `org_person.invite` command, because a generated temporary password cannot leave the Dispatcher. `adopt` turns an existing account into a person with id = its key (`obop admin adopt NAME | --all [--dry-run]`, `user_account.adopt`, REST `POST /api/auth/users/{id}/adopt`). Account creation, adoption and alias changes need `platform.admin`; `org.person_admin` manages profile, lifecycle, memberships and identifiers, not credentials. Person status is governed (D48): `invited`, `active`, `suspended`, `left`, moved by `activate`, `suspend`, `reinstate`, `leave` and `rehire`, each needs approval from an agent. `leave` is a resumable, fail-closed sequence: mark the person `suspended` with `leave_pending`, run D69's offboarding (which also ends the person's grants in its own atomic batch), then in one batch end every membership and identifier at `left_on`, write a leaver run and one counted ownership task per (model, `own` field) that still holds the id, and bump the epoch; running it again after any crash completes it and equals an uninterrupted run. An account bound to a person refuses the generic `disable`, `enable` and `offboard` on every surface (the operator CLI keeps `--force`, which writes a security event). `org_ownership_task.reassign` rewrites an owner field, which is a read escalation (`own` grants read and write), so it needs `platform.admin`.

*4. The organisation is data.* `org_level` (the vocabulary: id, rank, allowed parent levels, at most one `stamp` level; `seed_defaults` offers country, region, office, unit, site), `org_node` (a node is closed, never deleted), `org_node_edge` (the effective-dated truth: half-open `[valid_from, valid_to)` in `date` kind, one parent per child on every date, no cycle on any date, checked at every later edge start so a future-dated move cannot close one), `org_node_alias`, `org_company`, `org_company_relation`, `org_node_operator`. `org_membership`: many per person, one `main` on any date, effective-dated, and a membership grants nothing. There is no stored mirror of the current parent or main node (it would go stale at a date boundary with no scheduler to refresh it); as-of reads return them. Invariants are refused with a message that names them. Structure changes only through module commands: generic create, update, delete and `bulk_create` are refused for everyone, `platform.admin` included, on every model the index holds, and every batch that changes levels, nodes, edges, memberships or operators carries `authority::bump_op()` (decision 12). The engine sees the tree through one port, `OrgDirectory` (as-of ancestors, descendants, stamp node, operator, resolve, existence, the next date boundary, display names), implemented by an in-memory `OrgIndex` rebuilt when the permission epoch changes. A storage error, an unreadable index or a corrupt tree (a cycle, two parents) never widens anything: the call returns an error, or the nodes involved are detached from every set and listed by `org_problem`. A node set is the descendants as of today; a closed node drops out of every set, so its records are visible to unscoped holders only until it is reopened or its records are moved, and `org_node.close` reports how many records are stamped with it.

*5. Identifiers, matching, feed v0.* `org_external_identifier` keeps (kind, namespace, folded value) with a unique `active_key` for active rows; a pure matcher tries principal name, mail, mail alias and employee number in that order and never a name; a conflict opens an `org_match_case`, a `locked` identifier is never changed automatically, every decision writes an audit row that holds ids and not values. `org_field_source.apply_feed` is one idempotent command with per-field provenance (a field a human typed is never overwritten), at most 1,000 facts, `dry_run`. Connectors and a scheduler are not part of this record.

*6. One rule language.* Row rules stay data on `[model.access]` and stay closed (`deny_unknown_fields`). The five D58 keys keep their meaning exactly. There are four positions: READ, WRITE, DELETE and one per action. A position is a list of grants that OR together and a list of restrictions that AND after them; `WRITE = READ and w`, `DELETE = READ and d` (else WRITE), `ACTION(a) = READ and row_access(a)` (else WRITE), so a write rule is never wider than read and there is no blind write. New grants: `scope` (`record[field]` in the caller's node set for a group, or equal to the caller's stamp node), `related` (a member row of another model links the caller to the record by user, node or group, with optional roles; capped at 500 user and 200 node or group member rows per record, enforced by generated guards). New restriction: `restrict = [{ expr, unless = ["own" | group] }]`, an expression limited to the closed subset `true false ! && || is_set(F)` and `==`/`!=` of a stored text, select, ref or date field against a literal. Other keys: `[model.access.write]`, `[model.access.delete]`, per-action `row_access`, `[model.aggregate]`, `agent_writes = "propose"`, `create = "effects"` (no generated `create` or `bulk_create`), `access_waiver`. Field control: `write_group` (who may change the field), `read_when` and `write_when` (relations `own`, `scope`, `any`; a field with a non-`any` condition is not filterable, sortable or aggregable), `action_only = true` (written only by effects of declared actions; named so it cannot be confused with D75's reserved system fields), `computed_visibility = "all"` (keeps the old sum over every child). Any missing, null, empty or mistyped value is false, never an error. The `shared_with` list uses the member tokenizer of D78 (`keyexpr::member_tokens`, one constant for Rust and SQL), not the four separators of today's text.

*7. Caller facts.* One type carries them: `Reach { stamp, sets, epoch, resolved }` on `Principal` (an `Arc`) and on `Actor`. `sets` maps a group to a node set and holds only groups the caller has through scoped grants alone; a held group absent from `sets` is held everywhere (direct groups and unscoped grants), so `Principal.groups` keeps meaning "held in any scope" and `allows` is unchanged. `stamp` is the person's main-membership node lifted to the stamp level, as of today (None when there is none). A principal built without resolving a reach (a connector, an observer, a test) is `resolved = false`, and every `scope` grant is false for it: a scoped group can never turn into an unscoped one by being copied. For a `scope` rule the evaluator reads the caller as follows: group not held, false; held in `sets`, the record's field must be in the set; held and not in `sets`, true for every record, stamped or not. A holder of a scoped root grant sees the records stamped with an existing node and no record with an empty node (pinned by a test). `see_all_group`, `all_group` and every bypass-like rule use `holds_everywhere` (an unscoped hold or the superuser), never the any-scope `holds`. A holder of a model's read group through scoped grants alone, on a model with no `scope` entry for that group, sees no row by the group route (`own`, `shared_with`, `see_all` still apply): a scoped grant can never become an unscoped one by landing on a model that has no scope rule. Reach is stamped into a call as `_actor_reach` and `_actor_approver` (the `_actor` prefix is already stripped from client input), only for the groups some installed `scope` or `related` rule names. Caps, each fail-closed: an expansion of one grant target is refused above 5,000 nodes, a set holds at most 10,000 nodes and a caller at most 50 groups. An agent's reach is its owner's, narrowed to the groups its token scopes allow, so what an agent can read is a subset of what its owner can read.

*8. Order and wording of the checks.* (1) the command group (`p.allows`, any-scope hold; none: 404 as today); (2) guards before the handler: a filter or order on a field the caller cannot read, then the governed-field, action and delete guards and a new `row_guard` that loads the record with READ and then the position (so an agent's proposal fails at proposal time); (3) the handler: READ on the record (hidden: `no <model> <id>`, status 400, unchanged and byte-identical to a missing record), then the position (readable but not allowed: `CallError::Denied`, REST 403, MCP `isError` with `denied`, CLI exit 1 (every failed call exits 1; only a refused store exits 75, and 3 is `restore_key_required`; the Proposed class 13 of docs/spec/cli.md 2.4 is not built), text `<model> <id> cannot be changed by you`, the same for update, delete and action), then field rules, then kinds and refs (a ref needs READ of its target), assertions, unique values, sequences; (4) `create`: stamp every empty `scope` field with the caller's stamp, check the node (a non-empty scope value that is supplied on create or changed on update must equal the stamp or lie in a set for a scope rule on that field, so an owner cannot stamp a foreign unit; the superuser passes), then WRITE and READ on the candidate; (5) `update`: WRITE before and WRITE after (a move needs the group at both nodes; the caller cannot lose write on the result). Create and update preparation moves into one function (`engine/write.rs`, used by `create`, `bulk_create` and effects), so one door applies stamping, field rules and positions. `list` and `search` use READ only: results, totals and pages are identical with and without a write block. `Gate` stays the one predicate holder: `Gate::decide(def, pos, id, rec, trace) -> Decision` returns the verdict and the reasons, `allows` is `decide` without a trace, `visible` is `allows(Read)`, and the permission explorer calls the same function. Raw store reads are renamed `raw_get`, `raw_find`, `raw_all`, `raw_find_in`; a register file lists every source file that calls one outside tests with its purpose and count, and a test fails on a new call site.

*9. Side channels.* Each has a decision and a named test. S1 a blob is readable when some record that holds it passes READ and the field's group and `read_when`; none: the same not-found as a missing blob. S2 computed totals count the children the caller can see on every read path (`computed_visibility = "all"` restores the old sum for a field), while assertions and rule evaluation read raw. S3 a unique value stays global unless `unique_with` names the scope field, and its message never names a record. S4 `Response.denied` is emptied unless `OBOP_DISCLOSE_WITHHELD` is on, withheld fields are omitted silently, `/api/meta` gets `fields_withheld`. S5 counts, totals and pages are READ-filtered. S6 hidden and missing are one message builder, byte-equal on every surface (timing is measured, not claimed). S7 effects (`for_each`, children targets, `match` locators) read visible rows only, hidden ones are skipped silently and do not count toward the 500 cap; `for_each ... where` and `match ... require = "one"` exist; there is no escape until service principals exist. S8 connector principals are rebuilt live from the owner (decision 11). S9 discovery (meta, commands, OpenAPI, MCP tool list) names no withheld field and never exposes a restrict expression. S10 `bulk_create` runs the checks of `create` per row; seeds, restore and imports by the store are unchecked and the spec says so. S11 no audit event carries row data. S12 the reads register. Accepted residuals, each pinned by a test: a refused duplicate says that a (possibly hidden) record holds the value, a refused delete says that records the caller cannot see refer to it, assertions read all children, sequences have gaps.

*10. Aggregate-only access.* `[model.aggregate]` generates `<model>.aggregate` with its own permission group (it is not an overload of `search`, which has one group per command). Input is limited to declared dimensions and measures (`count`, `sum`, `avg`; no `min` or `max`, which show single values); a date dimension needs a bucket at or above `min_bucket`; the rows are those passing READ restricts and `aggregate.scope`, whatever the caller's READ. `engine::aggregate_only::suppress` is the only suppression code, used by every surface: groups with fewer distinct `cohort_field` values than `min_cohort` (default 5, never below 2) merge into one `suppressed` bucket, a single fallen group takes the smallest remaining one with it (`secondary: true`), no total is returned, calls are rate limited per principal and model and logged with a fingerprint and no data. Dimensions may not be personal or sensitive fields. Differencing across overlapping queries is not prevented and product text must not claim it is.

*11. Grants and roles (module `access`).* Authority is data. `access_role` (id `module.role`; groups split into unscoped and scoped lists; `scope` none, node or company; `approvals` 1 to 3 frozen into each grant and at least 2 when the bundle holds a reserved group; `may_grant`; status) and `access_grant` (person, role, target node or company id, `descendants`, `valid_from` and `valid_to` as `datetime`, state `proposed`, `active`, `ended` or `rejected`, basis, approvers, `open_key` made unique with `ensure_unique` so one open grant per person, role and target). Both are read-only generated models; every change is a declared command with a bound handler. `auth_user.groups` stay as direct groups: unscoped, undated, permanent. A person's effective groups are direct groups plus the groups of live grants, and an unscoped hold of a group removes it from the scoped map. A user with no grants resolves exactly as today, so there is no flag day. Instances move person by person, or all at once with `access_batch.adopt_groups` (a digest-checked dry run, then one grant per account and non-reserved direct group under role `legacy.<group>`), and may then switch `security.direct_groups` to `reserved_only` (opt-in per instance; refused while an enabled account would lose a group it holds only directly). Reserved: the prefixes `platform.`, `access.`, `security.`, `audit.`, `privacy.` and the three exact names `org.person_admin`, `org.node_admin`, `org.feed_operator`; a reserved group in a bundle forces `approvals >= 2` and removes the role from every `may_grant`; `platform.admin` is never in a bundle. The `org` module ships the role `org.member` (bundle `org.staff`) so a person always has a role to be granted once direct groups are retired. Lifecycle: `propose` (agents may propose), `confirm` (counted approvals; humans only; a fresh session; not the grantee or the proposer when two approvals are needed; separation of duties over the resulting explicit groups; delegation only through `may_grant` and only inside the delegate's own node set; a grant that cannot be covered answers `no access_grant <id>` as for a missing record), `reject`, `end`, `shorten`, `sweep`, `import` (CSV, dry run first, at most 1,000 rows), a cover grant (`basis = cover`) of at most 90 days; nobody can propose a grant for themselves or for their own agent; the last unscoped holder of `access.role_admin` or `access.grant_admin` cannot be ended. Freshness is session age (`security.step_up_secs`, 0 to 3,600, 900 on new instances, the error `reauth_required`) until a real step-up exists. Connector principals are rebuilt at each run from the owner's live principal, intersected with the model's read group; a disabled or offboarded owner makes the flow refuse.

*12. One epoch, one resolver, one cache.* The permission epoch is the record `obop_meta/permission_epoch`, bumped with `Op::add(...).or_create(...)` in the same `apply_checked` batch as every change that can alter who holds what: grant confirm, end, shorten, sweep, adopt; role groups, authority or retirement; package role sync; `end_grants_of`; a change of the `security` settings; and every write that changes the organisation (levels, nodes, edges, memberships, operators). The tree and the grants share the one counter because a grant's node set is computed from the tree: two counters would let one go stale. There is no separate organisation epoch. `Authority::effective(person, now)` is the only resolver: it reads the epoch once per request, uses one generation per epoch per process (all active grants indexed by person, the role catalogue, memoised node sets, per-person results valid until the next grant, edge or membership boundary, the boundary being midnight in the platform zone), rebuilds under a single-flight lock, and fails closed: an epoch read or generation build error means no principal (401), a missing node gives a held group with an empty set. `OBOP_ACCESS_EPOCH_CACHE_MS` (0 to 5,000, default 0, start-up refuses more) trades epoch reads for bounded staleness. After a restore the epoch may move backwards; caches compare with `!=`. With the default setting a request that comes after a commit in any process sees it: a revoke is effective on the next request.

*13. The log and the explorer.* `security_event` is the change log. It gains optional `subject`, `ref`, `before`, `after`, `retention`, its read group becomes `audit.reader` (`platform.admin` still satisfies it), and authority commands write change, bump and event in one batch (`audit::event_op`). `access_decision.explain` evaluates one person's current principal on a model, an operation and **supplied candidate fields** (it reads no stored record, so it is no existence oracle) and returns groups with provenance, the scope sets, the matching reasons, what would have granted it, and field outcomes; naming a stored record id instead needs `platform.admin` and a fresh session and is logged. `who_can` (audit.reader, fresh, at most 500 ids), `mine`, `can` (which of up to 200 readable ids the caller may update, delete or act on) and the virtual model `access_rule` (the rules per model as data and sentences, lints, waivers) complete the surface; each use of the first two is an event.

*14. The install proof.* One module, `obop_registry::proof`, runs the lints L1 to L19 (rules, waivers, roles, scoped bundles, groups, expressions, effects, aggregates, e-mail-shaped literals) from the installer, `obop modules check` and a test over the built-in modules, never from `check_module` or `to_module`, so a module already installed can never vanish at the next start. The instance setting `security.access_gate` selects `enforce` (new instances) or `warn` (migrated instances) for the lints that may be downgraded; the structural ones (a waiver on a model that has rules, `public_field` or `shared_with` with sensitive fields, e-mail literals, reserved or foreign groups in a role) block in both modes, and `modules check --strict` raises all. A model with personal or sensitive fields and neither rules nor a waiver (40 to 400 characters) fails L1; a waiver is shown in `/api/meta`, the install plan and the install event; a waiver does not excuse a sensitive field without its own group, so a model with a special-category field is accepted with a waiver only when each such field has an explicit field group. The 25 bundled models that carry such fields and have no rules (in 16 of the 22 packages) get waivers with honest texts in this change and change no behaviour.

*15. Operator, agents, approvals.* One flag, `OBOP_ADMIN_BYPASS` (default 1), read once into `Principal.admin_bypass`. `Principal::superuser()` (bypass on and `platform.admin` held) is the single bypass point for group checks and row rules; `Principal::is_admin_account()` is the group test for administrative gates (account, people, MFA, passkeys, grants). With the flag off, `platform.admin` passes row rules like anyone and holds only groups it is given plus the commands registered to it. A guard test fails when a non-test use of `ADMIN_GROUP` appears outside an allow-list. Agents: the owner's id, groups and reach narrowed by token scopes; `agent_writes = "propose"` makes `create`, `bulk_create` and `update` need approval and an action that writes such a model too. `Dispatcher::approve` rebuilds the run principal from the owner's current principal intersected with the stored plan (groups and node sets, token scopes kept); a disabled, offboarded or missing owner voids the plan; the approver must hold the command group and pass a row check on the record the plan names; `$approver` is available in effect mappings; every approval writes `plan_approve`.

*16. Storage and compilation.* The same code runs on memory, SQLite and PostgreSQL. A `Policy` is compiled lazily from `Access`; `specialise(def, pos, caller)` folds it for one caller into a `Residual` (`True`, `False`, `And`, `Or`, `Eq`, `In`, `ListHas`, `Flag`, `Cond`, `Parent`, `Member`) with every caller fact bound as a constant. The in-memory `Gate` and D76's SQL lowering both consume `Residual`; D76 adds `Pred::Member` and the SQL forms (`= ANY($n::text[])`, NULL-safe leaves, computed flags relaxed to TRUE and re-checked by the gate), and a registry test fails when a `Residual` variant has no vector in `tests/access/vectors.json` that D76's compiler must also pass. As-of reads of the organisation are plain text comparisons of canonical dates, never a recursive query over JSON: the node set arrives as one bind array from `OrgDirectory`. Unique indexes (`access_grant.open_key`, `org_external_identifier.active_key`, node `key` with level, company `registration_no`) go through `Backend::ensure_unique`; module writes bypass the engine's `check_unique`, so the module re-checks under the write lock and the index is the backstop. `Backend::find_in(model, field, values)` is owned by D76 (a default that lists and filters until then).

*17. Not a second system.* There is no second policy store (no `access_policy` records), no separate scope-source port beside `OrgDirectory`, no second epoch, no second oracle: one rule grammar, one port to the organisation, one epoch, one resolver, one decision function and one test kit.

**Threat model.** *Assets:* the person registry and its personal data; the tree; the authority to grant; the rules; the visibility of every record; the log. *A stolen or over-reaching agent:* it can propose and read within its owner's narrowed reach; `confirm`, role commands, batch apply and adopt refuse agents; its approval re-resolves the owner live and is void for a revoked owner (`agent_of_revoked_owner_gets_nothing`, `plan_is_reresolved_at_approval`). *Privilege escalation through grants:* self-grant, self-approval through an agent label, four-eyes with one person twice, delegation outside the delegate's node set, cover grants that never end, a reserved group smuggled into a bundle with holders, `platform.admin` in a bundle, a stale session confirming (`self_grant_self_approval_and_own_agent_refused`, `four_eyes_needs_two_distinct_non_proposers`, `delegated_confirm_outside_scope_answers_as_missing`, `reserved_group_cannot_join_a_role_with_holders`, `stale_session_needs_reauth_and_agents_cannot_confirm`). *Stale authority:* the epoch is in the same batch as the change, read once per request, compared with `!=`; a clock skew shifts a boundary by the skew but revocation is a write, so skew never extends it (`two_processes_honour_a_revoke_on_the_next_request_100_of_100`, `restore_to_lower_epoch_rebuilds`). *Identity confusion:* an id derived from a mail address, an alias that shadows a key, two processes claiming one alias, alternating alias and id to double the guesses, a generic disable that drifts the status (`auth_login::alias_cannot_shadow_a_key_or_look_like_an_id`, `auth_login::lockout_is_keyed_by_the_resolved_key`, `lifecycle::person_bound_account_refuses_generic_disable_enable_offboard`). *Reading across scopes by a side channel:* blobs, totals, effects, counts, pages, unique and delete messages, discovery, error text, aggregates, bulk; a world-pair test changes only what a persona cannot see and demands byte-identical responses. *Aggregate differencing and small cohorts:* suppression with a secondary bucket, no total, rate limit; differencing is documented as not prevented. *A corrupt or racing tree:* a future-dated move cannot close a cycle, two writers cannot leave two open edges (compare-and-set), a bad tree is detached and reported. *Feed poisoning and false matches:* human fields and locked identifiers survive a feed, a name is never an input to the matcher, an ambiguous match opens a case. *Mass reassignment as a read escalation:* `platform.admin` only, at most 500 rows per call. *A half-done leave:* resumable and idempotent. *A scoped group widened by a code path that copies group text* (connector principals): unresolved reach makes scope grants false until the principal is rebuilt live. *Operators:* the bypass flag, reserved groups, the last-holder guard; with the bypass on the operator still reads every row, which the instance operator is. *Residual:* no real step-up re-authentication until the IdP work (session age stands in); a person holding a direct group holds it everywhere by definition; records of a closed node are invisible to scoped holders; timing differences between hidden and missing records are not claimed away; direct store writes, seeds and restore are unchecked; ids are UUIDv7, so anyone who sees an `own` value learns a creation time (as with every obop id); the waiver texts are the authors' statements, checked only for length and shape.

**Acceptance tests (named).** Each name is a test its work package must add; a package is not done until its names exist and pass on memory, SQLite and PostgreSQL where storage matters. The complete lists are in the specifications; these are the ones the record rests on. *Persons and identity:* `person_id::mint_is_uuidv7_lowercase`, `person_id::adopted_refuses_reserved_prefixes_and_slashes`, `person_id::adopted_has_exactly_one_caller`, `person::delete_is_refused_for_every_group_including_admin`, `auth_login::alias_resolves_at_password_totp_second_step_and_oauth_page`, `auth_login::passkey_login_stays_keyed_by_id`, `auth_login::two_processes_cannot_claim_one_alias`, `auth_login::rename_changes_no_token_grant_or_passkey`, `create::person_account_is_one_batch_failure_leaves_nothing`, `create::generated_password_is_returned_once_and_not_logged`, `compat::legacy_users_own_rules_and_tokens_are_unchanged`, `atomic::every_account_writer_commits_change_and_event_together`. *Organisation:* `org_index::tree_matches_recursive_reference_over_1000_random_moves`, `org_index::cycle_two_parents_wrong_level_duplicate_key_are_refused`, `org_index::future_dated_move_cannot_close_a_cycle`, `org_index::corrupt_tree_is_detached_not_fatal`, `org_index::second_process_sees_a_new_epoch`, `org_index::as_of_both_sides_of_a_change_date`, `membership::per_day_oracle_for_overlap_main_and_gap`, `identifier::sixty_case_matching_fixture`, `identifier::two_writers_one_active_value_exactly_one_succeeds`, `feed::replay_adds_zero_rows_and_keeps_human_fields`, `leave::crash_after_each_step_then_rerun_equals_clean_run`, `leave::ends_every_credential_and_enable_is_refused`, `reassign::needs_platform_admin_and_moves_only_the_own_field`, `node::close_reports_stamped_records`. *Grants and epoch:* `resolution::direct_only_user_is_byte_for_byte_unchanged`, `resolution::matches_a_naive_reference_over_random_histories`, `resolution::boundary_ends_and_starts_without_a_write`, `epoch::bump_is_in_the_grant_batch`, `epoch::org_and_grant_writes_share_one_counter`, `epoch::every_listed_write_bumps_and_no_other`, `epoch::cache_ms_above_5000_refuses_to_start`, `grants::lifecycle_and_invariants`, `grants::one_open_grant_per_key_across_processes`, `grants::adopt_changes_nobody_s_effective_groups`, `grants::flip_refused_while_a_person_would_lose_a_group`, `grants::offboard_ends_grants_in_one_batch`, `approve::runs_with_live_authority_intersected`, `connector::principal_is_live_and_scoped`, `reach::unresolved_reach_denies_scope_grants`, `reach::scoped_only_holder_without_scope_rule_sees_nothing`, `log::every_kind_writes_exactly_one_event_in_its_batch`, `explain::agrees_with_outcome_for_10000_pairs`, `explain::reads_no_stored_record`. *Rules:* `legacy_gate_and_policy_agree_on_100k_random_cases`, `restrict_only_narrows`, `residual_vectors_json`, `scope_grants_node_and_descendants_not_siblings_or_parent`, `unscoped_hold_of_a_scope_group_sees_every_row`, `root_scope_equals_see_all_on_stamped_rows`, `create_stamps_and_refuses_foreign_nodes`, `owner_cannot_stamp_a_foreign_node`, `write_table_splits_read_from_write`, `list_and_search_ignore_the_write_table`, `via_write_follows_parent_write`, `related_follows_member_rows_next_request`, `member_caps_refuse_501st_and_201st`, `write_group_allows_unchanged_echo`, `action_only_field_set_only_by_effects`, `filter_on_conditional_field_is_unknown_field`, `bypass_off_denies_admin_business_rows`, `agent_writes_propose`, `approver_must_see_the_record`, `denied_is_403_on_rest`, `denied_marker_never_reaches_a_client`. *Side channels:* `s1_blob_held_by_two_records`, `s1_blob_held_only_by_hidden_is_not_found`, `s2_totals_skip_hidden_children`, `s2_all_keeps_the_old_sum`, `s3_unique_message_hidden_equals_visible`, `s4_body_byte_equal_to_a_model_without_the_field`, `s6_hidden_equals_missing_byte_equal`, `s7_hidden_children_neither_copied_nor_counted`, `s7_500_hidden_do_not_block`, `s9_discovery_names_nothing_withheld`, `s10_bulk_applies_write_and_stamp`, `s11_events_carry_no_row_data`, `reads_register_matches_source`. *Aggregate-only:* `aggregate_only_list_is_refused`, `aggregate_cohort_and_secondary_suppression`, `suppression_identical_on_rest_mcp_cli`, `min_bucket_refuses_day`, `aggregate_response_has_no_total`. *Proof:* one test per lint L1 to L19, `proof::personal_data_without_rules_refused`, `proof::waiver_visible_in_four_places`, `proof::waiver_does_not_excuse_an_ungrouped_special_category_field`, `proof::scoped_bundle_refused_until_a_scope_rule_exists`, `proof::warn_mode_installs_with_warnings`, `proof::installed_modules_never_run_the_proof_at_load`, `bundled_catalog_gate_report` (golden: 30 flagged, 5 with rules, 25 waived), `builtin_modules_pass_the_access_proof`, `admin_group_uses_are_classified`, `no_email_literals_in_security_paths`, `route_register_matches_source`. *Across topics:* `integration::scoped_grant_over_org_tree_sees_exactly_the_subtree_on_every_read_path`, `integration::future_dated_move_changes_scope_at_midnight_without_a_write`, `integration::leave_ends_grants_memberships_and_tokens_in_one_run`, `integration::closed_node_drops_out_of_every_set`, `integration::org_model_rules_follow_scoped_roles`. *The proof of the proof, in `obop-accesstest`:* the independent reference oracles (resolution, rows, organisation; a different author, each under 500 lines, never importing the engine's rule code), `oracle::engine_equals_reference_on_all_read_paths` over get, list pages, search with `total`, aggregate, ref validation, blob read, delete referrers, actions and effects, runnable actions and CSV and connector payloads (20,000 seeded pairs per pull request, 100,000 nightly, shrinking into `tests/access/regressions/`), `oracle::writes_equal_reference`, `oracle::planted_defect_found_within_1000_pairs`, `non_interference_world_pairs`, the generated cross-scope matrix `matrix::every_model_command_and_path_has_a_row_and_matches_it` (personas times models times the 20 read and write paths times REST, MCP and CLI times session, agent and OAuth credentials; expectations from the reference unless a row of `tests/access/baseline.toml` says otherwise; an unknown cell or an unexplained skip fails), `matrix::known_gaps_fail_when_the_register_is_ignored`, and `matrix::mutations_are_caught` (the de-duplicated union of the three designs' seeded weakenings, each must fail at least one cell; the feature `access-mutants` cannot be built in release). Gates: `cargo fmt`, `clippy -D warnings`, `cargo test --workspace` with PostgreSQL, `vp check`, `vp build`, `i18n:check`, Playwright `organisation.spec.ts`, `access.spec.ts`, `people.spec.ts` (unchanged and green).

**Why.** Today obop's access control is groups plus five record rules. It cannot say who a person is (`own` holds free text that nothing validates), where a person sits in an organisation, which part of it a role applies to or for how long, who approved that, or what a person could see at a given moment; a write is allowed wherever a read is; aggregates, blobs, computed totals and effect children ignore the rules, so a person who may see one record can learn about hidden ones; `platform.admin` satisfies every check; an agent's approval runs with the rights it had when it proposed. The first downstream project built a module on obop and recorded the same defect classes (one predicate for read, write, delete and action; a computed share list that grants nobody; no write protection for a field or for fields filled by evidence; an ungated child read in effects; admin satisfying every group; agent approvals) and two architecture records for the deviations it made because obop could not express them. A business with offices and units needs these generically. Doing it as one change, with one person id, one organisation port, one epoch and one rule language, avoids three subsystems that each carry a private copy of "who is this and where do they sit".

**Rejected.** Keeping the person registry in `auth_user` (it is outside the registry, so no `ref` can point at it). Deriving the id from an e-mail address, a principal name or an IdP subject (it changes when the person's mail changes; every `own` value would have to be rewritten). Making the UUID the login name (nobody types it; a rename of an alias must not touch a token). An `org_person.invite` command that returns a temporary password (a password cannot leave the Dispatcher). Random v4 ids through an engine change (a marginal gain; every id in obop is v7). Stored mirrors of the current parent and main node (stale at a date boundary with no scheduler). A second epoch for the organisation (the grants' node sets are computed from the tree). A second port to the tree (`ScopeSource`, `ScopeTree`) beside `OrgDirectory`. `access_policy` records and rules stored as data in grants (a change to a rule is a package upgrade with a plan, audit and rollback). A profile keyed on `requires_core` (the core version stays 0.2.0 and all 22 packages say `^0.2`). Any lint inside `check_module` (`load_installed` calls it, so an installed package would vanish at the next start). An overload of `search` for aggregate-only access (one permission group per command). A per-model "exclude the operator" flag beside the global bypass switch. Per-row forward lookups for `related` (one inverse lookup per request instead). A `Viewer` or `Seen` newtype refactor of every engine file (the rename to `raw_*`, a register test and a world-pair test give almost the same guarantee). Polling the epoch every second (not "the next request"). A kv counter as the epoch (outside `apply_checked`, so a crash leaves caches stale). A `shared_with` length cap that silently revokes long lists. Pushing authority into groups only (no dates, no scope, no approval trail). Database row-level security as the primary mechanism (D10 keeps it as a later backstop).

**Consequences.** *Modules and crates added:* built-in `org` (15 stored models and `org_problem`, always on, hidden from users without `org.staff`) and `access` (2 stored and 4 virtual models: `access_group`, `access_decision`, `access_rule`, `access_batch`), the kernel crate `obop-access`, the dev-only crate `obop-accesstest`, `docs/spec/access.md`, `docs/spec/organisation.md`, labels in de, fr, pl, fi, sv, es for both modules. *Groups:* `org.staff`, `org.person_admin`, `org.node_admin`, `org.directory_contact`, `org.feed_operator`, `access.grant_delegate`, `access.grant_admin`, `access.role_admin`; `audit.reader` and `security.officer` as before. *Security event kinds:* the person kinds (`person_create`, `person_adopt`, `person_login`, `person_activate`, `person_activate_failed`, `person_suspend`, `person_reinstate`, `person_leave`, `person_rehire`), the grant and role kinds (`grant_propose`, `grant_confirm`, `grant_reject`, `grant_end`, `grant_shorten`, `grant_sweep`, `grant_import`, `access_adopt`, `role_define`, `role_groups`, `role_authority`, `role_details`, `role_retire`, `package_roles`, `install_waiver`, `explain_used`, `who_can_used`, `plan_approve`, `access_integrity`) and `aggregate_only`, `scope_resolve_error`. *Settings and flags:* `security.access_gate`, `step_up_secs`, `direct_groups`, `conflicts`, `cover_max_days`; environment `OBOP_ADMIN_BYPASS`, `OBOP_DISCLOSE_WITHHELD`, `OBOP_ACCESS_EPOCH_CACHE_MS`, `OBOP_ACCESS_MAX_ACTIVE_GRANTS` (50,000); metrics `obop_permission_epoch`, `obop_access_generation_rebuilds_total`, `obop_access_resolve_errors_total`, `obop_access_epoch_read_errors_total`. *Wire:* errors `denied` (403) and `reauth_required` (400) are rows of the one error table of step 1; `GENERATED_COMMANDS` gains `aggregate`; `CommandDef` gains `record` (`no_record()`), so a command that acts on no record has no required `id` in its MCP schema and its OpenAPI body lists its fields; `/api/meta` gains `me`, `fields_withheld`, per-model rules in kinds and names only, `waiver`, `modules[].groups`, `writable`; `GET /api/auth/users` rows gain `person` and `login`; `GET /api/auth/groups` gains a catalogue. *Amended texts:* D58 (rules now have positions; the sentence that computed totals and aggregates ignore the rules), D57's consequence sentence on aggregates, D56 (effects read visible rows), D69 (account writes are atomic with their event; a person-bound account refuses the generic disable, enable and offboard), D10's consequence (one decision function; the oracle test is delivered here). *Changed by default on existing instances, with the escapes named:* blob reads follow the holding record (S1), computed totals count visible children (S2, `computed_visibility = "all"`), effects see visible rows (S7), `approve` re-resolves its owner. The flags default to today's behaviour. *Migration:* additive; new models appear empty; one ledger step (the next free number at merge) merges `security.access_gate = "warn"` and `security.step_up_secs = 0` into `obop_setting/instance` on a database that already holds users or packages, while a fresh database takes the code defaults (enforce, 900); the runbook in `docs/DEPLOY.md` is: adopt persons (`obop admin adopt --all --dry-run`, then apply), adopt groups into grants (`obop call access_batch adopt_groups`, dry run first), then optionally flip `security.direct_groups`; before a package with a `ref org_person` owner field is used, every account that creates its records must be adopted. A binary older than this record ignores the new fields and tables, cannot load a package that uses the new keys, and must not be run after the first `leave` (it would let `enable` revive a left account). *Cost:* a call reads the epoch once; a row test is a set lookup; `related` costs one `find` or `find_in` per rule per request; lists and searches still read the whole model before filtering until D76 (this record changes no cost class). *Order of merging with step 1:* the three engine seams (`raw_*` rename and the one write-preparation function, then the gate positions, then field control and the channels) are queued with D75, D76 and D77's command work on the same files, one writer at a time; the plan is in the build plan. *Deviations from the requirement text, each recorded:* the person id is not typed as the login (an alias is); the stored mirrors of parent and main node are returned as of a date; "an active person has exactly one main membership" is reported (`org_problem`), not enforced; a root grant sees no record with an empty node; human four-eyes for rehire and moves is not delivered; a lint, not a runtime error, enforces the install proof.

**Not delivered, and why.** The operator's separate groups, time-boxed elevation, break-glass and the binding of an IdP subject to a person (step 3: only the interfaces `superuser()`, `is_admin_account()`, `Effective.bypass` and `idp_subject` identifiers exist); service principals and `run_as` (step 4: an effect cannot see children the caller cannot, and says so); feed connectors, a scheduler and a service principal for the feed (step 5: `apply_feed` runs as an administrator session, an agent proposal or the operator CLI); retention enforcement and tamper evidence of the log, notifying a person of a grant, erasure and anonymisation of persons (step 6); a legal-entity partition (`partition` is parsed and refused; `restrict` is the hook); time-window restricts and restrict expressions beyond the closed subset; pushdown of `related` and SQL on SQLite (D76 owns the compiler; this record ships the contract and vectors); database row-level security as a backstop; differencing and timing defences; denial signals and periodic access review; the human four-eyes primitive for rehire, moves and closures; automatic transfer of owned records when the owner leaves (counted tasks and manual `reassign` and `retain` exist); effective-dated `employer` history (waits for D75); linking `party`, `hr_employee` and the bundled packages to persons (an additive later step); a builder screen for `scope`, `related` and `aggregate` (TOML only); self-service profile editing, photos, retroactive correction of structure history, rekeying an adopted id; removal of the accepted residuals (duplicate message, delete-referrer message, assertion reads, sequence gaps); the permission explorer for grant authors inside their own scope (`pending` serves them).

**Not tried.** Several PostgreSQL processes under load with the epoch and the generations; a managed PostgreSQL behind the D74 pool; the real Claude apps through a grant change and through the OAuth pages with an alias; real passkey freshness; a directory feed from a real source; a directory of 400 people, 2,000 nodes and 20,000 memberships measured (the budgets are 5 ms p95 for a warm and a cold resolution and 5 ms for 250-node descendants); PostgreSQL at 100,000 rows for the rule compilation; a person-bound account created through a real IdP; a deployed instance (nothing is deployed by this record).

#### D56 Action effects and assertions
**Status** BINDING (owner-directed in chat, 2026-10-17). **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** An action (a transition, D48) may declare `assert` conditions and `[[model.action.effect]]` entries. Conditions are boolean expressions (docs/MODULES.md 3.8) over the record and its child records, checked before anything is written; a failure refuses the action with the declared message. Effects run in order after the new state is set, in the same unit of work: the command layer builds every write in an overlay (so later effects see earlier ones), applies them with one `Backend::apply` transaction on SQLite and PostgreSQL (`insert_many` generalised to inserts, merges and deletes), and the sequence counters taken on the way are part of it. Kinds: `create` (fields from `$self.f`, `$self.id`, `$line.f`, `$created.name`, `$now`, `$today`, `$caller` or constants; `for_each` repeats it per child row), `update` (a ref'd record or the children of this record), `adjust` (add or subtract an amount from a number field of the record a ref names or the one a `match` finds, optionally creating it, optionally with a floor), and `set_state` (run an action of another model, whose own group, from-state, required fields, assertions, sequences and effects all apply; an effect never sets a governed field any other way). Effects run with the caller's permissions: the caller needs the write group of every model an effect writes and the group of every action it starts, every record involved must be visible to the caller (D58), and typed validation (D47) applies to what is written. The guard computes this before anything runs, so an agent without the groups is refused at once; if any action reachable through `set_state` needs approval, the whole operation is one proposal and the approver must hold all those groups. Limits: 12 effects per action, 500 records created, changed or looked at per operation, `set_state` chains at most 4 deep with no cycle; expressions are at most 400 characters and 80 parts. The install refuses an effect whose target model or field does not exist or is not in the package's dependencies, whose value does not fit the target field's kind, that omits a required field, that loops over a model that is not a child of this one, that refers to `$created` before it exists, or that closes a cycle of `set_state` effects. Built-in modules declare the same metadata in Rust (`Effect::create` and friends): confirming a sale order creates the draft invoice and its lines, `invoice.post` is asserted to have a total, `inventory_move.validate` adjusts the stock items at both ends, accounting refuses to post an unbalanced entry.

**Why.** Actions that can only flip a field left every workflow to be completed by hand, and the platform's promise (one data model, generated surfaces, agents that propose and humans that approve) fails if the interesting part of a workflow is outside it. **Rejected.** Code in packages (D11 reserves it for Wasm); a general expression or scripting language (unbounded, unreviewable); effects that run as the system (an escalation that would let any action holder write anywhere); eventual consistency through a queue (a half-applied posting is the failure this exists to prevent); triggers on plain create and update (hidden control flow). **Consequences.** The unit lives in one process: the dispatcher holds one lock around every write of a model that has a rule or is an effect target, so two servers on one PostgreSQL database can still race between the reads and the transaction (one process per database stays the rule, D30, D48). The transaction makes the writes atomic, not the reads consistent across servers. Effects cannot call out; notifications and payments wait for the Wasm tier. Data written outside the commands (seeds, imports) does not run effects. **Evidence.** D11, D18, D41, D47, D48, MODULES.md 3.4 and 3.8, tests `engine_tests` and `model_tests`.

#### D57 Computed fields, uniqueness and sequences
**Status** BINDING (owner-directed in chat, 2026-10-17). **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** *Computed fields.* `computed = "<expression>"` on a `text`, `number`, `money` or `bool` field: integers, text and booleans only, `+ - * /` (division rounds half away from zero, null counts as 0, `""` or false), comparisons, `&&`, `||`, `!`, `if`, `concat`, `is_set`, and the aggregates `sum`, `min`, `max`, `count` over child records (`sum(lines.amount)`, optionally `where <condition on the child>`). A computed field is not stored: it is evaluated when a record is read, from the current children, and put into the record before filtering and sorting, so it can be filtered and sorted on and is in `get`, `list`, `search` and the response of every write. It cannot be written (sending its current value back is accepted, anything else is refused), is never required, and may read other computed fields as long as there is no cycle. *Why on read:* a stored total drifts whenever any write path forgets it (an effect, an import, a child changed by another action); a total computed from the children cannot. The cost is a look at the children per read: an indexed lookup for one record, one scan of the child table grouped by parent for a list. A computed value is as protected as its inputs: the install requires the same permission group, a class that covers the inputs' (the same class if they are sensitive) and personal-data status. *Uniqueness.* `unique = true` (optionally `unique_with = ["company"]`): checked against the records as the unit would leave them, under the dispatcher lock, so there is no check-then-write race inside a server, and backed by a partial unique index on SQLite and PostgreSQL (an expression index over the JSON, empty values excluded) that also catches a second server; a violation is `model.field must be unique: "x" is already used by another record`. *Sequences.* `kind = "sequence"` with a `format` such as `INV-{year}-{seq:05}`, `assign_on` (an action; default: at create), `gapless` and `reset` (`never`, `year`, `month`). The number comes from a counter record (`sequence_counter`, per model, field and period) read and written inside the same unit as the record, so a failed posting consumes no number and parallel postings cannot share one. `gapless` additionally forbids deleting a record that holds a number. A sequence is read-only and is unique by construction (and indexed). `obop sequences backfill [MODEL [FIELD]]` numbers existing records in creation order, in one transaction per field; records created before the field existed stay unnumbered until then, and the year of a backfilled number is the current one.

**Why.** Totals, balances, numbers and duplicate checks were conventions that people kept by hand (MODULES.md section 10 listed them as limitations); invoice numbers in particular were a placeholder. **Rejected.** Stored computed values with recompute hooks (drift, and every write path needs the hook); a general formula language with floats and functions (rounding bugs, unbounded cost); database sequences (not rolled back, so not gapless, and not available on SQLite); numbering at create for invoices (draft cancellations would leave holes). **Consequences.** Aggregates ignore row-level access (D58) and sum all children of a record the caller can see; give children `via` the parent so the two agree. Computed values cost reads and are not indexed. Sorting by a computed field reads all rows. Date arithmetic does not exist: SLA elapsed times stay out. A unique index cannot be created over existing duplicates; the instance then logs a warning and keeps the write-time check only. **Evidence.** D30, D47, D56, MODULES.md 3.3 and 3.8, tests `engine_tests`, `model_tests` and the storage contract.

#### D58 Row-level access
**Status** BINDING (owner-directed in chat, 2026-10-17). **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** no.

**Decision.** `[model.access]` adds record rules to the group checks of a model: `own` (a text or ref field holding the user id of the person the record belongs to), `via` (a ref field: visible when the parent record is, up to four hops), `public_field` (a bool, which may be computed: visible to everyone who may read the model when true), `shared_with` (a text field of user ids separated by commas) and `see_all_group` (a group, or a list of groups, that sees every record). A record is visible when any rule grants it; `platform.admin` sees everything; a model with no rules is unrestricted. Enforced in `get`, `list`, `search` (the rule is applied before the filter, the sort, the page and the count, so `total` counts only visible rows), `update`, `delete`, every action including those started by effects, the referrers named when a delete is refused, and ref validation (a ref to a record the caller cannot see fails with the message for a record that does not exist). A forbidden record answers exactly like a missing one in every command (`no <model> <id>`). An agent acts for its owner: `own` and `shared_with` match the owner's id, and the groups are the owner's intersected with the token's scopes, so it never sees more than its owner and sees `see_all` records only if the scope includes the group. On create, `own` defaults to the creator and a person who cannot see the result is refused (`set employee to yourself`); an update cannot move a record out of the caller's own sight. The public site (D50) is a separate layer with its own whitelist and is unchanged. Applied to hr leave requests, expenses, timesheets, documents (by confidentiality, through a computed `open_to_all`), dashboards (`shared`, closing A6; tiles follow their dashboard through `via`), while connectors keep their own owner scoping.

**Why.** Group checks cannot say "your own", so every leave request, expense and confidential document was readable by everyone who could read the model, and the dashboard sharing flag was a label. **Rejected.** Post-filtering in the surfaces (counts and totals leak, and each surface would need it); per-field rules (D40 and field groups already cover columns); rules in expressions (an evaluator on the hot path of every read; the five declarative rules cover the cases that occur). **Consequences.** Rows are filtered in the command layer after the model is read from the store, as filtering already is (D49), so the cost is linear in the table. Aggregates and computed totals ignore the rules (D57). The id of a user is the login name; a record owned by a name that is not a login is invisible to everyone but the managers. Existing records get no owner: a model that gains `own` hides its old records from non-managers until their field is filled in. Data written outside the commands is not checked. A unique constraint can still reveal that some record holds a value (a duplicate is refused), because uniqueness is global. **Evidence.** D10, D18, D40, D48, D49, D56, D57, MODULES.md 3.9, the oracle test in `engine_tests` and `model_tests`.

#### D80 Agentic-first surface: one command catalogue, three equal surfaces, the UI as a client
**Status** RATIFIED (OR-4, OR-16); the details marked Proposed are not ruled. **Reversibility** one-way door once public (the catalogue is the public contract). **Owner action** Q22 (CLI name and grammar); approving each entry of the exemption list (OR-16). Q30 is closed.

**Decision.** Owner: "AGENTIC FIRST EVERYTHING CLI FOR EVERYTHING". Every capability is a typed command: one entry in a command catalogue generated from the runtime registry (D29), reachable through three equal surfaces, the `obop` CLI, the REST API (D31) and MCP (D18). The web UI (React on Vite+, driven through `vp` with pnpm, D15) is a client of the same commands and holds no business logic. Build order: define the command (registry metadata, permission rows, dry-run behaviour), generate CLI, API and MCP, then build views on top. **Definition of done (parity).** A feature is not done until it exists in CLI, API and MCP with the same input and output schemas, the D10 permission check, a dry-run with a digest-bound `plan_id` for mutating commands and an idempotency key (D18), an audit entry (D33), reference documentation generated from the registry (D38) and a passing CLI/API/MCP parity test (D36). The UI is not part of the per-feature definition of done but reaches parity with the commands before the first stable release, and that parity is literal (OR-16): every command of every shipped module gets a UI, except the commands on the exemption list below. 1.0 does not ship until the UI parity test is green for every command that is not on the list. "CLI for everything" includes operations, not only business commands: install, upgrade (`obop upgrade --dry-run`, Proposed; today `obop migrate plan|run` and `obop update` exist), backup and restore, diagnostics, the plugin commands (D81) and the development loop are typed commands too. Agents are principals (D18) and use the same commands as people. **Exemption list (RATIFIED mechanism, OR-16).** The only exceptions to literal UI parity are operator-only commands (for example `backup`, `doctor`, `upgrade`, `pack verify`) that the owner approves as CLI-only, each with a written reason. The approved exceptions form the exemption list, and a command is on it only by the owner's approval. Each entry records the command, the reason and the surfaces it lacks: at least the UI, and for a command that cannot be served by REST or MCP (the bootstrap and local commands of [spec/cli.md](spec/cli.md) 1.1) those too. The ruling says "CLI-only"; this register reads that as "no UI" unless the entry also records the missing REST and MCP surfaces (an interpretation, which the owner confirms or corrects when approving entries). The catalogue marks the command, and the parity test (D36) fails on any command that lacks a surface and is not on the list. [spec/cli.md](spec/cli.md) 1.1 is the single enumerated list of proposed entries and this register cites it without restating it; its entries, and its division into bootstrap and local commands, are proposals until the owner approves them one by one (Proposed timing: the initial list before the UI parity work starts in W2, 2027-10-01; later additions when a command is added). The browser-only act class that the earlier text of Q30 floated (a UI-only step) is not covered by OR-16 and is not adopted: OR-4 bars UI-only features, so a step that only a person can perform (a browser consent, a signer's own act, a Web Push prompt) stays part of a catalogue command that exists on all three surfaces; the command starts the step, the person performs it in the UI, and a status command reads the result (the reading of spec/cli.md 1.1). Until the owner approves an entry, "exempt" in any document means a proposed entry. Proposed details, defined in [spec/cli.md](spec/cli.md): the grammar `obop <noun> <verb>` (Q22), machine-readable output on every command, exit codes, confirmation and `--dry-run` conventions, shell completion generated from the catalogue.

**Why.** Agents need the same permission path as users (D18), and three surfaces can be built by a very small team only if they are generated from metadata (D29). A UI that is a client of the commands cannot drift from the API. **Superseded alternative.** UI-equals-API ordering, where the API followed the screens, and a hand-written CLI (R19, R21 and older documents). **Rejected.** A parallel AI API; a UI-first build; business logic in the browser. **Consequences.** No module ships a feature as UI-only (OR-4, OR-16); the only surface gaps are entries on the exemption list. Computed fields and onchange behaviour live server-side behind commands. The parity test joins the W1 exit criteria (section 5.2). Documents that order the work "UI then API" are corrected (section 7). The CLI is a Rust binary and is not distributed through npm (Q32). **Evidence.** OR-4, OR-16; R21, R16, D18, D29, D31.

#### D81 Free, open plugin registry
**Status** RATIFIED (OR-3, OR-17) for the registry, the CLI modes, the two trust tiers, the OSI-approved-licence listing rule, the meaning of verified and the absence of payments; the staging and the remaining admission details are PROPOSED. **Reversibility** two-way door until the index is public, one-way afterwards (identifiers, manifest, signatures). **Owner action** Q24 (who operates what), Q25 (copy-mode provenance). Q23 is closed (OR-17).

**Decision.** obop has a free, open registry of plugins (in architecture text: packages of kind pack, wasm, remote or theme, D32). There are no fees, no paid listings, no payouts, no take rate and no payments of any kind in the registry; it handles no money (OR-2, OR-3). The CLI has two ways to use a plugin: **install**, which fetches a signed, version-pinned package into the instance and records the exact version and digest, so updates are explicit commands; and **copy**, which copies the plugin's source into the user's own instance, where it becomes the business's own code with a recorded provenance (source, version, digest, licence) and no automatic updates. Both exist as typed commands (D80), alongside `search`. **Admission (RATIFIED, OR-17).** The registry lists only plugins with an OSI-approved licence (an SPDX identifier on the OSI list), in either tier. A closed plugin can be sideloaded but never listed, and copy mode needs source, so copy mode stays legally clean. **Trust tiers:** `community` (automated gates only, labelled unreviewed) and `verified` (a verified publisher plus automated gates: signatures, SBOM and the capability manifest; OR-17). `private` (business-own, never listed) and `core` (first-party) are the non-registry levels. Sideloading stays always allowed (D32) and the kill switch stays (a revocation list in the index). Proposed detail, which OR-17 does not fix: how a publisher is verified (domain or GitHub organisation proof, good standing), the full list of automated gates of R24 s9 (manifest lint, licence check, SBOM and OSV scan, capability diff per version, secret scan, name reservation, compatibility runs) and manual security review as best effort, never promised. **Staged delivery (PROPOSED).** Stage 1: a git-backed static index (a public repository holding one manifest per plugin version, digests, signatures, tier and revocations) plus the CLI commands; artefacts are hosted by publishers in OCI registries or git, and SpaceCorps serves only the index. Stage 2: a registry service (API, search, publishing, review tooling, compatibility badges) only when stage 1's volume justifies it, and only after an owner decision: a registry service is an operated service for other people, which OR-11 and D83 did not allow for SpaceCorps and which the hosting ruling OR-22 does not cover (it covers dedicated obop servers only), so stage 2 needs an owner decision (an amendment of D83, or a ruling that a registry service is not "hosted obop"). Volume makes the question worth asking; it does not answer it (Q24). Placement: stage 1 in W2, stage 2 in W3 or W4 on demand (D86).

**Why.** The owner's ruling: a free, open registry reachable from the CLI, with trust tiers and no commerce. A static index is the smallest honest start for a team of one person plus agents. **Superseded alternative.** A package marketplace with paid listings, billing, payouts and tax, a 0% to 15% take rate, a `partner` tier with SLAs, white-label and a hosted registry sold as an operated service (former D2 marketplace clause, R24 s9-s11). **Rejected.** A hosted registry service as a paid product; Twenty-style no-review as the only model. **Consequences.** D32's trust levels change (`reviewed` merges into `verified`; `partner` is retired). Copy mode must not promote third-party code to a higher trust tier by itself (Q25). The index operator's CRA and takedown role, and the publisher terms, go to counsel (Q5, Q24). Review capacity is scarce, so manual review is best effort, never a promised service. **Evidence.** OR-3, OR-17; R24 s5, s9.

#### D82 Contribution model: DCO, no CLA, Apache-2.0 in and out
**Status** RATIFIED (OR-1, OR-18). **Reversibility** one-way door once outside contributions exist (a CLA cannot be added retroactively). **Owner action** none; counsel is asked to confirm the DCO wording for agent-authored commits (BUSINESS CN-02, inside the Q16 list). Q29 is closed.

**Decision.** Every commit carries a DCO sign-off (`Signed-off-by`, Developer Certificate of Origin); a required CI check rejects commits without it (not built yet: `.github/workflows/ci.yml` has no DCO job on 2026-10-27; OR-30: it is built when outside contributors appear). There is no CLA and no copyright assignment: contributors keep their copyright and license it to the project under Apache-2.0, inbound equals outbound (Apache-2.0 section 5), with the patent licence that Apache-2.0 section 3 carries. Source files carry SPDX identifiers (`Apache-2.0`); NOTICE holds the attribution ("Copyright 2026 SpaceCorps contributors"). The clean-room attestation (F3) stays in the PR template; the provenance rule of ENGINEERING section 8 is unchanged. Trademarks are not licensed by Apache-2.0 (section 6); the policy is Q26. **Agent-authored commits (RATIFIED, OR-18).** Because the team is largely AI agents (D84), the owner signs off every commit (`git commit -s`), including every agent-authored one, and agents are recorded with a `Co-Authored-By` trailer that names the agent. An agent never provides the sign-off, so the `Signed-off-by` line is always a person's. Counsel is still asked to confirm that this satisfies the DCO; that is a counsel item (BUSINESS CN-02, inside the Q16 list), not an owner question. Reading of OR-18 (an interpretation, not the owner's words): because a sign-off certifies the Developer Certificate of Origin, the owner reviews each agent-authored commit before signing it; how that review is organised is engineering detail (ENGINEERING section 8), not an owner question.

**Why.** OR-1: permissive licence, DCO, no CLA. The DCO is the lightest contributor friction and needs no bot that stores signatures. **Superseded alternative.** A licence-grant CLA with a patent licence and an irrevocable "OSI licence forever" pledge, DCO for documentation only, a steward veto and an investor covenant (former D1, D2 and D5 clauses). **Rejected.** A CLA; copyright assignment; no sign-off at all. **Consequences.** With no CLA, relicensing needs the consent of every contributor, so a later closed or copyleft relicence of the whole is impractical by design. A dependency allow-list (D1) keeps the shipped tree Apache-2.0-compatible. CLA tooling questions (ENGINEERING Q-E8) are moot. **Evidence.** OR-1, OR-10, OR-18; R28 C.2, R17 s4-s5.

#### D83 Hosting posture and a free sandbox at the public beta
**Status** RATIFIED (OR-11, OR-20, OR-22); the sandbox details (terms and data policy, host and cost ceiling, abuse and screening approach) are PROPOSED (Q34 to Q36). **Reversibility** two-way door (the sandbox can be closed at any time; the hosting service of OR-22 is not offered yet; any wider hosting would need an amendment of this record and of D2). **Owner action** Q34, Q35, Q36.

**Decision.** Two kinds of hosting by SpaceCorps are ruled. **(1) A free sandbox (OR-20).** A free, hosted **sandbox**, run by SpaceCorps and opened at the public beta (0.5; 2027-07-06 in the current calendar, a Proposed date under D26, so the sandbox moves if 0.5 moves). The sandbox is ephemeral, holds sample data only, has abuse controls and auto-reset, and carries no service level and no support. This replaces the register's earlier default of no hosted sandbox before 1.0, with a local demo for evaluation; a local demo (`obop dev up`, a seeded instance on the user's machine) may still exist as an option. **(2) A planned paid hosting service (OR-22, 2026-10-27).** SpaceCorps plans to run, for a business that asks, a dedicated Sliplane server with obop and PostgreSQL on it, priced as three times the provider's list price rounded up to a price ending in 9, extra storage extra (D2 holds the rule; the prices are one table, `web/apps/site/data/hosting.ts`). It is planned, not offered or charged: no billing, terms of use, processing agreement or sub-processor disclosure exist, and nothing is sold until they do. OR-22 overrides the part of OR-11 that said SpaceCorps offers no paid hosting; the rest of OR-11 stands (no hosted obop on the free sandbox for other people's real data; third parties may host obop under Apache-2.0). The Data Act switching duties, SLAs, SOC 2 and ISO plans and the data-processing (GDPR Art. 28) duties of a hosting provider do not apply to the sandbox, because it holds no customer data and no customer contracts. They do apply to the hosting service once it is offered; OR-22 lists what counsel and the owner must settle first (a processing agreement, the disclosure of Sliplane as sub-processor, terms of use and a privacy notice for hosted instances, the Data Act switching duties, the CRA role, VAT and invoicing, a payment provider, the abuse and sanctions approach, the on-call burden and Sliplane's terms). obop is a product that others run: self-hosters, companies, partners and third-party hosts are free to host it under Apache-2.0, and they carry a host's duties. Everything the project ships for operators stays open: cells (D6), fleet and release tooling, backups, the single-business install and full export (D38). The sandbox is an operating duty, not a W1 module, so the W1 cut of section 5.3 (OR-15) is unchanged by it.

**Duties the sandbox creates.** This list is the single statement of them; every document that discusses hosting duties cites it. (1) Terms of use that forbid personal data and production data. (2) A privacy notice for what is processed anyway (connection data for abuse control, server logs), and provider identification where the law requires it (which depends on Q20). (3) Abuse and rate controls: per-address and per-session limits, resource quotas and a kill switch. (4) Egress blocking and outbound-mail blocking, so the sandbox cannot be used to attack or spam (the SSRF-hardened egress client of D33 with nothing allowed; outbound mail goes to a sink). (5) Auto-reset: every session business has a time to live and is dropped, and a scheduled full reset runs. (6) Sanctions screening (BUSINESS CN-20, which now applies). (7) A named small running cost, paid by the owner because the project is self-funded (D84); the Proposed ceiling is EUR 100 per month all-in (an estimate, not a quote; Q35). (8) On-call burden: one human plus agents run a public service with no promised availability, which is why it can be switched off at any time. (9) Counsel review of the terms, the privacy notice and the screening approach before it opens, and a counsel view of whether the sandbox, and later the hosting service, change the CRA analysis of Q5. The sandbox creates no Data Act switching duty and no SLA, for the reason given above.

**Proposed details (Q34 to Q36).** Form: the ordinary single binary and PostgreSQL (D37) with one database per business (D6); each visitor session is a short-lived business cloned from a seeded template database and dropped at its time to live; sample data only, from the seed set shared with the local demo; no sign-up data by default; no new product component and no SpaceCorps-only code path. Opening rule: the sandbox opens with the public beta only if duties 1 to 9 are in place (go/no-go 2027-06-01, the old Q27 date); otherwise it opens later, and the public beta does not wait for it. The sandbox is not part of the protected core of 5.3.

**Why.** The owner's ruling (OR-20), taken against the recommendation to offer a local demo only. **Superseded alternative.** (1) obop Cloud with plans Free, Team, Growth and Scale, hosted fleet rings, a paid beta on 2027-07-06, an SLA plan and Assurance service, hosted gateways, SOC 2 and ISO plans and a Data Act switching contract (former D2, D16, D17, D26 and D38 clauses). (2) The register's default "no hosted sandbox before 1.0; evaluation through a local demo" (the former Q27 default), replaced by OR-20. **Rejected.** Paid multi-tenant hosting plans of the former obop Cloud kind (OR-11; OR-22 later allowed a plain dedicated-server hosting service instead); a free hosted tier for real customer data; a persistent sandbox. **Consequences.** The CRA analysis for counsel assumed a free, non-hosted project (Q5); counsel is now also asked whether the sandbox and the planned hosting service change it. "Hosted" statements in companion documents become statements about operators, except for the sandbox and the hosting service of OR-22. The 0.5 milestone, the operating duties, the data policy, the risk register, the topology and every mention of a local-only demo are conformed to this record (section 7 lists the superseded default). **Evidence.** OR-11, OR-20, OR-22; R18, R26, R28 E (historical).

#### D84 Team and funding posture
**Status** RATIFIED (OR-6). **Reversibility** two-way door. **Owner action** none; Q31 is closed (OR-21).

**Decision.** The project is self-funded for now. Owner: "for now self funded dont worry about this we are GIGA DEVELOPERS YOU AND ME". The team is the owner plus AI agents. There are no investors, no hiring plan, no FTE cap or headcount gate, no ARR gate, no covenants and no grant dependency; grants (for example NLnet) remain an optional idea only (OR-21: no NLnet Restack application now; revisit when a self-contained reusable crate exists). No financial model governs the plan, because the software has no revenue model (D2); the planned hosting service (OR-22) is priced as a multiple of the provider's price and is not a funding plan. **Named risk: schedule feasibility.** The kept dates (OR-13: the German pack on 2027-09-30, 1.0 on 2028-04-18) sit on effort estimates that assumed 4 to 17 people (ROADMAP 4.5 found W1 already over-subscribed for 6 to 10 FTE); a team of one human plus agents may not deliver even the cut W1 on time, and the one hard external driver is the 2028-01-01 e-invoicing mandate. Mitigation is scope, never dates: the slip order of D86, a measured-throughput review at the W0 exit (2026-12-31) of how much of K1 and K2 landed against plan (Proposed), and the rule that nothing in a protected core item slips before everything ahead of it in the order. Related named risks: one human owner (key-person risk), external experts the project cannot buy (the German accountant sign-off, counsel), and agent-generated code quality (mitigated by the ledger proptests, the oracles and golden cases of D30 and D36). Costs (counsel, CI runners, domains) are paid by the owner; nothing in the plan depends on revenue.

**Why.** The owner's ruling. **Superseded alternative.** About EUR 3M seed and EUR 5M against EUR 1M ARR, at most 17 FTE before EUR 1M ARR, a cut to 6 FTE at M24, a pledge, veto and investor covenant before any priced round, and an NLnet application (former D5). **Rejected.** Hiring plans; funding gates. **Consequences.** R26's economics (ARPA, break-even, peak funding, hiring curves) and R20's headcount bands are historical. ROADMAP 4 (team shape and hiring gates), the ROADMAP 8.2 rows on paying organisations and ARR, and BUSINESS 6 and 9 (BR-01, BR-04, BR-08, BR-15, BR-31 and the funding rows) were conformed to this record: the gates and rows are withdrawn, and BUSINESS 9.4 lists the retired and recast risks. Exit criteria are correctness and adoption measures a small team controls (section 5.2). **Evidence.** OR-6, OR-13, OR-21; ROADMAP 4.5, R20 s10, R26 (historical).

#### D85 Support promise and release train
**Status** RATIFIED (OR-12) for the promise; the release cadence and the deprecation window after 1.0 are PROPOSED. **Reversibility** one-way door once public (promises to users). **Owner action** Q28.

**Decision.** The promise is scaled to capacity. **Before 1.0:** an N-1 upgrade path (any 0.N upgrades to 0.N+1 with `obop upgrade --dry-run`, a Proposed command that does not exist yet, and a chain test from 0.3; today `obop migrate plan|rehearse|run` is the built path) and a 12-month deprecation window for public contracts (REST and MCP, the CLI command catalogue, the pack schema, `obop.toml`, the UI SDK). **From the first stable release (1.0, target 2028-04-18):** a 2-year security-fix window, to be extended toward 5 years only as users and funding appear. Nothing beyond this is promised: no SLA, no response times, no "LTS" label, no patch train, no hosted fleet, no paid support (the terms of the planned hosting service, OR-22, are open and not part of this promise). Security fixes land on `main` and are backported inside each series' window; fixes and advisories are never withheld or tiered (D2, D38).
